
 
 
 The matrix 𝐴_2 conjecture fails, i.e. 3/2>1. 
 
 
 
   
 
 
  // Restore the saved color scheme preference, or
  // enact the browser preference if "automatic", 
  // without expecting DOM load to have completed.
  //
  // Also restore any saved readingmode and ToC display preferences.
  function initializeReadingPreferences() {
    let saved_theme = localStorage.getItem("ar5iv_theme") || "automatic";
    if (saved_theme === "automatic") {
      if (window.matchMedia("(prefers-color-scheme: dark)").matches) {
        saved_theme = "dark";
      }
    }
    if (saved_theme == "dark") {
      document.documentElement.setAttribute("data-theme", "dark");
    } else {
      document.documentElement.setAttribute("data-theme", "light");
    }

    const tocDisplay = localStorage.getItem('arxiv_html_paper_toc_display');
    if (tocDisplay) {
      document.documentElement.setAttribute("data-toc-display", tocDisplay);
    }
    const readingMode = localStorage.getItem('arxiv_html_paper_reading_mode');
    if (readingMode) {
      document.documentElement.setAttribute("data-reading-mode", readingMode);
    }
  }
  // Run as soon as JS starts, to minimize repainting
  initializeReadingPreferences();

     icon by making its span a block). */
  .arxiv-html-site-chrome .ds-site-header-tools svg { display: block; width: 18px; height: 18px; flex-shrink: 0; fill: currentColor; }

     arXiv's own render as they are (the logo on the bar, the funder logos on their white
     chips). Important: ar5iv's dark rules are unlayered, as specific as
     :root:not([data-theme]) img:not(.ltx_no_dark_filter). */
  :is(.ds-site-header, .ds-site-footer) img { filter: none !important; }
  .ds-site-header img { background-color: transparent !important; }

  /* Wide (the theme's 1280px): the theme lays the body out as a grid whose full-width top rows
     were the band's and its sticky reader bar's. The band takes the first and scrolls away; the

  }

 Abstract. 
    
 We show that the famous matrix  𝐀 2 \mathbf{A}_{2}  conjecture is false: the norm of the Hilbert Transform in
the
space  L 2 ​ ( W ) L^{2}(W)  with matrix weight  W W  is estimated below by  C ​ [ W ] 𝐀 2 3 / 2 C[W]{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbf{A}_{2}}^{3/2} . 
  
 
 
 Key words and phrases:  matrix weights,
martingale transform, Hilbert transform
 
 2010 Mathematics Subject Classification 42B20, 42B35, 47A30

 1.  Definitions and main result 

 
 Recall that a ( d d -dimensional) matrix weight on  ℝ \mathbb{R} 
is a locally integrable function on  ℝ \mathbb{R} 
with values in the set of positive-semidefinite  d × d d\times d  matrices 1 1 
             1 
            
            
            
          There are of course similar definitions on the unit circle  𝕋 \mathbb{T}  or
 ℝ N \mathbb{R}^{N} . .
The weighted space  L 2 ​ ( W ) L^{2}(W) 
is defined as the space of all measurable functions  f : ℝ → 𝔽 d f:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{F}^{d} , (here  𝔽 = ℝ \mathbb{F}=\mathbb{R} , or  𝔽 = ℂ \mathbb{F}=\mathbb{C} )
for which 
 

 
 
 ‖ f ‖ L 2 ​ ( W ) 2 := ∫ ( W ⁡ ( x ) ​ f ​ ( x ) , f ⁡ ( x ) ) 𝔽 d ​ 𝑑 x < ∞ ; \|f\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(W)}^{2}:=\int(W(x)f(x),f(x)){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbb{F}^{d}}{\mathrm{d}}x<\infty\,; 
 
 
 here  ( ⋅ , ⋅ ) 𝔽 d (\cdot,\cdot){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbb{F}^{d}}  means the standard inner product in  𝔽 d \mathbb{F}^{d} . 
 
 
 A matrix weight  W W  is said to satisfy the matrix  𝐀 2 \mathbf{A}_{2}  condition (write  W ∈ 𝐀 2 W\in\mathbf{A}_{2} ) if 
 

 
 (1.1) 
 
 [ W ] 𝐀 𝟐 := sup I ‖ ⟨ W ⟩ I 1 / 2 ​ ⟨ W − 1 ⟩ I 1 / 2 ‖ 2 < ∞ , \displaystyle[W]{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\bf A_{2}}:=\sup_{I}\left\|\langle W\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{1/2}\langle W^{-1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{1/2}\right\|^{2}<\infty\,, 
 
 
 where  I I  runs over all intervals.
The quantity  [ W ] 𝐀 2 [W]{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbf{A}_{2}}  is called the  𝐀 2 \mathbf{A}_{2}  characteristic of the weight 
 W W . In the scalar case, when  W W  is a scalar weight  w w , this coincides with the classical  A 2 A_{2} 
characteristic  [ w ] A 2 [w]{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{A_{2}} . 
 
 
 Let  ℋ \mathcal{H}  denote the Hilbert transform, 
 

 
 
 ℋ ​ f ​ ( s ) = 1 π ​ p.v. ​ ∫ ℝ f ⁡ ( t ) s − t ​ 𝑑 t . \displaystyle\mathcal{H}f(s)=\frac{1}{\pi}\text{p.v.}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{f(t)}{s-t}{\mathrm{d}}t. 
 
 
 In this paper, we disprove the famous  matrix  𝐀 2 \mathbf{A}_{2}  conjecture , which stated that for any
 𝐀 2 \mathbf{A}_{2}  weight  W W ,  [ W ] 𝐀 2 ≤ 𝓠 [W]{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbf{A}_{2}}\leq\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}} 
 

 
 
 ‖ ℋ ​ g ‖ L 2 ​ ( W ) ≤ C ​ 𝓠 ​ ‖ g ‖ L 2 ​ ( W ) ∀ g ∈ L 2 ​ ( W ) . \displaystyle\|\mathcal{H}g\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(W)}\leq C\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}\|g\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(W)}\qquad\forall g\in L^{2}(W). 
 
 
 
 
 More precisely, our main result is: 
 
 
 Theorem 1.1 . 
 
 There exists a constant  c > 0 c>0  such that for all sufficiently large
 𝓠 \boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}  there exist a  2 × 2 2\times 2  matrix weight  W = W 𝓠 W=W{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\!\!\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}}   ( with real entries ) ,
 [ W ] 𝐀 2 ≤ 𝓠 [W]{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbf{A}_{2}}\leq\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}  and
a function  g ∈ L 2 ​ ( W ) g\in L^{2}(W) ,  g : ℝ → ℝ 2 g:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}^{2} ,  g ≠ 0 g\neq 0  such that 
 

 
 
 ‖ ℋ ​ g ‖ L 2 ​ ( W ) ≥ c ​ 𝓠 3 / 2 ​ ‖ g ‖ L 2 ​ ( W ) . \displaystyle\|\mathcal{H}g\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(W)}\geq c\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}^{3/2}\|g\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(W)}. 
 
 
 
 
 
 In fact, by picking a sufficiently small  c c  we can state it for all  𝓠 ≥ 1 \boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}\geq 1 . By a simple
reduction, we can state it for all dimensions  d ≥ 2 d\geq 2  of matrices. 
 
 
 Theorem  1.1  also shows that the upper bound 
 

 
 
 ‖ T ‖ L 2 ​ ( W ) → L 2 ​ ( W ) ≤ C T ​ 𝓠 3 / 2 \displaystyle\|T\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(W)\to L^{2}(W)}\leq C{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{T}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}^{3/2} 
 
 
 obtained in  [ 32 ]  for general Calderón–Zygmund operators is sharp. 
 
 
 
 2.  Introduction 

 
 In the scalar case, the  A 2 A_{2}  conjecture, i.e. the estimate  ‖ T ‖ L 2 ​ ( w ) → L 2 ​ ( w ) ≤ C T ​ [ w ] A 2 \|T\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(w)\to L^{2}(w)}\leq C{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{T}[w]{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{A_{2}}  turned out to be true, so it is now known as the  A 2 A_{2}  Theorem. It was a long
standing open
problem with a fascinating history that we describe briefly below. 
 
 
 2.1.  Motivation 

 
 An important motivation for sharp estimates of the Hilbert transform in  L 2 ​ ( W ) L^{2}(W)  with
matrix weight  W W  comes from probability theory, more precisely from the theory of stationary
Gaussian processes. For Gaussian processes all information is encoded
in the means and correlations, so the study of multivariate
stationary Gaussian processes (say with discrete time) is reduced to the
study of the subspaces  z n ​ ℂ d z^{n}\mathbb{C}^{d} ,  n ∈ ℤ n\in\mathbb{Z} , in the Hilbert space  L 2 ​ ( 𝐖 ) L^{2}(\mathbf{W}) , where  𝐖 \mathbf{W} 
is the spectral measure of the process. For a multivariate process of dimension  d d  the spectral
measure is a  d × d d\times d  matrix-valued measure. 
 
 
 The regularity properties of stationary stochastic
processes in terms of their spectral measures  𝐖 \mathbf{W}  is a classical area that attracted the
attention of many mathematicians, and a huge bibliography can be found in  [ 19 ] ,
 [ 29 ] ,  [ 46 ] ,  [ 47 ] . In the case of scalar processes many different types of
regularities
were studied and very detailed results were found in many papers, to name just a few  [ 13 ] ,
 [ 14 ] ,  [ 15 ] ,  [ 34 ] ,  [ 35 ] . 
 
 
 One of the questions about regularity was the question when the angle between past and future of
the process is positive, which reduces to the question when the Riesz Projection  P + P_{+} , or,
equivalently the Hilbert Transform  ℋ \mathcal{H}  is bounded in the weighted space  L 2 ​ ( 𝐖 ) L^{2}(\mathbf{W}) .
This question goes back to N. Wiener and P. Masani, and was the
main motivation behind the famous Helson–Szegö Theorem, which solved the problem in the
one-dimensional case. 
 
 
 It was well known for a long time that even weaker regularity conditions than the positivity of the
angle imply that the measure  𝐖 \mathbf{W}  is absolutely continuous  d ​ 𝐖 = W ​ d ​ x {\mathrm{d}}\mathbf{W}=W{\mathrm{d}}x , so everything is
reduced to estimates with matrix weights. 
 
 
 Another motivation comes from the theory of Toeplitz operators (Riemann Hilbert Problem). A
Toeplitz operator  T F T{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{F}  in the vector-valued Hardy space  H 2 ​ ( ℂ d ) H^{2}(\mathbb{C}^{d})  is defined as 
 

 
 
 T F ​ f = P + ​ ( F ​ f ) , f ∈ H 2 ​ ( ℂ d ) , \displaystyle T{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{F}f=P_{+}(Ff),\qquad f\in H^{2}(\mathbb{C}^{d}), 
 
 
 where  P + P_{+}  is the Riesz Projection, i.e. the orthogonal projection from  L 2 L^{2}  onto the Hardy space
 H 2 H^{2} .
The  d × d d\times d  matrix-valued function  F ∈ L ∞ ​ ( M d × d ) F\in L^{\infty}(M_{d\times d})  is called the symbol of the
Toeplitz operator. It is well known that the operator  T F T{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{F}  is invertible if and only if it can
be factorized as 
 

 
 (2.1) 
 
 F = G 1 ∗ ​ G 2 , G 1 , 2 ± 1 ∈ H 2 ​ ( M d × d ) F=G_{1}^{*}G_{2},\qquad G_{1,2}^{\pm 1}\in H^{2}(M_{d\times d}) 
 
 
 (the functions  G 1 , 2 G_{1,2}  and their inverses are in the matrix-valued Hardy class  H 2 H^{2} ), and the
formal inverse 
 

 
 (2.2) 
 
 f ↦ G 2 − 1 ​ P + ​ ( ( G 1 − 1 ) ∗ ​ f ) \displaystyle f\mapsto G_{2}^{-1}P_{+}((G_{1}^{-1})^{*}f) 
 
 
 of  T F T{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{F}  is bounded. It is not hard to see that the operator ( 2.2 ) is bounded if
and only if the weighted estimate 
 

 
 (2.3) 
 
 ‖ P + ​ f ‖ L 2 ​ ( V ) ≤ C ​ ‖ f ‖ L 2 ​ ( W ) ∀ f ∈ L 2 ​ ( W ) \displaystyle\|P_{+}f\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(V)}\leq C\|f\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(W)}\qquad\forall f\in L^{2}(W) 
 
 
 with weights  W = G 1 ​ G 1 ∗ W=G_{1}G_{1}^{*} ,  V = ( G 2 − 1 ) ∗ ​ G 2 − 1 V=(G_{2}^{-1})^{*}G_{2}^{-1}  holds, and the norm of the operator is the
best constant  C C  in ( 2.3 ). This looks like a two weight inequality, but in reality it
reduces to the one weight case. Namely, the invertibility of  T F T{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{F}  implies that  F F  is
invertible in  L ∞ L^{\infty} , and therefore it is not hard to check that the weights  V V  and  W W  are
equivalent, i.e. 
 

 
 
 A − 1 ​ W ≤ V ≤ A ​ W , for some  ​ A ∈ ( 0 , ∞ ) ; \displaystyle A^{-1}W\leq V\leq AW,\qquad\text{for some }A\in(0,\infty); 
 
 
 the inequality is understood as a matrix inequality. Therefore at the cost of the constant  A A ,
( 2.3 ) is equivalent to the one weight estimate 
 

 
 (2.4) 
 
 ‖ P + ​ f ‖ L 2 ​ ( W ) ≤ C ​ ‖ f ‖ L 2 ​ ( W ) ∀ f ∈ L 2 ​ ( W ) , \displaystyle\|P_{+}f\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(W)}\leq C\|f\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(W)}\qquad\forall f\in L^{2}(W), 
 
 
 i.e. to the boundedness of  P + P_{+}  in the weighted space  L 2 ​ ( W ) L^{2}(W) . On the real line the Riesz
Projection  P + P_{+}  and the Hilbert Transform are related as  ℋ = − i ​ P + + i ⁡ ( 𝐈 − P + ) \mathcal{H}=-iP_{+}+i(\mathbf{I}-P_{+}) , so  P + P_{+} 
is bounded in  L 2 ​ ( W ) L^{2}(W)  if and only if  ℋ \mathcal{H}  is, and the norms are equivalent in the sense of
two-sided estimates. 
 
 
 
 2.2.  Some scalar history 

 
 As we already mentioned above, the first solution for scalar weights is given by the famous
Helson–Szegö Theorem  [ 13 ] . Later in  [ 15 ]  a real variable characterization of such
weights was obtained, and the Muckenhoupt  A 2 A_{2}  condition came to prominence. 
 
 
 Finding quantitative estimates of the weighted norms of singular integral operators in terms of
 A p A_{p}  characteristics was a natural problem to investigate. The interest in sharp estimates peaked
after the paper  [ 39 ] , where the sharp weighted estimate  ≲ 𝓠 \lesssim\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}  for the
Beurling–Ahlfors
Transform was obtained and used to solve a long standing problem about regularity of the Beltrami
PDE at the critical exponent (Conjecture 12.9 in  [ 21 ]  in dimension  2 2 ). Note that for other
exponents  any  weighted estimate works, see  [ 1 ] , but for the critical exponent the
sharp estimate  ≲ 𝓠 \lesssim\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}  is required. The proof of this sharp estimate in  [ 39 ]  featured a
novel  implicit Bellman function  approach using the heat equation. 
 
 
 For the Hilbert Transform the scalar  A 2 A_{2}  conjecture was resolved in  [ 37 ] .
The main part of the proof was purely dyadic and provided the upper bound  ≲ 𝓠 \lesssim\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}  for the
so-called Haar Shift  Ш \mathord{\mathchoice{\mbox{Ш}}{\mbox{Ш}}{\mbox{Ш}}{\mbox{Ш}}} 
 [ 36 ] . The estimate for the Hilbert Transform follows, since the Hilbert Transform is (up to a
multiplicative constant) the average of the Haar Shifts over all translations and dilations of the
dyadic lattice: an idea that had great impact on the study of the Hilbert Transform. 
 
 
 The area generated much interest at one of the forefronts of analysis, which resulted in
extraordinary development that goes far beyond the interest in weights, see e.g. the following
articles and the literature cited therein:  [ 25 ] ,  [ 16 ] ,  [ 18 ] ,  [ 28 ] ,
 [ 24 ] ,  [ 8 ] ,  [ 27 ] . 
 
 
 For general Calderón-Zygmund operators the  A 2 A_{2}  conjecture was settled in  [ 16 ]  via
representation of such operators as the average of generalized Haar Shifts and and sharp estimates
of such shifts. 
 
 
 Later, as a result of all this interest, the highly influential and flexible sparse
domination technique appeared in pioneering works begun by A. Lerner that found its greatest impact
in the development of the pointwise sparse domination. Here such a vast literature exists by now
that we cite only a few early papers:  [ 27 ] ,  [ 28 ] ,  [ 8 ] ,  [ 24 ] . Again, these
sequels included completely new, brilliant ideas and received much recognition. 
 
 
 
 2.3.  Vector history 

 
 The matrix  𝐀 2 \mathbf{A}_{2} 
condition was introduced in  [ 42 ] , where it was conjectured that it is necessary and sufficient for the
boundedness of the Hilbert Transform. This conjecture was proved in  [ 45 ] , which started the
development of the theory of matrix-valued weights. 
 
 
 After much development in the scalar case, with a number of proofs giving the linear bound,
interest spiked in the matrix case, but the matrix  𝐀 2 \mathbf{A}_{2}  conjecture remained an intriguing
question for years. The conjectured linear bound in the matrix case was a puzzling, long standing
open question, resisting the abundance of new techniques that had appeared in the scalar theory
over the last twenty years. Still, there was a firm belief in scalar estimates holding in the
matrix case. For example for the Christ–Goldberg maximal function  [ 7 ]  this was confirmed
early on in  [ 20 ] . The belief in a linear estimate for  ℋ \mathcal{H}  was strengthened as the matrix
weighted Carleson Embedding Theorem was finally proved  [ 9 ]  circumventing the
non-commutativity of matrix multiplication with an elegant argument. Later, the dyadic square
function with matrix weight was shown to have a linear dependence  [ 17 ] ,  [ 44 ] .
All these results hinted that the Hilbert Transform and other Calderón–Zygmund operators
should have an upper bound of the form  ≲ 𝓠 \lesssim\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}} . 
 
 
 Turning to the Hilbert Transform, the constant one obtains by combing ideas from the scalar
estimate  [ 38 ]  with  [ 45 ]  and tracking certain constants in  [ 45 ] , was improved
in  [ 3 ]  to an estimate of the form  ≲ 𝓠 3 / 2 ​ log ⁡ 𝓠 \lesssim\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}^{3/2}\log\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}} .
The logarithmic term was then dropped in  [ 32 ]  using the so-called convex body sparse
domination, but the  3 / 2 3/2  power remained. Many alternative proofs were known to us, all with power
 3 / 2 3/2 . 
 
 
 There were however some hints that the answer in not that simple.
An interesting mile stone was the failure of the convex body Carleson Embedding Theorem as well as
failure of the boundedness of the convex body Maximal Function as demonstrated in  [ 31 ] .
In addition, while the matrix weighted Carleson Embedding Theorem holds  [ 9 ] , its bilinear
version (a crucial ingredient in some sharp scalar proofs) fails so miserably that a trivial
counterexample shows not only that it fails, but that the  𝐀 2 \mathbf{A}_{2}  characteristic is not the
right quantity at all  [ 12 ] . 
 
 
 But on the other hand, it was shown in  [ 32 ]  that a simple sparse operator, based on a
sparse family where each
sparse interval has at most one sparse descendant, had the expected linear bound in the matrix
case. Dominating singular integral operators using sparse families does not generally yield
such a simple collection. However, analyzing the construction of dominating sparse operators and
using the  T ⁡ ( 1 ) T(1) -Theorem for matrix weights  [ 2 ]  one can show that for weights with one
singularity the estimate is reduced to estimating simple sparse operators.
This means that any counterexample should be
quite complicated, and cannot be anything like a weight with one or finitely many singularities. 
 
 
 
 2.4.  The idea of our counterexample 

 
 In this paper, we first show the desired lower bound for some dyadic operators in terms of the
 dyadic   𝐀 2 \mathbf{A}_{2}  condition, see ( 3.1 ) for the definition. 
 
 
 The weight we construct is necessarily quite complicated, and we construct it using the martingale
approach, i.e. by tracking the averages of the weight  W W  and its inverse  W − 1 W^{-1} . 
 
 
 In our top down approach, we apply subtle rotations of the eigenvectors of the averages in one step
(which is only possible in the vector case) and in the
next step we disbalance the weight, which is the action also giving the sharp lower bound in the
scalar case. The rotation, which is only possible in the vector case, adds some extra blow up. 
 
 
 For the constructed weight we then prove the estimate from below
 ≳ ( [ W ] 𝐀 2 dy ) 3 / 2 \gtrsim([W]{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbf{A}_{2}}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm dy}})^{3/2}  for several dyadic operators relevant to our task. 
 
 
 We start with a special sparse paraproduct, which will be the main building block for all the
estimates. We also show that the classical Haar Shift  Ш \mathord{\mathchoice{\mbox{Ш}}{\mbox{Ш}}{\mbox{Ш}}{\mbox{Ш}}}  has the desired bound.
We then prove the lower bound for the special dyadic operator  ℋ dy \mathcal{H}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm dy}}  which in our situation serves
as a dyadic model for the Hilbert Transform. 
 
 
 It is well known that the average of the Haar Shift gives the Hilbert Transform – as such, getting
upper estimates for Hilbert Transform from the estimates of the Haar Shift is more immediate,
intuitive and obvious; this idea has been used for years. 
 
 
 The case of lower bounds is much more involved.
In our passage from the dyadic operators  ℋ dy \mathcal{H}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm dy}}  to the Hilbert Transform we
take inspiration from Bourgain  [ 5 ]  in the use of separating frequencies (the
periodizations). A modification of his construction by considering the second order martingale
differences allowed us to obtain the lower bounds for
the Hilbert Transform  ℋ \mathcal{H}  (with a modified weight) from the lower bounds for the dyadic operator
 ℋ dy \mathcal{H}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm dy}}  . The choice of the dyadic operator  ℋ dy \mathcal{H}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm dy}}  is forced upon us by the construction, we do not
have any freedom here. 
 
 
 But the construction does not stop here. The weight we constructed for the dyadic operator and its
periodization are in the dyadic  𝐀 2 \mathbf{A}_{2} 
class  𝐀 2 dy \mathbf{A}_{2}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm dy}} ; however they both have large jumps and do not satisfy the classical matrix
 𝐀 2 \mathbf{A}_{2}  condition. 
 
 
 To overcome this difficulty we borrow from the technique of quasi-periodization, developed by
F. Nazarov  [ 30 ] 
in his counterexample to the Sarason
conjecture. This technique allows us to get the classical  𝐀 2 \mathbf{A}_{2}  condition, but only in a two
weight situation. However in the present paper we are in the one weight
situation, and thus have even less freedom. So, in the final step we use a modification of the
so-called  iterated remodeling  technique, developed in  [ 23 ]  for the scalar case. 
 
 
 
 
 3.  Preliminaries 

 
 3.1.  Some definitions 

 
 Our construction starts in the dyadic martingale setting, so let us remind the reader of the main
definitions. A  dyadic interval  in this paper is an interval  I I  of the form 
 

 
 
 2 k ​ ( [ 0 , 1 ) + j ) , j , k ∈ ℤ . \displaystyle 2^{k}\Bigl([0,1)+j\Bigr),\qquad j,k\in\mathbb{Z}. 
 
 
 For a dyadic interval  I I  we denote by  𝒟 ⁡ ( I ) \mathscr{D}(I)  all dyadic subintervals of  I I  (including  I I ). 
 
 
 We will use the notation  I 0 := [ 0 , 1 ) I^{0}:=[0,1) , and we often abbreviate  𝒟 := 𝒟 ⁡ ( I 0 ) \mathscr{D}:=\mathscr{D}(I^{0}) . For a
dyadic interval  I I  we denote by  ch k ⁡ ( I ) \operatorname{ch}^{k}(I) ,  k ∈ ℕ k\in\mathbb{N}  its descendants of  k k th generation,
i.e. the the set of all dyadic subintervals of  I I  of length  2 − k ​ | I | 2^{-k}|I| . We will also use the
notation  𝒟 k := ch k ⁡ ( I 0 ) \mathscr{D}_{k}:=\operatorname{ch}^{k}(I^{0}) . 
 
 
 Finally, we denote by  I + I_{+}  and  I − I_{-}  the right and left child of  I I  respectively. 
 
 
 We say that a weight  W W  on  I 0 I^{0}  satisfies the  dyadic  matrix  𝐀 2 \mathbf{A}_{2}  condition, and write
 W ∈ 𝐀 2 dy W\in\mathbf{A}_{2}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm dy}}  if 
 

 
 (3.1) 
 
 [ W ] 𝐀 2 dy := sup I ∈ 𝒟 ‖ ⟨ W ⟩ I 1 / 2 ​ ⟨ W − 1 ⟩ I 1 / 2 ‖ 2 < ∞ ; \displaystyle[W]{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbf{A}_{2}}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm dy}}:=\sup_{I\in\mathscr{D}}\left\|\langle W\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{1/2}\langle W^{-1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{1/2}\right\|^{2}<\infty\,; 
 
 
 the difference from the classical matrix  𝐀 2 \mathbf{A}_{2}  condition is that in the dyadic  𝐀 2 dy \mathbf{A}_{2}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm dy}} 
condition we take the supremum only over dyadic intervals, not over all intervals as in
( 1.1 ). 
 
 
 
 3.2.  Properties of  𝐀 2 \mathbf{A}_{2}  weights 

 
 For Hermitian matrices  A A ,  B B  the notation  A ≤ B A\leq B  means that the matrix  B − A B-A  is positive
semidefinite (denoted  B − A ≥ 0 B-A\geq 0 ). 
 
 
 Lemma 3.1 . 
 
 Let  𝐯 \mathbf{v} ,  𝐰 \mathbf{w}  be  d × d d\times d  positive definite matrices. Then the inequality
 ‖ 𝐯 1 / 2 ​ 𝐰 1 / 2 ‖ 2 ≤ 𝓠 \|\mathbf{v}^{1/2}\mathbf{w}^{1/2}\|^{2}\leq\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}  is equivalent to 
 

 
 (3.2) 
 
 𝐯 ≤ 𝓠 ​ 𝐰 − 1 , or, equivalently 𝐰 ≤ 𝓠 ​ 𝐯 − 1 . \displaystyle\mathbf{v}\leq\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}\mathbf{w}^{-1},\qquad\text{or, equivalently}\qquad\mathbf{w}\leq\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}\mathbf{v}^{-1}. 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Taking the Hermitian square of  𝐯 1 / 2 ​ 𝐰 1 / 2 \mathbf{v}^{1/2}\mathbf{w}^{1/2}  and recalling that  ‖ A ‖ 2 = ‖ A ∗ ​ A ‖ \|A\|^{2}=\|A^{*}A\|  we see
that the condition  ‖ 𝐯 1 / 2 ​ 𝐰 1 / 2 ‖ 2 ≤ 𝓠 \|\mathbf{v}^{1/2}\mathbf{w}^{1/2}\|^{2}\leq\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}  can be rewritten as 
 

 
 
 ‖ 𝐰 1 / 2 ​ 𝐯𝐰 1 / 2 ‖ \displaystyle\left\|\mathbf{w}^{1/2}\mathbf{v}\mathbf{w}^{1/2}\right\| 
 ≤ 𝓠 \displaystyle\leq\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}} 
 
 or, equivalently 
 
 
 𝐰 1 / 2 ​ 𝐯𝐰 1 / 2 \displaystyle\mathbf{w}^{1/2}\mathbf{v}\mathbf{w}^{1/2} 
 ≤ 𝓠 ​ 𝐈 . \displaystyle\leq\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}\mathbf{I}. 
 
 
 Left and right multiplying the last inequality by  𝐰 − 1 / 2 \mathbf{w}^{-1/2}  we get the first inequality in
( 3.2 ); the second inequality in ( 3.2 ) is trivially equivalent to the
first one.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Lemma 3.2 . 
 
 Let  W W  be a  d × d d\times d  matrix weight. Then 
 

 
 (3.3) 
 
 ⟨ W ⟩ I − 1 ≤ ⟨ W − 1 ⟩ I , or equivalently ⟨ W − 1 ⟩ I − 1 ≤ ⟨ W ⟩ I . \displaystyle\langle W\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{-1}\leq\langle W^{-1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I},\quad\text{or equivalently}\qquad\langle W^{-1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{-1}\leq\langle W\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}. 
 
 
 Moreover
 ‖ ⟨ W ⟩ I 1 / 2 ​ ⟨ W − 1 ⟩ I 1 / 2 ‖ 2 ≤ 𝓠 \|\langle W\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{1/2}\langle W^{-1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{1/2}\|^{2}\leq\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}  if and only if 
 

 
 (3.4) 
 
 ⟨ W − 1 ⟩ I ≤ 𝓠 ​ ⟨ W ⟩ I − 1 , or equivalently ⟨ W ⟩ I ≤ 𝓠 ​ ⟨ W − 1 ⟩ I − 1 . \displaystyle\langle W^{-1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\leq\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}\langle W\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{-1},\quad\text{or equivalently}\qquad\langle W\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\leq\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}\langle W^{-1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{-1}. 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Inequalities ( 3.4 ) follow immediately from Lemma  3.1 . As for the
inequalities ( 3.3 ), they are just Property 1 of the  𝒜 p , q \mathcal{A}_{p,q}  weights in
 [ 33 , s. 2.5] , see also  [ 26 , Lemma 1.4] ; in both cases  p = q = 2 p=q=2 . 
 
 
 To save the reader a trip to a library and the hurdle of translating from a different
(mathematical)
language, we present a proof here: it is the same proof as in  [ 33 ,  26 ]  written in
different notation. Namely, we get for arbitrary  𝐞 ∈ 𝔽 d \mathbf{e}\in\mathbb{F}^{d} 
 

 
 
 ⟨ ( ⟨ W ⟩ I − 1 ​ 𝐞 , 𝐞 ) 𝔽 d ⟩ I \displaystyle\left\langle\left(\langle W\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{-1}\mathbf{e},\mathbf{e}\right){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbb{F}^{d}}\right\rangle_{I} 
 = ⟨ ( W 1 / 2 ⟨ W ⟩ I − 1 𝐞 , W − 1 / 2 𝐞 ) 𝔽 d ⟩ I \displaystyle=\left\langle\left(W^{1/2}\langle W\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{-1}\mathbf{e},W^{-1/2}\mathbf{e}\right){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbb{F}^{d}}\right\rangle_{I} 
 
 
 
 
 ≤ ⟨ ( ⟨ W ⟩ I − 1 ​ W ​ ⟨ W ⟩ I − 1 ​ 𝐞 , 𝐞 ) 𝔽 d ⟩ I 1 / 2 ​ ⟨ ( W − 1 ​ 𝐞 , 𝐞 ) 𝔽 d ⟩ I 1 / 2 \displaystyle\leq\left\langle\left(\langle W\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{-1}W\langle W\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{-1}\mathbf{e},\mathbf{e}\right){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbb{F}^{d}}\right\rangle_{I}^{1/2}\left\langle\left(W^{-1}\mathbf{e},\mathbf{e}\right){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbb{F}^{d}}\right\rangle_{I}^{1/2} 
 
 
 
 
 = ( ⟨ W ⟩ I − 1 ​ 𝐞 , 𝐞 ) 𝔽 d 1 / 2 ​ ( ⟨ W − 1 ⟩ I ​ 𝐞 , 𝐞 ) 𝔽 d 1 / 2 . \displaystyle=\left(\langle W\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{-1}\mathbf{e},\mathbf{e}\right){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbb{F}^{d}}^{1/2}\left(\langle W^{-1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\mathbf{e},\mathbf{e}\right){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbb{F}^{d}}^{1/2}. 
 
 
 Dividing both sides by  ( ⟨ W ⟩ I − 1 ​ 𝐞 , 𝐞 ) 𝔽 d 1 / 2 \left(\langle W\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{-1}\mathbf{e},\mathbf{e}\right){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbb{F}^{d}}^{1/2}  and squaring, we
get the first inequality in ( 3.3 ); the second inequality is trivially equivalent to
the first one.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Remark 3.3 . 
 
 It is trivial and well-known that the matrix rescaling of the weight  W ↦ A ​ W ​ A W\mapsto AWA , where
 A = A ∗ > 0 A=A^{*}>0  is a constant matrix, does not change the  𝐀 2 \mathbf{A}_{2}  characteristic, 
 

 
 
 [ W ] 𝐀 2 = [ A ​ W ​ A ] 𝐀 2 . [W]{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbf{A}_{2}}=[AWA]{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbf{A}_{2}}. 
 
 
 It is also well-known and easy to see that for an operator of the form  T ⊗ 𝐈 T\otimes\mathbf{I} , where  T T 
is
an operator acting on scalar-valued functions, its norms in  L 2 ​ ( W ) L^{2}(W)  and in  L 2 ​ ( A ​ W ​ A ) L^{2}(AWA)  coincide. 
 
 
 
 Lemma 3.4 . 
 
 Let  𝐰 = 𝐰 ∗ > 0 \mathbf{w}=\mathbf{w}^{*}>0  and  𝐯 = 𝐯 ∗ > 0 \mathbf{v}=\mathbf{v}^{*}>0  be  d × d d\times d  matrices satisfying 
 

 
 (3.5) 
 
 𝐯 − 1 ≤ 𝐰 ≤ 𝓠 ​ 𝐯 − 1 . \displaystyle\mathbf{v}^{-1}\leq\mathbf{w}\leq\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}\mathbf{v}^{-1}. 
 
 
 Then, given an interval  I I , there exists an  𝐀 2 \mathbf{A}_{2}  weight  W W  on  I I , constant on the children of
 I I , such that 
 

 
 
 ⟨ W ⟩ I = 𝐰 , ⟨ W − 1 ⟩ I = 𝐯 , \displaystyle\langle W\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}=\mathbf{w},\qquad\langle W^{-1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}=\mathbf{v}, 
 
 
 and  [ W ] 𝐀 2 ≤ 𝓠 [W]{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbf{A}_{2}}\leq\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}} . 
 
 
 
 Remark 3.5 . 
 
 In our construction we only need the fact that the resulting weight  W W  is a  dyadic   𝐀 2 \mathbf{A}_{2} 
weight satisfying  [ W ] 𝐀 2 dy ≤ 𝓠 [W]{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbf{A}_{2}}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm dy}}\leq\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}} , so the reader can skip the part of the proof
below dealing with the estimate  [ W ] 𝐀 2 ≤ 𝓠 [W]{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbf{A}_{2}}\leq\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}} . However this estimate is of independent
interest, so we present its proof here. 
 
 
 
 Proof of Lemma  3.4 . 
 
 As it was mentioned above in Remark  3.3  the  𝐀 2 \mathbf{A}_{2}  characteristic
 [ W ] 𝐀 2 [W]{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbf{A}_{2}}  is invariant under the matrix rescaling  W ↦ A ​ W ​ A W\mapsto AWA ,  A = A ∗ > 0 A=A^{*}>0 .
The condition ( 3.5 ) is also invariant under the matrix rescaling  𝐰 ↦ A ​ 𝐰 ​ A \mathbf{w}\mapsto A\mathbf{w}A ,
 𝐯 ↦ A − 1 ​ 𝐯 ​ A − 1 \mathbf{v}\mapsto A^{-1}\mathbf{v}A^{-1} ,  A = A ∗ > 0 A=A^{*}>0 , so without loss of generality we may assume  𝐰 = 𝐈 \mathbf{w}=\mathbf{I} . 
 
 
 Define  W := ( 𝐈 + Δ ) ​ 𝟏 I + + ( 𝐈 − Δ ) ​ 𝟏 I − W:=(\mathbf{I}+\Delta)\mathbf{1}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I_{+}}+(\mathbf{I}-\Delta)\mathbf{1}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I_{-}} , where  Δ = Δ ∗ ≥ 0 \Delta=\Delta^{*}\geq 0  is a
constant matrix to be found. Through the
use of Neumann series or simple algebra one checks that 
 

 
 
 ( ⟨ W ⟩ I + + ⟨ W ⟩ I − ) / 2 = ( ( 𝐈 + Δ ) − 1 + ( 𝐈 − Δ ) − 1 ) / 2 = ( 𝐈 − Δ 2 ) − 1 . \left(\langle W\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I_{+}}+\langle W\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I_{-}}\right)/2=\left((\mathbf{I}+\Delta)^{-1}+(\mathbf{I}-\Delta)^{-1}\right)/2=(\mathbf{I}-\Delta^{2})^{-1}. 
 
 
 The above average should be equal to  𝐯 \mathbf{v} , so
 Δ 2 = 𝐈 − 𝐯 − 1 ≥ 0 \Delta^{2}=\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{v}^{-1}\geq 0  (because  𝐯 − 1 ≤ 𝐰 \mathbf{v}^{-1}\leq\mathbf{w} ). Taking the unique positive root gives
us the desired  Δ \Delta . 
 
 
 Since the weight  W W  is constant on  I ± I_{\pm} , it is trivial that  [ W ] 𝐀 2 dy ≤ 𝓠 [W]{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbf{A}_{2}}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm dy}}\leq\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}} . The
reader only interested in the construction of the counterexample can stop here, see Remark
 3.5 . 
 
 
 But for an inquisitive reader let us show that in fact  [ W ] 𝐀 2 ≤ 𝓠 [W]{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbf{A}_{2}}\leq\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}} . Take an interval
 J ⊂ I J\subset I , and let
 J 1 := J ∩ I + J_{1}:=J\cap I_{+} ,  α := | J 1 | / | J | \alpha:=|J_{1}|/|J| . Then, still assuming  𝐰 = 𝐈 \mathbf{w}=\mathbf{I}  and recalling that  𝐯 = ( 𝐈 − Δ 2 ) − 1 \mathbf{v}=(\mathbf{I}-\Delta^{2})^{-1} , we get 
 

 
 
 ⟨ W ⟩ J \displaystyle\langle W\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\!J} 
 = α ⁡ ( 𝐈 + Δ ) + ( 1 − α ) ​ ( 𝐈 − Δ ) = 𝐈 + ( 2 ​ α − 1 ) ​ Δ = : A α \displaystyle=\alpha(\mathbf{I}+\Delta)+(1-\alpha)(\mathbf{I}-\Delta)=\mathbf{I}+(2\alpha-1)\Delta=:A_{\alpha} 
 
 
 
 ⟨ W − 1 ⟩ J \displaystyle\langle W^{-1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\!J} 
 = α 𝐈 + Δ + 1 − α 𝐈 − Δ = 𝐈 − ( 2 ​ α − 1 ) ​ Δ 𝐈 − Δ 2 = : B α 𝐈 − Δ 2 \displaystyle=\frac{\alpha}{\mathbf{I}+\Delta}+\frac{1-\alpha}{\mathbf{I}-\Delta}=\frac{\mathbf{I}-(2\alpha-1)\Delta}{\mathbf{I}-\Delta^{2}}=:\frac{B_{\alpha}}{\mathbf{I}-\Delta^{2}} 
 
 
 
 
 = B α 1 / 2 ​ ( 𝐈 − Δ 2 ) − 1 ​ B α 1 / 2 = B α 1 / 2 ​ 𝐯 ​ B α 1 / 2 . \displaystyle=B_{\alpha}^{1/2}\left(\mathbf{I}-\Delta^{2}\right)^{-1}B_{\alpha}^{1/2}=B_{\alpha}^{1/2}\mathbf{v}B_{\alpha}^{1/2}. 
 
 
 The estimate  𝐰 ≤ 𝓠 ​ 𝐯 − 1 \mathbf{w}\leq\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}\mathbf{v}^{-1}  is equivalent to  𝐯 ≤ 𝓠 ​ 𝐰 − 1 = 𝓠 ​ 𝐈 \mathbf{v}\leq\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}\mathbf{w}^{-1}=\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}\mathbf{I} , so
 ‖ 𝐯 1 / 2 ‖ ≤ 𝓠 1 / 2 \|\mathbf{v}^{1/2}\|\leq\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}^{1/2} . The trivial scalar inequality 
 

 
 
 1 − x ≤ 1 1 + x 1-x\leq\frac{1}{1+x} 
 
 
 implies the matrix version 
 

 
 
 B α = 𝐈 − ( 2 ​ α − 1 ) ​ Δ ≤ ( 𝐈 + ( 2 ​ α − 1 ) ​ Δ ) − 1 = A α − 1 , \displaystyle B_{\alpha}=\mathbf{I}-(2\alpha-1)\Delta\leq(\mathbf{I}+(2\alpha-1)\Delta)^{-1}=A_{\alpha}^{-1}, 
 
 
 and since  ‖ 𝐯 1 / 2 ‖ ≤ 𝓠 1 / 2 \|\mathbf{v}^{1/2}\|\leq\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}^{1/2}  we conclude that 
 

 
 
 ⟨ W − 1 ⟩ J = B α 1 / 2 ​ 𝐯 ​ B α 1 / 2 ≤ 𝓠 ​ B α ≤ 𝓠 ​ A α − 1 = 𝓠 ​ ⟨ W ⟩ J − 1 . \displaystyle\langle W^{-1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\!J}=B_{\alpha}^{1/2}\mathbf{v}B_{\alpha}^{1/2}\leq\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}B_{\alpha}\leq\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}A_{\alpha}^{-1}=\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}\langle W\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\!J}^{-1}. 
 
 
 Since  J J  is an arbitrary interval, Lemma  3.2  implies that  [ W ] 𝐀 2 ≤ 𝓠 [W]{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbf{A}_{2}}\leq\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}} .
∎ 
 
 
 
 
 3.3.  Representation of weights as martingales 

 
 It is a common place in dyadic harmonic analysis to treat functions as martingales, i.e. not to
track functions themselves, but their averages over dyadic intervals. Namely, if we define
the averaging operators (conditional expectations)  𝐄 I \mathbf{E}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I} ,  𝐄 k \mathbf{E}_{k} , 
 

 
 
 𝐄 I ​ φ = ⟨ φ ⟩ I ​ 𝟏 I , 𝐄 k := ∑ I ∈ 𝒟 k 𝐄 I , \displaystyle\mathbf{E}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\varphi=\langle\varphi\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\mathbf{1}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I},\qquad\mathbf{E}_{k}:=\sum_{I\in\mathscr{D}_{k}}\mathbf{E}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}, 
 
 
 then given a function  φ ∈ L 1 ​ ( I 0 ) \varphi\in L^{1}(I^{0})  we can see that the sequence  ( φ n ) n ≥ 0 (\varphi_{n})_{n\geq 0} ,
 φ n := 𝐄 n ​ φ \varphi_{n}:=\mathbf{E}_{n}\varphi  is a martingale, meaning that  𝐄 n ​ φ n + 1 = φ n \mathbf{E}_{n}\varphi_{n+1}=\varphi_{n} . 
 
 
 From a naïve point of view one just tracks the average  φ I := ⟨ φ ⟩ I \varphi{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}:=\langle\varphi\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I} ; the
martingale property simply means that  φ I = ( φ I + + φ I − ) / 2 \varphi{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}=(\varphi{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I_{+}}+\varphi{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I_{-}})/2  for all  I ∈ 𝒟 I\in\mathscr{D} .
Of course not all martingales come from functions, the so-called  uniform integrability  is
the necessary and sufficient condition. But in our construction all martingales will stabilize
after
finitely many steps, so they will be trivially given by averages of some functions. 
 
 
 The following martingale difference decomposition is well known, 
 

 
 
 φ n = ∑ I ∈ 𝒟 n 𝐄 I ​ f = 𝐄 0 ​ φ + ∑ k = 0 n − 1 ∑ I ∈ 𝒟 k Δ I ​ φ = ⟨ φ ⟩ I 0 + ∑ k = 0 n − 1 ∑ I ∈ 𝒟 k ( φ , h I ) ​ h I ; \varphi_{n}=\sum_{I\in\mathscr{D}_{n}}\mathbf{E}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}f=\mathbf{E}_{0}\varphi+\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\sum_{I\in\mathscr{D}_{k}}\Delta{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\varphi=\langle\varphi\rangle_{I^{0}}+\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\sum_{I\in\mathscr{D}_{k}}(\varphi,h{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I})h_{I}; 
 
 
 here 
 

 
 
 Δ I ​ φ := 𝐄 I ​ φ − ∑ J ∈ ℋ ⁡ ( I ) 𝐄 J ​ φ = ( φ , h I ) ​ h I , \displaystyle\Delta{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\varphi:=\mathbf{E}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\varphi-\sum_{J\in\mathcal{H}(I)}\mathbf{E}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\!J}\varphi=(\varphi,h{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I})h{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}, 
 
 
 and  h I := | I | − 1 / 2 ( 𝟏 I + − 𝟏 I − ) h{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}:=|I|^{-1/2}\left(\mathbf{1}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I_{+}}-\mathbf{1}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I_{-}}\right)  is the normalized Haar function. 
 
 
 In our construction of the (dyadic)  𝐀 2 \mathbf{A}_{2}  weights we will track the averages  𝐰 I = ⟨ W ⟩ I \mathbf{w}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}=\langle W\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I} ,  𝐯 I = ⟨ V ⟩ I \mathbf{v}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}=\langle V\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I} , satisfying the matrix inequalities 
 

 
 (3.6) 
 
 𝐯 I − 1 ≤ 𝐰 I ≤ 𝓠 ​ 𝐯 I − 1 . \displaystyle\mathbf{v}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{-1}\leq\mathbf{w}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\leq\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}\mathbf{v}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{-1}. 
 
 
 The martingales will terminate (i.e. stabilize) after finitely many steps, so trivially they are
obtained from weights  W W ,  V V . Adding one more step and using Lemma  3.4  there, we can
guarantee that  V = W − 1 V=W^{-1} . The fact that on each step the inequalities ( 3.6 ) are
satisfied implies that the resulting weight is a  dyadic   𝐀 2 \mathbf{A}_{2}  weight and that
 [ W ] 𝐀 2 dy ≤ 𝓠 [W]{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbf{A}_{2}}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm dy}}\leq\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}} . 
 
 
 Remark 3.6 . 
 
 We do not claim here that the weight  W W  is an  𝐀 2 \mathbf{A}_{2}  weight, we only claim that it is a
 dyadic   𝐀 2 \mathbf{A}_{2}  weight. The constructed dyadic weight  W W  gives the lower bound
 ≳ 𝓠 3 / 2 \gtrsim\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}^{3/2}  for
the norms of some dyadic operators in  L 2 ​ ( W ) L^{2}(W) . 
 
 
 To get the lower bound for the Hilbert Transform  ℋ \mathcal{H}  we perform the so-called  iterated
remodeling  on the weight  W W , to get a new weight  W ~ \widetilde{W} . This new weight will satisfy the
 𝐀 2 \mathbf{A}_{2} 
condition with  [ W ] 𝐀 2 ≤ 16 ​ 𝓠 [W]{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbf{A}_{2}}\leq 16\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}} , and  ‖ ℋ ‖ L 2 ​ ( W ) → L 2 ​ ( W ) ≳ 𝓠 3 / 2 \|\mathcal{H}\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(W)\to L^{2}(W)}\gtrsim\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}^{3/2} . 
 
 
 
 
 
 4.  Construction of the dyadic weight 

 
 4.1.  Families of weights 

 
 In this section we will construct a family of weighs  W = W 𝓠 , δ 0 W=W{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}},\delta_{0}}  depending
on the dyadic  𝐀 2 \mathbf{A}_{2}  characteristic  𝓠 = : [ W ] 𝐀 2 dy \boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}=:[W]{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbf{A}_{2}}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm dy}}  and some auxiliary small
parameter  δ 0 := q ( β 0 / α 0 ) 1 / 2 = q E 0 − 1 / 2 \delta_{0}:=q(\beta_{0}/\alpha_{0})^{1/2}=qE_{0}^{-1/2} ,  q = 0.1 q=0.1 , where
 E 0 := α 0 / β 0 E_{0}:=\alpha_{0}/\beta_{0}  is
the initial eccentricity of the average  ⟨ W − 1 ⟩ I 0 \langle W^{-1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}} , see ( 4.1 ). We will then show in Sections  5 ,
 6  that for
any sufficiently large
 𝓠 \boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}  we will have
for all sufficiently small  δ 0 \delta_{0}  the desired lower bound  c ​ 𝓠 3 / 2 c\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}^{3/2}  for relevant
dyadic operators. 
 
 
 For typographical reasons we will usually omit the indices  𝓠 \boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}  and  δ 0 \delta_{0} ,
but the reader should keep in mind that  W W  is not one weight but the whole family. 
 
 
 Let us introduce some notation, simplifying the write-up. The symbols  ≲ \lesssim  and  ≳ \gtrsim  mean
the inequalities with some absolute constants, and  ≍ \asymp  means the two sided estimate with
absolute constants.
We will use the notation
 ≍ δ 0 < \underset{\delta_{0}<}{\asymp} ,  ≲ δ 0 < \underset{\delta_{0}<}{\lesssim} ,  ≳ δ 0 < \underset{\delta_{0}<}{\gtrsim} 
to indicate
that the estimates hold for sufficiently small  δ 0 \delta_{0} , and that the same implied constants
work for  all  variables involved and for
 all  sufficiently small  δ 0 \delta_{0} .
Similarly we will use the notation  O δ 0 < ​ ( X ) O{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\delta_{0}<}(X)  for a quantity whose absolute value is
bounded by  C ​ X CX  with an absolute constant  C C  for all sufficiently small  δ 0 \delta_{0}  (and for all
variables involved). 
 
 
 We
will use  ≤ δ 0 < \underset{\delta_{0}<}{\leq} ,  ≥ δ 0 < \underset{\delta_{0}<}{\geq}  to indicate that the inequalities
hold for all sufficiently small  δ 0 \delta_{0}  (with the constants explicitly presented). 
 
 
 And finally, the symbol  ∼ δ 0 < \underset{\delta_{0}<}{\sim}  means that the ratio of right and left hand
side is as close to  1 1  as we want, if  δ 0 \delta_{0}  is sufficiently small. 
 
 
 
 4.2.  Starting point 

 
 We will start with the interval  I = I 0 I=I^{0} , and on this interval the averages  ⟨ W − 1 ⟩ I = 𝐯 I 0 \langle W^{-1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}=\mathbf{v}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}}  and  ⟨ W ⟩ I 0 = 𝐰 I 0 \langle W\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}}=\mathbf{w}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}}  will be diagonal in the same
orthonormal basis  𝐚 I 0 \mathbf{a}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}} ,  𝐛 I 0 \mathbf{b}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}} , 
 

 
 (4.1) 
 
 ⟨ W − 1 ⟩ I 0 = 𝐯 I 0 = α 0 ​ 𝐚 I 0 ​ 𝐚 I 0 ∗ + β 0 ​ 𝐛 I 0 ​ 𝐛 I 0 ∗ , ⟨ W ⟩ I 0 = 𝐰 I 0 = β 0 # ​ 𝐚 I 0 ​ 𝐚 I 0 ∗ + α 0 # ​ 𝐛 I 0 ​ 𝐛 I 0 ∗ . \displaystyle\langle W^{-1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}}=\mathbf{v}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}}=\alpha_{0}\mathbf{a}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}}\mathbf{a}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}}^{*}+\beta_{0}\mathbf{b}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}}\mathbf{b}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}}^{*},\qquad\langle W\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}}=\mathbf{w}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}}=\beta_{0}^{\#}\mathbf{a}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}}\mathbf{a}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}}^{*}+\alpha_{0}^{\#}\mathbf{b}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}}\mathbf{b}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}}^{*}. 
 
 
 We will require that  α 0 ​ β 0 # = β 0 ​ α 0 # = 𝓠 \alpha_{0}\beta_{0}^{\#}=\beta_{0}\alpha_{0}^{\#}=\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}} , and
that  α 0 ≫ β 0 \alpha_{0}\gg\beta_{0} . 
 
 
 Remark 4.1 . 
 
 The assumption that  α 0 ≫ β 0 \alpha_{0}\gg\beta_{0}  might look superfluous because of the rescaling
invariance of the problem.
Indeed, for the rescaling of a weight  W W , i.e. for the weight  A ​ W ​ A AWA , where
 A = A ∗ > 0 A=A^{*}>0  is a constant matrix, we have 
 

 
 
 [ A ​ W ​ A ] 𝐀 2 = [ W ] 𝐀 2 , \displaystyle[AWA]{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbf{A}_{2}}=[W]{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbf{A}_{2}}, 
 
 
 i.e. the rescaling preserves the  𝐀 2 \mathbf{A}_{2}  characteristics. 
 
 
 It is also easy to see that for operators of the form  T ⊗ 𝐈 T\otimes\mathbf{I} , where  T T  is
an operator acting on scalar-valued functions, its norms in  L 2 ​ ( W ) L^{2}(W)  and in  L 2 ​ ( A ​ W ​ A ) L^{2}(AWA)  coincide. 
 
 
 Thus, one can always rescale the weight and assume without loss of generality that
 α 0 = β 0 \alpha_{0}=\beta_{0} . 
 
 
 However, choosing a large
eccentricity simplifies formulas by allowing us to easily identify terms we can ignore, and that is
the reason we assume that the initial eccentricity
 E 0 := α 0 / β 0 E_{0}:=\alpha_{0}/\beta_{0}  is as large as we want. 
 
 
 
 The initial eccentricity  E 0 = α 0 / β 0 = α 0 # / β 0 # E_{0}=\alpha_{0}/\beta_{0}=\alpha_{0}^{\#}/\beta_{0}^{\#} , or equivalently
the quantity  δ 0 := q ( β 0 / α 0 ) 1 / 2 = q E 0 − 1 / 2 \delta_{0}:=q(\beta_{0}/\alpha_{0})^{1/2}=qE_{0}^{-1/2}  with  q = 0.1 q=0.1 , will be the parameter of the construction. We will choose  δ 0 \delta_{0}  as
small as we want, so  E 0 E_{0}  will be as large as we want. 
 
 
 4.2.1.  About initial eigenvalues 

 
 Now, what about the choice of the initial eigenvalues  α 0 \alpha_{0} ,  β 0 \beta_{0} ? Since in our
construction we will
not be taking the limit as  δ 0 → 0 \delta_{0}\to 0  (equivalently as  E 0 → ∞ E_{0}\to\infty ), but just take a
sufficiently small  δ 0 \delta_{0}  that gives us the desired bound, we do not need to worry much about
it. For each value of  δ 0 \delta_{0}  we just pick  α 0 \alpha_{0} ,  β 0 \beta_{0}  satisfying
 q ⋅ ( β 0 / α 0 ) 1 / 2 = δ 0 q\cdot(\beta_{0}/\alpha_{0})^{1/2}=\delta_{0} ; the eigenvalues  α 0 # \alpha_{0}^{\#} ,  β 0 # \beta_{0}^{\#}  are
uniquely determined by the relations  α 0 ​ β 0 # = β 0 ​ α 0 # = 𝓠 \alpha_{0}\beta_{0}^{\#}=\beta_{0}\alpha_{0}^{\#}=\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}} . 
 
 
 The reader uncomfortable with such freedom can always assume that the eigenvalue  α 0 \alpha_{0}  is
fixed.
Alternatively, given  δ 0 \delta_{0}  one can choose eigenvalues symmetrically, 
 

 
 
 α 0 = α 0 # = q ​ δ 0 − 1 ​ 𝓠 1 / 2 , β 0 = β 0 # = q − 1 ​ δ 0 ​ 𝓠 1 / 2 . \displaystyle\alpha_{0}=\alpha_{0}^{\#}=q\delta_{0}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}^{1/2},\qquad\beta_{0}=\beta_{0}^{\#}=q^{-1}\delta_{0}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}^{1/2}. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 4.3.  Rotations 

 
 On the next step of the construction we perform what we call the  rotation . 
 
 
 Suppose we are given a positive self-adjoint operator  𝐯 \mathbf{v}  in  ℝ 2 \mathbb{R}^{2} , 
 

 
 
 𝐯 = α ​ 𝐚𝐚 ∗ + β ​ 𝐛𝐛 ∗ , \displaystyle\mathbf{v}=\alpha\mathbf{a}\mathbf{a}^{*}+\beta\mathbf{b}\mathbf{b}^{*}, 
 
 
 where  𝐚 \mathbf{a} ,  𝐛 \mathbf{b}  is an orthonormal basis in  ℝ 2 \mathbb{R}^{2}  and  α , β > 0 \alpha,\beta>0 .
Recall that we agreed that  α ≫ β \alpha\gg\beta . 
 
 
 Let us find matrices  𝐯 + \mathbf{v}_{+}  and  𝐯 − \mathbf{v}_{-}  that have a similar form to  𝐯 \mathbf{v}  but in slightly
rotated bases, such that  𝐯 = ( 𝐯 + + 𝐯 − ) / 2 \mathbf{v}=(\mathbf{v}_{+}+\mathbf{v}_{-})/2 . 
 
 
 Namely, define 
 

 
 
 𝐯 ± = α ~ ​ 𝐚 ± ​ 𝐚 ± ∗ + β ~ ​ 𝐛 ± ​ 𝐛 ± ∗ , \displaystyle\mathbf{v}_{\pm}=\widetilde{\alpha}\mathbf{a}_{\pm}\mathbf{a}_{\pm}^{*}+\widetilde{\beta}\mathbf{b}_{\pm}\mathbf{b}_{\pm}^{*}, 
 
 
 where 
 

 
 (4.2) 
 
 𝐚 ± = ( 1 + δ 2 ) − 1 / 2 ( 𝐚 ± δ 𝐛 ) , 𝐛 ± = ( 1 + δ 2 ) − 1 / 2 ( 𝐛 ∓ δ 𝐚 ) , \displaystyle\mathbf{a}_{\pm}=(1+\delta^{2})^{-1/2}(\mathbf{a}\pm\delta\mathbf{b}),\qquad\mathbf{b}_{\pm}=(1+\delta^{2})^{-1/2}(\mathbf{b}\mp\delta\mathbf{a}), 
 
 
 with appropriately chosen small parameter  δ \delta . Note, that here we just rotated the basis
 𝐚 \mathbf{a} ,  𝐛 \mathbf{b}  through the angle
 ± θ \pm\theta ,  θ = arctan ⁡ δ \theta=\arctan\delta . 
 
 
 If  δ \delta  is fixed, the eigenvalues  α ~ \widetilde{\alpha}  and  β ~ \widetilde{\beta}  (note that they are the same for
 𝐯 + \mathbf{v}_{+}  and  𝐯 − \mathbf{v}_{-} ) are uniquely defined by the martingale relations  𝐯 = ( 𝐯 + + 𝐯 − ) / 2 \mathbf{v}=(\mathbf{v}_{+}+\mathbf{v}_{-})/2 .
Namely, noticing that 
 

 
 
 𝐚 + ​ 𝐚 + ∗ + 𝐚 − ​ 𝐚 − ∗ 2 \displaystyle\frac{\mathbf{a}_{+}\mathbf{a}_{+}^{*}+\mathbf{a}_{-}\mathbf{a}_{-}^{*}}{2} 
 = 𝐚𝐚 ∗ 1 + δ 2 + δ 2 ​ 𝐛𝐛 ∗ 1 + δ 2 , \displaystyle=\frac{\mathbf{a}\mathbf{a}^{*}}{1+\delta^{2}}+\frac{\delta^{2}\mathbf{b}\mathbf{b}^{*}}{1+\delta^{2}}, 
 
 and 
 
 
 𝐛 + ​ 𝐛 + ∗ + 𝐛 − ​ 𝐛 − ∗ 2 \displaystyle\frac{\mathbf{b}_{+}\mathbf{b}_{+}^{*}+\mathbf{b}_{-}\mathbf{b}_{-}^{*}}{2} 
 = δ 2 ​ 𝐚𝐚 ∗ 1 + δ 2 + 𝐛𝐛 ∗ 1 + δ 2 , \displaystyle=\frac{\delta^{2}\mathbf{a}\mathbf{a}^{*}}{1+\delta^{2}}+\frac{\mathbf{b}\mathbf{b}^{*}}{1+\delta^{2}}, 
 
 
 we conclude that 
 

 
 
 α = α ~ + δ 2 ​ β ~ 1 + δ 2 , β = δ 2 ​ α ~ + β ~ 1 + δ 2 . \displaystyle\alpha=\frac{\widetilde{\alpha}+\delta^{2}\widetilde{\beta}}{1+\delta^{2}},\qquad\beta=\frac{\delta^{2}\widetilde{\alpha}+\widetilde{\beta}}{1+\delta^{2}}. 
 
 
 Solving for  α ~ \widetilde{\alpha} ,  β ~ \widetilde{\beta}  we get 
 

 
 (4.3) 
 
 α ~ = α − δ 2 ​ β 1 − δ 2 , β ~ = β − δ 2 ​ α 1 − δ 2 . \displaystyle\widetilde{\alpha}=\frac{\alpha-\delta^{2}\beta}{1-\delta^{2}},\qquad\widetilde{\beta}=\frac{\beta-\delta^{2}\alpha}{1-\delta^{2}}. 
 
 
 Note that  β ~ > 0 \widetilde{\beta}>0  if and only if  δ 2 < β / α \delta^{2}<\beta/\alpha , and if we follow our assumption that
 α > β \alpha>\beta , then in this case  α ~ > β ~ \widetilde{\alpha}>\widetilde{\beta} , so both eigenvalues are positive. It is
also clear that in this case  α ~ > α \widetilde{\alpha}>\alpha . 
 
 
 In what follows we will set  δ = q ​ ( β / α ) 1 / 2 \delta=q(\beta/\alpha)^{1/2} , where  q q  is a
small parameter (say
 q = 0.1 q=0.1 ) to be
fixed later at the beginning of the construction, before all other parameters. 
 
 
 4.3.1.  Applying the rotations 

 
 In what follows we will apply the rotations every other generation, for example for some intervals
in the even generations. On each such  rotation  interval  I I  we will have 
 

 
 
 ⟨ W − 1 ⟩ I = 𝐯 I = α I ​ 𝐚 I ​ 𝐚 I ∗ + β I ​ 𝐛 I ​ 𝐛 I ∗ , ⟨ W ⟩ I = 𝐰 I = β I # ​ 𝐚 I ​ 𝐚 I ∗ + α I # ​ 𝐛 I ​ 𝐛 I ∗ \displaystyle\langle W^{-1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}=\mathbf{v}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}=\alpha{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\mathbf{a}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\mathbf{a}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{*}+\beta{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\mathbf{b}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\mathbf{b}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{*},\qquad\langle W\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}=\mathbf{w}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}=\beta{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{\#}\mathbf{a}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\mathbf{a}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{*}+\alpha{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{\#}\mathbf{b}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\mathbf{b}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{*}

constant
as intervals get smaller.

constant
is a nice bonus, making the construction possible. 
 
 
 Note that in our construction  α I > β I \alpha{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}>\beta{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}  so  s I > 1 − q 2 = 0.99 s{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}>1-q^{2}=0.99 .

 4.4.  Stretching 

 
 The next step of the construction is the so-called  stretching . It will be performed
individually in each eigenspace, so it is an operation on scalar objects (eigenvalues). 
 
 
 Fix a  stretching parameter   r = 2 − 1 / 𝓠 r=2-1/\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}} .
Consider a point  z = ( x , y ) ∈ ℝ 2 z=(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2} ,  x , y > 0 x,y>0 ,  x ​ y = s ​ 𝓠 xy=s\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}} , where  0.9 ≤ s ≤ 1 0.9\leq s\leq 1 . Then for  z + = ( x + , y + ) z_{+}=(x_{+},y_{+}) , 
 

 
 
 x + = r ​ x , y + = y / ( s ​ r ) , x_{+}=rx,\qquad y_{+}=y/(sr), 
 
 
 we have  x + ​ y + = 𝓠 x_{+}y_{+}=\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}} . 
 
 
 A point  z − = ( x − , y − ) z_{-}=(x_{-},y_{-})  such that  z = ( z + + z − ) / 2 z=(z_{+}+z_{-})/2  is given by 
 

 
 (4.6) 
 
 x − = ( 2 − r ) ​ x = x / 𝓠 , y − = ( 2 − 1 / ( s ​ r ) ) ​ y . \displaystyle x_{-}=(2-r)x=x/\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}},\qquad y_{-}=(2-1/(sr))y. 
 
 
 Then 
 

 
 
 x − ​ y − = ( 2 − 1 / ( s ​ r ) ) ​ x ​ y / 𝓠 = ( 2 − 1 / ( s ​ r ) ) ​ s . \displaystyle x_{-}y_{-}=(2-1/(sr))xy/\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}=(2-1/(sr))s. 
 
 
 As we are interested in asymptotics with large values of  𝓠 \boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}} , we can assume that  𝓠 > 2 \boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}>2 , so
recalling that
 s ≥ 0.9 s\geq 0.9  we can see that 
 

 
 
 1 ≤ x − ​ y − ≤ 2 . \displaystyle 1\leq x_{-}y_{-}\leq 2. 
 
 
 In particular  z − = ( x − , y − ) z_{-}=(x_{-},y_{-})  lies in the region  { ( x , y ) : x , y > 0 ,  1 ≤ x y ≤ 𝓠 } \left\{(x,y):x,y>0,\,1\leq xy\leq\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}\right\}  as required.
However, we keep in mind that  x − ​ y − ≪ x + ​ y + = 𝓠 x_{-}y_{-}\ll x_{+}y_{+}=\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}} , as well as  x − ​ y − ≪ x ​ y = s ​ 𝓠 x_{-}y_{-}\ll xy=s\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}  for
large values of  𝓠 \boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}} . We refer to Figure  2  below for a sketch of the stretching
step. 
 
 
 Figure 2.  Stretching  ( x , y ) ↦ ( x + , y + ) (x,y)\mapsto(x_{+},y_{+}) 
 
 
 
 4.5.  Complete construction 

 
 So, let us gather everything together. We start with the interval  I = I 0 = [ 0 , 1 ) I=I^{0}=[0,1) , which will give
us the initial generation
 𝒮 0 \mathscr{S}_{0}  of  stopping intervals .
As we are interested in asymptotics,  𝓠 \boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}  can be as large as we want.

be constructed.

 (4.11) 
 
 α n + 1 > r ​ α n , α n + 1 # > r ​ α n # . \displaystyle\alpha_{n+1}>r\alpha_{n},\qquad\alpha_{n+1}^{\#}>r\alpha_{n}^{\#}. 
 
 
 Since  α n + 1 ​ β n + 1 # = α n + 1 # ​ β n + 1 = 𝓠 = α n ​ β n # = α n # ​ β n \alpha_{n+1}\beta_{n+1}^{\#}=\alpha_{n+1}^{\#}\beta_{n+1}=\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}=\alpha_{n}\beta_{n}^{\#}=\alpha_{n}^{\#}\beta_{n} , we conclude that 
 

 
 (4.12) 
 
 α n + 1 / α n = β n # / β n + 1 # , α n + 1 # / α n # = β n / β n + 1 , \displaystyle{\alpha_{n+1}}/{\alpha_{n}}={\beta_{n}^{\#}}/{\beta_{n+1}^{\#}},\qquad{\alpha_{n+1}^{\#}}/{\alpha_{n}^{\#}}={\beta_{n}}/{\beta_{n+1}}, 
 
 
 so 
 

 
 (4.13) 
 
 β n + 1 < β n / r , β n + 1 # < β n # / r . \displaystyle\beta_{n+1}<\beta_{n}/r,\qquad\beta_{n+1}^{\#}<\beta_{n}^{\#}/r. 
 
 
 Recalling that  δ n = q ​ ( β n / α n ) 1 / 2 = q ​ ( β n # / α n # ) 1 / 2 \delta_{n}=q(\beta_{n}/\alpha_{n})^{1/2}=q(\beta_{n}^{\#}/\alpha_{n}^{\#})^{1/2}  we can
see that 
 

 
 (4.14) 
 
 δ n + 1 < δ n / r . \displaystyle\delta_{n+1}<\delta_{n}/r. 
 
 
 
 
 Remark 4.2 . 
 
 One can easily see that  α ~ n ​ ∼ δ 0 < ​ α n \widetilde{\alpha}_{n}\underset{\delta_{0}<}{\sim}\alpha_{n} ,  α ~ n # ​ ∼ δ 0 < ​ α n # \widetilde{\alpha}_{n}^{\#}\underset{\delta_{0}<}{\sim}\alpha_{n}^{\#} , so 
 

 
 
 α n + 1 ​ ∼ δ 0 < ​ r ​ α n , α n + 1 # ​ ∼ δ 0 < ​ r ​ α n # , \displaystyle\alpha_{n+1}\underset{\delta_{0}<}{\sim}r\alpha_{n},\qquad\alpha_{n+1}^{\#}\underset{\delta_{0}<}{\sim}r\alpha_{n}^{\#}, 
 
 
 so by ( 4.12 ) 
 

 
 
 β n + 1 ​ ∼ δ 0 < ​ β n / r , β n + 1 # ​ ∼ δ 0 < ​ β n # / r . \displaystyle\beta_{n+1}\underset{\delta_{0}<}{\sim}\beta_{n}/r,\qquad\beta_{n+1}^{\#}\underset{\delta_{0}<}{\sim}\beta_{n}^{\#}/r. 
 
 
 Therefore, we can conclude that 
 

 
 
 δ n + 1 ​ ∼ δ 0 < ​ δ n / r . \displaystyle\delta_{n+1}\underset{\delta_{0}<}{\sim}\delta_{n}/r. 
 
 
 
 
 
 Remark 4.3 . 
 
 One can see from the construction that 
 

 
 (4.15) 
 
 α n # = c α n , β n = c β n # , where c = α 0 # / α 0 . \displaystyle\alpha_{n}^{\#}=c\alpha_{n},\quad\beta_{n}=c\beta_{n}^{\#},\qquad\text{where}\quad c=\alpha_{0}^{\#}/\alpha_{0}. 
 
 
 Indeed, since  α 0 ​ β 0 # = α 0 # ​ β 0 = 𝓠 \alpha_{0}\beta_{0}^{\#}=\alpha_{0}^{\#}\beta_{0}=\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}} , this trivially holds for  n = 0 n=0 .
Assuming that ( 4.15 ) holds for  n n , we see from ( 4.8 ) that 
 

 
 
 α ~ n # = c ​ α ~ n , β ~ n = c ​ β ~ n # , \displaystyle\widetilde{\alpha}_{n}^{\#}=c\widetilde{\alpha}_{n},\quad\widetilde{\beta}_{n}=c\widetilde{\beta}_{n}^{\#}, 
 
 
 and ( 4.10 ) implies that ( 4.15 ) holds for  n + 1 n+1 . 
 
 
 We do not need this fact to prove the main result, but it is used later in Section
 6.2  to prove the lower bound for the Haar Shift  Ш \mathord{\mathchoice{\mbox{Ш}}{\mbox{Ш}}{\mbox{Ш}}{\mbox{Ш}}} . 
 
 
 
 The intervals  I + ⁣ + I_{++}  and  I − ⁣ + I_{-+} , with  I ∈ 𝒮 n I\in\mathscr{S}_{n}  will give us the next generation  𝒮 n + 1 \mathscr{S}_{n+1} 
of stopping intervals. Finally, let us denote  𝒮 = 𝒮 ⁡ ( I 0 ) := ⋃ n ≥ 0 𝒮 n \mathscr{S}=\mathscr{S}(I^{0}):=\bigcup_{n\geq 0}\mathscr{S}_{n} . Denote also
 𝒮 < = 𝒮 < ​ ( I 0 ) := 𝒮 ∖ { I 0 } = ⋃ n > 0 𝒮 n \mathscr{S}_{<}=\mathscr{S}_{<}(I^{0}):=\mathscr{S}\setminus\{I^{0}\}=\bigcup_{n>0}\mathscr{S}_{n} . 
 
 
 
 4.5.2.  Terminal intervals 

 
 We do not care what happens on the intervals  I + ⁣ − I_{+-}  and  I − ⁣ − I_{--} , with  I ∈ 𝒮 n I\in\mathscr{S}_{n} , we
just terminate our
martingale
there.
Namely, we know from the
construction of  stretching , see Section  4.4 , that for
 J = I ± J=I_{\pm}  we have for the averages 
 

 
 
 ⟨ W − 1 ⟩ J − − 1 ≤ ⟨ W ⟩ J − ≤ 2 ​ ⟨ W − 1 ⟩ J − − 1 . \displaystyle\langle W^{-1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\!J_{-}}^{-1}\leq\langle W\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\!J_{-}}\leq 2\langle W^{-1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\!J_{-}}^{-1}. 
 
 
 Thus, by Lemma  3.4  there exists an  𝐀 2 \mathbf{A}_{2}  weight  W W  on  J − J_{-} , which is constant on the
children of  J − J_{-} 
and has the prescribed averages, and we define
 W W  on  J − J_{-}  to be this weight. 
 
 
 We will call such intervals  I + ⁣ − I_{+-} ,  I − ⁣ − I_{--} ,  I ∈ 𝒮 I\in\mathscr{S}   terminal
intervals , and denote by  𝒯 \mathcal{T}  the collection of all terminal intervals. 
 
 
 Figure 3.  Dyadic tree: the picture shows two dyadic time steps: a rotation followed by stretching. 
 
 
 Thus we constructed martingales given by the averages  𝐯 I \mathbf{v}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}  and  𝐰 I \mathbf{w}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I} . While it is
possible (and not hard) to show
that the martingales are uniformly integrable, and so are the averages of (matrix-valued) functions
 V V  and  W W  and that  V = W − 1 V=W^{-1}  (on terminal intervals we already have  V = W − 1 V=W^{-1}  by the
construction), we chose an easier way and terminate the process, using again Lemma  3.4 
after finitely many steps. We will write all the sums as infinite ones, but will keep finitely many
terms that give us the desired lower bound. 
 
 
 
 
 
 5.  Lower bounds for paraproducts 

 
 In this section we will prove the lower bound  c ​ 𝓠 3 / 2 c\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}^{3/2}  for a special paraproduct, which will
be a crucial part in the proof of the main result.
In this section  𝐀 2 \mathbf{A}_{2}  always means the  dyadic   𝐀 2 \mathbf{A}_{2}  class  𝐀 2 dy \mathbf{A}_{2}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm dy}} , and we will
be using the notation  𝓠 := [ W ] 𝐀 1 dy \boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}:=[W]{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbf{A}_{1}}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm dy}} . 
 
 
 Recall that  𝒮 < = 𝒮 < ​ ( I 0 ) := ⋃ n > 0 𝒮 n \mathscr{S}_{<}=\mathscr{S}_{<}(I^{0}):=\bigcup_{n>0}\mathscr{S}_{n} , where  𝒮 n \mathscr{S}_{n}  are the generations of
stopping intervals
constructed above in Section  4 , and that  𝒮 = 𝒮 ⁡ ( I 0 ) := ⋃ n ≥ 0 𝒮 n \mathscr{S}=\mathscr{S}(I^{0}):=\bigcup_{n\geq 0}\mathscr{S}_{n}  is
the collection of all stopping intervals. 
 
 
 Define the paraproduct  Π \Pi  by 
 

 
 
 Π ​ f = ∑ I ∈ 𝒮 < ⟨ f ⟩ I ​ h ^ I = ∑ I ∈ 𝒮 < | I | 1 / 2 ​ ⟨ f ⟩ I ​ h I , \displaystyle\Pi f=\sum_{I\in\mathscr{S}_{<}}\langle f\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\widehat{h}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}=\sum_{I\in\mathscr{S}_{<}}|I|^{1/2}\langle f\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}h{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}, 
 
 
 where  h ^ I \widehat{h}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}  is the  L ∞ L^{\infty}  normalized Haar function,  h ^ I = 𝟏 I + − 𝟏 I − \widehat{h}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}=\mathbf{1}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I_{+}}-\mathbf{1}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I_{-}} , and  h I = | I | − 1 / 2 h ^ I h{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}=|I|^{-1/2}\widehat{h}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}  is the standard  L 2 L^{2} -normalized Haar
function. 
 
 
 Since  𝒮 < \mathscr{S}_{<}  is a sparse family of intervals ( 2 2 -Carleson, see the Definition  6.4  in Section  6.1.2  below), the
paraproduct is bounded in the unweighted
 L 2 L^{2}  space, or, equivalently its symbol  b := ∑ I ∈ 𝒮 h ^ I b:=\sum_{I\in\mathscr{S}}\widehat{h}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}  is in the  dyadic 
BMO
space. 
 
 
 We claim that for the weight  W W  constructed above we have for all sufficiently large  𝓠 \boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}  that
 ‖ Π ‖ L 2 ​ ( W ) ​ ≳ δ 0 < ​ ( [ W ] 𝐀 2 dy ) 3 / 2 \|\Pi\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(W)}\underset{\delta_{0}<}{\gtrsim}([W]{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbf{A}_{2}}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm dy}})^{3/2} . 
 
 
 More precisely, the following lemma holds 
 
 
 Lemma 5.1 . 
 
 For the weight  W W  constructed above and all sufficiently large  𝓠 \boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}} , we have for a function
 f = 𝟏 I 0 ​ W − 1 ​ 𝐞 f=\mathbf{1}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}}W^{-1}\mathbf{e} , where  𝐞 = 𝐚 I 0 \mathbf{e}=\mathbf{a}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}}  or  𝐞 = 𝐚 I 0 + 𝐛 I 0 \mathbf{e}=\mathbf{a}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}}+\mathbf{b}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}} 
 

 
 
 ‖ Π ​ f ‖ L 2 ​ ( W ) ​ ≳ δ 0 < ​ 𝓠 3 / 2 ​ ‖ f ‖ L 2 ​ ( W ) ≍ 𝓠 3 / 2 ​ α 0 1 / 2 . \displaystyle\|\Pi f\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(W)}\underset{\delta_{0}<}{\gtrsim}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}^{3/2}\|f\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(W)}\asymp\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}^{3/2}\alpha_{0}^{1/2}. 
 
 
 
 
 
 Remark 5.2 . 
 
 For  𝐞 = 𝐚 I 0 \mathbf{e}=\mathbf{a}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}}  we have  ‖ f ‖ L 2 ​ ( W ) = α 0 1 / 2 \|f\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(W)}=\alpha_{0}^{1/2} , and for  𝐞 = 𝐚 I 0 + 𝐛 I 0 \mathbf{e}=\mathbf{a}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}}+\mathbf{b}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}}  we only have the two sided estimates. 
 
 
 
 Remark 5.3 . 
 
 It looks more natural to consider the paraproduct where the sum is taken over the whole family
 𝒮 \mathscr{S} , but for what follows later in Section  6  it is more convenient to
consider the sum only over  𝒮 < \mathscr{S}_{<} .
However, since both paraproducts differ by one term of the norm at most  𝓠 1 / 2 \boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}^{1/2}  (with
constant  1 1 ), it really does not matter which paraproduct is used in Lemma  5.1 : the bound for one immediately implies the bound for the other. 
 
 
 
 5.1.  Preliminaries and main idea 

 
 For an interval  I ∈ 𝒮 I\in\mathscr{S}  let us denote  𝒮 ⁡ ( I ) := { J ∈ 𝒮 : J ⊂ I } \mathscr{S}(I):=\{J\in\mathscr{S}:J\subset I\} ,
 𝒮 < ​ ( I ) := 𝒮 ⁡ ( I ) ∖ { I } \mathscr{S}_{<}(I):=\mathscr{S}(I)\setminus\{I\} . Recall also that  𝒮 < := 𝒮 < ​ ( I 0 ) \mathscr{S}_{<}:=\mathscr{S}_{<}(I^{0}) . 
 
 
 We can write 
 

 
 
 ‖ Π ⁡ ( W − 1 ​ 𝟏 I 0 ​ 𝐞 ) ‖ L 2 ​ ( W ) 2 \displaystyle\|\Pi(W^{-1}\mathbf{1}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}}\mathbf{e})\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(W)}^{2}

 + 2 ∑ I ∈ 𝒮 < ∑ J ∈ 𝒮 < ​ ( I ) ∫ J ( ⟨ W − 1 ⟩ I 𝐞 , W ( s ) h ^ I ( s ) h ^ J ( s ) ⟨ W − 1 ⟩ J 𝐞 ) ℝ 2 d s . \displaystyle\quad+2\sum_{I\in\mathscr{S}_{<}}\sum_{J\in\mathscr{S}_{<}(I)}\int_{J}\left(\langle W^{-1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\mathbf{e},W(s)\widehat{h}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}(s)\widehat{h}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\!J}(s)\langle W^{-1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\!J}\mathbf{e}\right){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}{\mathrm{d}}s. 
 
 
 It is known that the first sum is always estimated above by  C ​ 𝓠 C\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}} ,  𝓠 := [ W ] 𝐀 2 dy \boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}:=[W]{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbf{A}_{2}}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm dy}} .
It will not be used here, but it explains why we do not bother with the first sum, and only will be
estimating the second one below.
Formally, since the first sum is trivially non-negative, a lower bound for the second sum (the sum,
not just its absolute value) gives us the same lower bound for  ‖ Π ⁡ ( W − 1 ​ 𝟏 I 0 ​ 𝐞 ) ‖ L 2 ​ ( W ) 2 \|\Pi(W^{-1}\mathbf{1}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}}\mathbf{e})\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(W)}^{2} . 
 
 
 Denote by  𝒮 + ​ ( I ) \mathscr{S}_{+}(I)  (respectively  𝒮 − ​ ( I ) \mathscr{S}_{-}(I) ) the intervals  J ∈ 𝒮 ⁡ ( I ) J\in\mathscr{S}(I)  such that  J ⊂ I + J\subset I_{+} 
(respectively  J ⊂ I − J\subset I_{-} ). Since for  J ∈ 𝒮 ± ​ ( I ) J\in\mathscr{S}_{\pm}(I) 
 

 
 
 | J | − 1 ​ ∫ J W ​ h ^ I ​ h ^ J ​ 𝑑 s = ± ⟨ W ​ h ^ J ⟩ J , \displaystyle|J|^{-1}\int_{J}W\widehat{h}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\widehat{h}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\!J}{\mathrm{d}}s=\pm\langle W\widehat{h}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\!J}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\!J}, 
 
 
 we can rewrite the second sum as 
 

 
 
 ∑ I ∈ 𝒮 < \displaystyle\sum_{I\in\mathscr{S}_{<}} 
 ( ∑ J ∈ 𝒮 + ​ ( I ) ( ⟨ W − 1 ⟩ I ​ 𝐞 , ⟨ W ​ h ^ J ⟩ J ​ ⟨ W − 1 ⟩ J ​ 𝐞 ) ℝ 2 ​ | J | CLOSE \displaystyle\left(\sum_{J\in\mathscr{S}_{+}(I)}\left(\langle W^{-1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\mathbf{e},\langle W\widehat{h}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\!J}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\!J}\langle W^{-1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\!J}\mathbf{e}\right){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|J|\right. 
 
 
 (5.1) 
 
 
 − ∑ J ∈ 𝒮 − ​ ( I ) ( ⟨ W − 1 ⟩ I 𝐞 , ⟨ W h ^ J ⟩ J ⟨ W − 1 ⟩ J 𝐞 ) ℝ 2 | J | ) . \displaystyle\qquad\qquad\left.-\sum_{J\in\mathscr{S}_{-}(I)}\left(\langle W^{-1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\mathbf{e},\langle W\widehat{h}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\!J}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\!J}\langle W^{-1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\!J}\mathbf{e}\right){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|J|\right). 
 
 
 
 
 In the scalar case each term in ( 5.1 ) is estimated above by  ⟨ w − 1 ⟩ I ​ 𝓠 ​ | J | \langle w^{-1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}|J| . For  I ∈ 𝒮 n I\in\mathscr{S}_{n}  summing over
 J ∈ 𝒮 k ​ ( I ) J\in\mathscr{S}_{k}(I) ,  k > n k>n  one gets the bound 
 

 
 (5.2) 
 
 ∑ J ∈ 𝒮 k ​ ( I ) ⟨ w − 1 ⟩ I ​ ⟨ w ⟩ J ​ ⟨ w − 1 ⟩ J ​ | J | ≤ 2 − n − k ​ 𝓠 ​ ⟨ w − 1 ⟩ I = 2 n − k ​ 𝓠 ​ ⟨ w − 1 ⟩ I ​ | I | \displaystyle\sum_{J\in\mathscr{S}_{k}(I)}\langle w^{-1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\langle w\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\!J}\langle w^{-1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\!J}|J|\leq 2^{-n-k}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}\langle w^{-1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}=2^{n-k}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}\langle w^{-1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}|I| 
 
 
 (there are  2 k − n 2^{k-n}  intervals  J ∈ 𝒮 k ​ ( I ) J\in\mathscr{S}_{k}(I)  and  | J | = 2 − 2 ​ k |J|=2^{-2k} ).
The summation over  k > n k>n  then gives us 
 

 
 (5.3) 
 
 ∑ J ∈ 𝒮 < ​ ( I ) ⟨ w − 1 ⟩ I ​ ⟨ w ⟩ J ​ ⟨ w − 1 ⟩ J ​ | J | ≤ ∑ k > n 2 n − k ​ 𝓠 ​ ⟨ w − 1 ⟩ I ​ | I | ≤ 𝓠 ​ ⟨ w − 1 ⟩ I ​ | I | . \displaystyle\sum_{J\in\mathscr{S}_{<}(I)}\langle w^{-1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\langle w\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\!J}\langle w^{-1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\!J}|J|\leq\sum_{k>n}2^{n-k}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}\langle w^{-1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}|I|\leq\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}\langle w^{-1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}|I|. 
 
 
 It is well known that for a sparse sequence  𝒮 = 𝒮 ⁡ ( I 0 ) \mathscr{S}=\mathscr{S}(I^{0})  and an  A 2 A_{2}  weight  w w  with  A 2 A_{2} 
characteristic  𝓠 \boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}} 
 

 
 
 ∑ I ∈ 𝒮 ⁡ ( I 0 ) ⟨ w − 1 ⟩ I ​ | I | ≤ C ​ 𝓠 ​ ⟨ w − 1 ⟩ I 0 ​ | I 0 | , \displaystyle\sum_{I\in\mathscr{S}(I^{0})}\langle w^{-1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}|I|\leq C\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}\langle w^{-1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}}|I^{0}|, 
 
 
 where the constant depends only on the sparseness characteristic of  𝒮 \mathscr{S} . Thus, in the scalar case
the sum ( 5.1 ) is estimated above by  C ​ 𝓠 2 ​ ⟨ w − 1 ⟩ I 0 ​ | I 0 | C\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}^{2}\langle w^{-1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}}|I^{0}| , which gives, as
one would expect, the linear in  𝓠 \boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}  upper bound for the norm. 
 
 
 In the matrix-valued case the  𝐀 2 \mathbf{A}_{2}  condition does not imply any bounds on the norm of  ⟨ W − 1 ⟩ J ​ ⟨ W ⟩ J \langle W^{-1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\!J}\langle W\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\!J} . In our case we are able to estimate below  most  of the
terms
in ( 5.1 ),  I ∈ 𝒮 n I\in\mathscr{S}_{n} ,  J ∈ 𝒮 k ​ ( I ) J\in\mathscr{S}_{k}(I)  (with sign) by something like 
 

 
 
 c ​ 𝓠 ​ r k − n ​ ( ⟨ W − 1 ⟩ I ​ 𝐞 , 𝐞 ) ℝ 2 ​ | J | , \displaystyle c\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}r^{k-n}\left(\langle W^{-1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\mathbf{e},\mathbf{e}\right){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|J|, 
 
 
 i.e. to gain the extra factor  r k − n r^{k-n}  (recall that  r = 2 − 1 / 𝓠 r=2-1/\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}} ). Thus, instead of summing the
geometric series  ∑ 1 ∞ 2 − k = 1 \sum_{1}^{\infty}2^{-k}=1  as in ( 5.3 ), we end up summing a bigger
geometric series 
 

 
 
 ∑ k ≥ 1 ( r / 2 ) k = 2 ​ 𝓠 − 1 , \displaystyle\sum_{k\geq 1}(r/2)^{k}=2\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}-1, 
 
 
 which give us an extra factor  𝓠 \boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}  in the sum. Since the sum gives us the square of the norm,
this translates to the extra factor  𝓠 1 / 2 \boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}^{1/2}  in the lower bound for the norm. Of course, there
are a lot of technical details, that need to be carefully followed. 
 
 
 
 5.2.  Preliminary estimates 

 
 Let  I ∈ 𝒮 n I\in\mathscr{S}_{n} ,  J ∈ 𝒮 ⁡ ( I ) J\in\mathscr{S}(I) ,  J ∈ 𝒮 k J\in\mathscr{S}_{k} . Then it is
easy to see that in the basis
 𝐚 J \mathbf{a}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\!J} ,
 𝐛 J \mathbf{b}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\!J}  the

Writing this inner product we get
