 We will need the following simple lemma. 
 
 
 Lemma 5.4 . 
 
 Let  J ∈ 𝒮 ⁡ ( I ) J\in\mathscr{S}(I) ,  I ∈ 𝒮 n I\in\mathscr{S}_{n} ,  J ∈ 𝒮 k ​ ( I ) J\in\mathscr{S}_{k}(I) , and let  𝓠 ≥ 4 \boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}\geq 4 . Then the angle
 θ I , J \theta{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I,J} 
between  𝐚 I \mathbf{a}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}  and
 𝐚 J \mathbf{a}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\!J}  can be estimated as 
 

 
 
 | θ I , J | ≤ 3 ​ θ n , θ n = arctan ⁡ δ n . \displaystyle|\theta{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I,J}|\leq 3\,\theta_{n},\qquad\theta_{n}=\arctan\delta_{n}. 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 The angle is trivially estimated by 
 

 
 
 ∑ j = n ∞ θ j ≤ ∑ j = n ∞ δ j ≤ δ n 1 − 1 / r = δ n ​ 2 ​ 𝓠 − 1 𝓠 − 1 ≤ ( 7 / 3 ) ​ δ n . \displaystyle\sum_{j=n}^{\infty}\theta_{j}\leq\sum_{j=n}^{\infty}\delta_{j}\leq\frac{\delta_{n}}{1-1/r}=\delta_{n}\frac{2\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}-1}{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}-1}\leq(7/3)\delta_{n}. 
 
 
 By the construction  δ n ≤ q = 0.1 \delta_{n}\leq q=0.1 , so using concavity of  arctan \arctan  on  ℝ + \mathbb{R}_{+}  we get 
 

 
 
 θ n = arctan ⁡ δ n = δ n ​ ( arctan ⁡ δ n ) / δ n ≥ δ n ​ ( arctan ⁡ 0.1 ) / 0.1 ≥ 0.99 ​ δ n . \displaystyle\theta_{n}=\arctan\delta_{n}=\delta_{n}(\arctan\delta_{n})/\delta_{n}\geq\delta_{n}(\arctan 0.1)/0.1\geq 0.99\delta_{n}. 
 
 
 Thus the angle is bounded by  ( 7 / 2 ) ​ δ n / 0.99 ≤ 3 ​ θ n (7/2)\delta_{n}/0.99\leq 3\theta_{n} ,
which completes the estimate.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Assume that  J ∈ 𝒮 ⁡ ( I ) J\in\mathscr{S}(I) ,  I ∈ 𝒮 n I\in\mathscr{S}_{n} ,  J ∈ 𝒮 k J\in\mathscr{S}_{k} , and that  𝓠 ≥ 10 \boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}\geq 10 . We get from Lemma
 5.4  that 
 

 
 
 ( 𝐚 J , 𝐞 ) ℝ 2 = ( 𝐚 I , 𝐞 ) ℝ 2 + O δ 0 < ​ ( δ n ) . \displaystyle(\mathbf{a}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\!J},\mathbf{e}){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}=(\mathbf{a}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I},\mathbf{e}){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}+O{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\delta_{0}<}(\delta_{n}). 
 
 
 Since  𝐛 J ⟂ 𝐚 J \mathbf{b}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\!J}\perp\mathbf{a}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\!J} , Lemma  5.4  implies that  | ( 𝐚 I , 𝐛 J ) ℝ 2 | ≤ 3 ​ θ n ≤ 3 ​ δ n |(\mathbf{a}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I},\mathbf{b}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\!J}){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|\leq 3\theta_{n}\leq 3\delta_{n} , so 
 

 
 
 σ I ​ t J ​ ( 𝐚 I , 𝐛 J ) ℝ 2 \displaystyle\sigma{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}t{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\!J}(\mathbf{a}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I},\mathbf{b}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\!J}){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbb{R}^{2}} 
 = α n ​ t k ​ [ ( 𝐚 I , 𝐞 ) ℝ 2 2 + O δ 0 < ​ ( δ n ) ] ​ ( 𝐚 I , 𝐛 J ) ℝ 2 \displaystyle=\alpha_{n}t_{k}\left[(\mathbf{a}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I},\mathbf{e}){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}^{2}+O{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\delta_{0}<}(\delta_{n})\right](\mathbf{a}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I},\mathbf{b}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\!J}){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbb{R}^{2}} 
 
 
 
 
 = α n ​ t k ​ [ ( 𝐚 I , 𝐞 ) ℝ 2 2 ​ ( 𝐚 I , 𝐛 J ) ℝ 2 + O δ 0 < ​ ( δ n 2 ) ] . \displaystyle=\alpha_{n}t_{k}\left[(\mathbf{a}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I},\mathbf{e}){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}^{2}(\mathbf{a}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I},\mathbf{b}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\!J}){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}+O{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\delta_{0}<}(\delta_{n}^{2})\right]. 
 
 
 
 
 Lemma 5.5 . 
 
 Let  J ∈ 𝒮 ⁡ ( I ) J\in\mathscr{S}(I) ,  I ∈ 𝒮 n I\in\mathscr{S}_{n} ,  J ∈ 𝒮 k J\in\mathscr{S}_{k} , and let  𝓠 ≥ 10 \boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}\geq 10 . 
 

 
 (5.9) 
 
 ( ⟨ W − 1 ⟩ I ​ 𝐞 , ⟨ W ​ h ^ J ⟩ J ​ ⟨ W − 1 ⟩ J ​ 𝐞 ) ℝ 2 = − α n ​ t k ​ [ ( 𝐚 I , 𝐞 ) ℝ 2 2 ​ ( 𝐚 I , 𝐛 J ) ℝ 2 + O δ 0 < ​ ( δ n 2 ) ] \displaystyle\left(\langle W^{-1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\mathbf{e},\langle W\widehat{h}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\!J}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\!J}\langle W^{-1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\!J}\mathbf{e}\right){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}=-\alpha_{n}t_{k}\left[(\mathbf{a}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I},\mathbf{e}){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}^{2}(\mathbf{a}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I},\mathbf{b}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\!J}){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}+O{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\delta_{0}<}(\delta_{n}^{2})\right] 
 
 
 ( with implied constants not depending on  n n ,  k k ,  I I ,  J J ,  𝓠 \boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}} ) . 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 We have already shown above that  σ I ​ t J ​ ( 𝐚 I , 𝐛 J ) ℝ 2 \sigma{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}t{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\!J}(\mathbf{a}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I},\mathbf{b}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\!J}){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}  gives
us
the desired form (the minus sign here is because of the minus in front of  t J t{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\!J}  in ( 5.6 )). As we claimed before,
that is
the main term, and we just need to show that the sum of the
other three inner products are estimated by  C ​ α n ​ t k ​ δ n 2 C\alpha_{n}t_{k}\delta_{n}^{2} . 
 
 
 We have 
 

 
 
 | σ I ​ s J ​ ( 𝐚 I , 𝐚 J ) ℝ 2 | ≤ 𝓠 ​ α n ​ δ k ​ ≍ δ 0 < ​ α n ​ t k ​ δ k 2 ≤ α n ​ t k ​ δ n 2 ; |\sigma{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}s{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\!J}(\mathbf{a}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I},\mathbf{a}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\!J}){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|\leq\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}\alpha_{n}\delta_{k}\underset{\delta_{0}<}{\asymp}\alpha_{n}t_{k}\delta_{k}^{2}\leq\alpha_{n}t_{k}\delta_{n}^{2}; 
 
 
 here in the last inequality we used the fact that  δ k \delta_{k}  is decreasing, and the equivalence
follows from the fact that 
 

 
 
 t k ​ δ k 2 ​ ∼ δ 0 < ​ δ k ​ α k # ​ α k ​ δ k 2 = δ k ​ α k ​ α k # ​ q 2 ​ β k / α k = q 2 ​ δ k ​ 𝓠 ​ ≍ δ 0 < ​ δ k ​ 𝓠 . t_{k}\delta_{k}^{2}\underset{\delta_{0}<}{\sim}\delta_{k}\alpha_{k}^{\#}\alpha_{k}\delta_{k}^{2}=\delta_{k}\alpha_{k}\alpha_{k}^{\#}q^{2}\beta_{k}/\alpha_{k}=q^{2}\delta_{k}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}\underset{\delta_{0}<}{\asymp}\delta_{k}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}. 
 
 
 
 
 On the other hand, trivially 
 

 
 
 | τ I ​ t J ​ ( 𝐛 I , 𝐛 J ) ℝ 2 | ≤ β n ​ t k = q − 2 ​ α n ​ δ n 2 ​ t k . |\tau{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}t{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\!J}(\mathbf{b}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I},\mathbf{b}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\!J}){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|\leq\beta_{n}t_{k}=q^{-2}\alpha_{n}\delta_{n}^{2}t_{k}. 
 
 
 Finally  | s J | ​ ≲ δ 0 < ​ t k |s{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\!J}|\underset{\delta_{0}<}{\lesssim}t_{k}  (because  β k < α k \beta_{k}<\alpha_{k} ), so we
can
estimate 
 

 
 
 | τ I ​ s J ​ ( 𝐛 I , 𝐚 J ) ℝ 2 | ​ ≲ δ 0 < ​ β n ​ | s J | ≤ β n ​ t k = α n ​ δ n 2 ​ t k . \left|\tau{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}s{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\!J}(\mathbf{b}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I},\mathbf{a}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\!J}){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\right|\underset{\delta_{0}<}{\lesssim}\beta_{n}|s{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\!J}|\leq\beta_{n}t_{k}=\alpha_{n}\delta_{n}^{2}t_{k}. 
 
 
 Summing everything up, we get the desired estimate.
∎ 
 
 
 
 We claim that the term  α n ​ t k ​ ( 𝐚 I , 𝐞 ) ℝ 2 2 ​ ( 𝐚 I , 𝐛 J ) ℝ 2 \alpha_{n}t_{k}(\mathbf{a}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I},\mathbf{e}){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}^{2}(\mathbf{a}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I},\mathbf{b}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\!J}){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}  is the main part of the term 
 

 
 
 ( ⟨ W − 1 ⟩ I ​ 𝐞 , ⟨ W ​ h ^ J ⟩ J ​ ⟨ W − 1 ⟩ J ) ℝ 2 , \displaystyle\left(\langle W^{-1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\mathbf{e},\langle W\widehat{h}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\!J}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\!J}\langle W^{-1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\!J}\right){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}, 
 
 
 meaning that after summation (with
weights  | J | |J| )
it gives us the lower bound  ≳ δ 0 < ​ 𝓠 3 \underset{\delta_{0}<}{\gtrsim}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}^{3} , while the rest gives a smaller
contribution  ≲ δ 0 < ​ 𝓠 2 \underset{\delta_{0}<}{\lesssim}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}^{2} . 
 
 
 
 5.3.  Final estimates 

 
 Let us estimate the inner sum in ( 5.1 ).

 Remark 5.6 .

there holds

 If all inner products  ( 𝐚 I , 𝐛 J ) ℝ 2 (\mathbf{a}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I},\mathbf{b}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\!J}){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}  had the same sign ( ≤ 0 \leq 0 ) for all  J ∈ 𝒮 + k ​ ( I ) J\in\mathscr{S}_{+}^{k}(I) ,
one could use just this half of the intervals to get the estimate ( 5.12 ) (with a smaller
constant, which does not really matter). 
 
 
 Unfortunately, for  J ∈ 𝒮 + k J\in\mathscr{S}_{+}^{k}  with sufficiently large  k k , not all inner products  ( 𝐚 I , 𝐛 J ) ℝ 2 (\mathbf{a}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I},\mathbf{b}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\!J}){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}  have the same sign. Namely, see ( 4.14 ) we can only
guarantee that
 θ j + 1 ≤ θ j / r \theta_{j+1}\leq\theta_{j}/r  and we know that  1 / r > 1 / 2 1/r>1/2 . Moreover we know, see Remark

such
that for all sufficiently small  δ 0 \delta_{0}

 ∑ j > n θ j > θ n . \displaystyle\sum_{j>n}\theta_{j}>\theta_{n}. 
 
 
 Recall that a
vector  𝐛 J \mathbf{b}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\!J}  is obtained from  𝐛 I \mathbf{b}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}  by rotating it trough angle  θ n \theta_{n} , and then
through angles  ± θ j \pm\theta_{j} ,  n < j < k n<j<k .
So, by picking sufficiently large  k k  and all the rotations except the first one to be through
 − θ j -\theta_{j} ,  j > n j>n , we get that for all sufficiently small  δ 0 \delta_{0}  the corresponding vector
 𝐛 J \mathbf{b}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\!J}  is obtained from  𝐛 I \mathbf{b}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}  by a  negative  rotation, so  − ( 𝐚 I , 𝐛 J ) ℝ 2 < 0 -(\mathbf{a}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I},\mathbf{b}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\!J}){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}<0 . 
 
 
 Thus, a trivial estimate does not work, and we need a bit more involved reasoning; one possible

was presented above.

 6.  Lower bounds for Haar Shifts 

 
 In this section we prove the lower bound for the special Haar Shift  ℋ dy \mathcal{H}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm dy}} , which will be later
used to get the lower bound for the Hilbert Transform, i.e. to prove the main theorem (Theorem
 1.1 ). 
 
 
 This special operator  ℋ dy \mathcal{H}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm dy}}  is defined as a linear combination of classical Haar Shifts, so let us
recall some definitions.
The classical Haar Shift  Ш \mathord{\mathchoice{\mbox{Ш}}{\mbox{Ш}}{\mbox{Ш}}{\mbox{Ш}}} 
is defined by 
 

 
 
 Ш ​ f = ∑ I ∈ 𝒟 ( f , h I ) L 2 ​ [ h I + − h I − ] . \displaystyle\mathord{\mathchoice{\mbox{Ш}}{\mbox{Ш}}{\mbox{Ш}}{\mbox{Ш}}}f=\sum_{I\in\mathscr{D}}(f,h{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}}\left[h{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I_{+}}-h{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I_{-}}\right]. 
 
 
 The importance of the Haar Shift comes from the fact that its average over dyadic grids yields a
non-zero multiple of the Hilbert transform. 
 
 
 In our construction we will use the  odd  Haar Shift  𝕊 \mathbb{S} , where the summation is taken over
the set
 𝒟 odd = ⋃ k 𝒟 2 ​ k + 1 \mathscr{D}_{\scriptstyle\text{\rm odd}}=\bigcup_{k}\mathscr{D}_{2k+1} : 
 

 
 
 𝕊 ​ f = ∑ I ∈ 𝒟 odd ( f , h I ) L 2 ​ [ h I + − h I − ] . \displaystyle\mathbb{S}f=\sum_{I\in\mathscr{D}_{\scriptstyle\text{\rm odd}}}(f,h{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}}\left[h{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I_{+}}-h{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I_{-}}\right]. 
 
 
 
 
 We will also need the  odd  modification  𝕊 0 \mathbb{S}_{0}  of the operator  Ш 0 \mathord{\mathchoice{\mbox{Ш}}{\mbox{Ш}}{\mbox{Ш}}{\mbox{Ш}}}_{0}  introduced in
 [ 10 ] 
 

 
 
 Ш 0 ​ f \displaystyle\mathord{\mathchoice{\mbox{Ш}}{\mbox{Ш}}{\mbox{Ш}}{\mbox{Ш}}}_{0}f 
 = ∑ I ∈ 𝒟 [ ( f , h I + ) L 2 ​ h I − − ( f , h I − ) L 2 ​ h I + ] , \displaystyle=\sum_{I\in\mathscr{D}}\left[(f,h{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I_{+}}){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}}h{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I_{-}}-(f,h{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I_{-}}){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}}h{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I_{+}}\right], 
 
 
 
 𝕊 0 ​ f \displaystyle\mathbb{S}_{0}f 
 = ∑ I ∈ 𝒟 odd [ ( f , h I + ) L 2 ​ h I − − ( f , h I − ) L 2 ​ h I + ] . \displaystyle=\sum_{I\in\mathscr{D}_{\scriptstyle\text{\rm odd}}}\left[(f,h{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I_{+}}){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}}h{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I_{-}}-(f,h{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I_{-}}){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}}h{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I_{+}}\right]. 
 
 
 
 
 On vector-valued functions these operators are defined by the same formulas, if we, sightly abusing
notation, treat  ( f , h I ) L 2 (f,h{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}}  as the vector 
 

 
 (6.1) 
 
 ( f , h I ) L 2 := | I | 1 / 2 ​ ⟨ f ​ h I ⟩ I . \displaystyle(f,h{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}}:=|I|^{1/2}\langle fh{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}. 
 
 
 
 
 After the remodeling described below in Section  7 , the lower bound for the Hilbert
transform (for the remodeled weight) will be obtained
from the lower bound for the dyadic operator 
 

 
 
 ℋ dy = c 1 ​ ( 𝕊 − 𝕊 ∗ ) + c 2 ​ 𝕊 0 ; c 1 ≠ 0 . \mathcal{H}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm dy}}=c_{1}(\mathbb{S}-\mathbb{S}^{*})+c_{2}\mathbb{S}_{0};\qquad c_{1}\neq 0. 
 
 
 with  c 1 c_{1} ,  c 2 c_{2}  given by the identities ( 7.9 ) below. 
 
 
 We will use the lemma below with particular  c 1 c_{1} ,  c 2 c_{2}  given by ( 7.9 ), but it
remains true for any  c 1 ≠ 0 c_{1}\neq 0 ,  c 2 c_{2}  (with implied constants depending on  c 1 c_{1} ,  c 2 c_{2} ). 
 
 
 Lemma 6.1 . 
 
 For the matrix weight  W W  we constructed in Section  4  and  f = W − 1 ​ 𝟏 I 0 ​ 𝐞 f=W^{-1}\mathbf{1}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}}\mathbf{e}  there holds 
 

 
 
 ‖ ℋ dy ​ f ‖ L 2 ​ ( W ) ​ ≳ δ 0 < ​ 𝓠 3 / 2 ​ ‖ f ‖ L 2 ​ ( W ) . \|\mathcal{H}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm dy}}f\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(W)}\underset{\delta_{0}<}{\gtrsim}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}^{3/2}\|f\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(W)}. 
 
 
 
 
 
 6.1.  Proving Lemma  6.1 

 
 In this section we always denote  f := 𝟏 I 0 ​ W − 1 ​ 𝐞 f:=\mathbf{1}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}}W^{-1}\mathbf{e} . 
 
 
 6.1.1.  Introducing sparse counterparts 

 
 We first notice that for each stopping interval  I ∈ 𝒮 I\in\mathscr{S}  its
left sibling  L ⁡ ( I ) L(I)  is a terminal interval, so the weight  W W  is constant on children of  L ⁡ ( I ) L(I) ,
and so is the function  f f . Therefore, in the representation  ℋ dy ​ f \mathcal{H}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm dy}}f  a lot of terms are  0 0 , so we
can replace  ℋ dy \mathcal{H}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm dy}}  in  ℋ dy ​ f \mathcal{H}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm dy}}f  by its sparse counterpart  ℋ 𝒮 dy \mathcal{H}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm dy}}_{\mathscr{S}} . 
 
 
 Namely, for  I ∈ 𝒮 < I\in\mathscr{S}_{<}  let  I ^ \widehat{I}  be its dyadic parent. Then, defining sparse counterparts
 𝕊 𝒮 \mathbb{S}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathscr{S}} ,  𝕊 𝒮 0 \mathbb{S}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathscr{S}}^{0}  and  ℋ 𝒮 dy \mathcal{H}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm dy}}_{\mathscr{S}}  of  𝕊 \mathbb{S} ,  𝕊 0 \mathbb{S}_{0}  and  ℋ dy \mathcal{H}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm dy}}  respectively by 
 

 
 
 𝕊 𝒮 ​ f \displaystyle\mathbb{S}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathscr{S}}f 
 : = ∑ I ∈ 𝒮 < ( f , h I ^ ) L 2 ​ [ h I ^ + − h I ^ − ] , \displaystyle:=\sum_{I\in\mathscr{S}_{<}}(f,h{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\widehat{I}}){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}}\left[h{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\widehat{I}_{+}}-h{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\widehat{I}_{-}}\right], 
 
 
 
 𝕊 𝒮 0 ​ f \displaystyle\mathbb{S}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathscr{S}}^{0}f 
 : = ∑ I ∈ 𝒮 < [ ( f , h I ^ + ) L 2 ​ h I ^ − − ( f , h I ^ − ) L 2 ​ h I ^ + ] , \displaystyle:=\sum_{I\in\mathscr{S}_{<}}\left[(f,h{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\widehat{I}_{+}}){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}}h{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\widehat{I}_{-}}-(f,h{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\widehat{I}_{-}}){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}}h{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\widehat{I}_{+}}\right], 
 
 
 
 ℋ 𝒮 dy \displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm dy}}_{\mathscr{S}} 
 : = c 1 ​ ( 𝕊 𝒮 − 𝕊 𝒮 ∗ ) + c 2 ​ 𝕊 𝒮 0 , \displaystyle:=c_{1}(\mathbb{S}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathscr{S}}-\mathbb{S}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathscr{S}}^{*})+c_{2}\mathbb{S}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathscr{S}}^{0}, 
 
 
 we can see that for  f = W − 1 ​ 𝟏 I 0 ​ 𝐞 f=W^{-1}\mathbf{1}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}}\mathbf{e}  we have 
 

 
 
 ℋ dy ​ f \displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm dy}}f 
 = ℋ 𝒮 dy ​ f . \displaystyle=\mathcal{H}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm dy}}_{\mathscr{S}}f. 
 
 
 
 
 The operator  𝕊 𝒮 \mathbb{S}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathscr{S}}  can be represented as a linear combination of paraproducts 
 

 
 
 𝕊 𝒮 = 2 − 1 / 2 ( Π + Π 1 − Π 2 − Π 3 ) \displaystyle\mathbb{S}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathscr{S}}=2^{-1/2}\left(\Pi+\Pi_{1}-\Pi_{2}-\Pi_{3}\right) 
 
 
 where  Π \Pi  is the paraproduct introduced before in Section  5 , and 
 

 
 
 Π 1 ​ g = ∑ I ∈ 𝒮 < ⟨ g ⟩ L ⁡ ( I ) ​ h ^ L ⁡ ( I ) , Π 2 ​ g = ∑ I ∈ 𝒮 < ⟨ g ⟩ L ⁡ ( I ) ​ h ^ I , Π 3 ​ g = ∑ I ∈ 𝒮 < ⟨ g ⟩ I ​ h ^ L ⁡ ( I ) , \displaystyle\Pi_{1}g=\sum_{I\in\mathscr{S}_{<}}\langle g\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L(I)}\widehat{h}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L(I)},\qquad\Pi_{2}g=\sum_{I\in\mathscr{S}_{<}}\langle g\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L(I)}\widehat{h}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I},\qquad\Pi_{3}g=\sum_{I\in\mathscr{S}_{<}}\langle g\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\widehat{h}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L(I)}, 
 
 
 where, recall,  L ⁡ ( I ) L(I)  is the left sibling of  I I . 
 
 
 
 6.1.2.  Essential part of  ℋ dy ​ f = ℋ 𝒮 dy ​ f \mathcal{H}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm dy}}f=\mathcal{H}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm dy}}_{\mathscr{S}}f 

 
 The following lemma shows that  2 − 1 / 2 ( Π − Π ∗ ) f 2^{-1/2}(\Pi-\Pi^{*})f  is the main part of  ℋ dy ​ f = ℋ 𝒮 dy ​ f \mathcal{H}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm dy}}f=\mathcal{H}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm dy}}_{\mathscr{S}}f ,
and the rest can be ignored. That means the estimate 
 

 
 
 ‖ ( Π − Π ∗ ) ​ f ‖ L 2 ​ ( W ) ≳ 𝓠 3 / 2 ​ ‖ f ‖ L 2 ​ ( W ) \displaystyle\|(\Pi-\Pi^{*})f\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(W)}\gtrsim\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}^{3/2}\|f\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(W)} 
 
 
 would prove Lemma  6.1 . 
 
 
 Lemma 6.2 . 
 
 The norms of the operators  Π 1 \Pi_{1} ,  Π 2 \Pi_{2} ,  Π 3 \Pi_{3} ,  𝕊 𝒮 0 \mathbb{S}^{0}_{\mathscr{S}}  and of their adjoints
are estimated above by  C ​ 𝓠 C\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}  in  L 2 ​ ( W ) L^{2}(W)    ( and so in  L 2 ​ ( W − 1 ) L^{2}(W^{-1})  as
well ) . 
 
 
 
 Remark 6.3 . 
 
 The above lemma implies immediately that for  f = 𝟏 I 0 ​ W − 1 ​ 𝐞 f=\mathbf{1}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}}W^{-1}\mathbf{e}  and
sufficiently large  𝓠 \boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}  one has the lower bound 
 

 
 
 ‖ 𝕊 ​ f ‖ L 2 ​ ( W ) ​ ≳ δ 0 < ​ 𝓠 3 / 2 ​ ‖ f ‖ L 2 ​ ( W ) . \displaystyle\|\mathbb{S}f\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(W)}\underset{\delta_{0}<}{\gtrsim}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}^{3/2}\|f\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(W)}. 
 
 
 
 
 Indeed, for this particular  f f 
 

 
 
 𝕊 f = 𝕊 𝒮 f = 2 − 1 / 2 ( Π + Π 1 − Π 2 − Π 3 ) f , \displaystyle\mathbb{S}f=\mathbb{S}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathscr{S}}f=2^{-1/2}\left(\Pi+\Pi_{1}-\Pi_{2}-\Pi_{3}\right)f, 
 
 
 and we have the desired lower bound for  ‖ Π ​ f ‖ L 2 ​ ( W ) \|\Pi f\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(W)} . By Lemma  6.2  the
rest is small in comparison, and can be ignored. 
 
 
 
 To prove Lemma  6.2  we need some results from  [ 31 ] . 
 
 
 Definition 6.4 . 
 
 Recall that a collection  𝒞 \mathcal{C}  of dyadic intervals is called  λ \lambda -Carleson ,
 λ > 0 \lambda>0 , if for any
dyadic interval  J J 
 

 
 
 ∑ I ∈ 𝒞 ⁡ ( J ) | I | ≤ λ ​ | J | ; \displaystyle\sum_{I\in\mathcal{C}(J)}|I|\leq\lambda|J|; 
 
 
 here we use the notation  𝒞 ⁡ ( J ) := { I ∈ 𝒞 : I ⊂ J } \mathcal{C}(J):=\{I\in\mathcal{C}:I\subset J\} . 
 
 
 
 Note that the collection  𝒮 \mathscr{S}  of
stopping intervals is trivially  2 2 -Carleson, and therefore the collection  𝒮 ^ := { I ^ : I ∈ 𝒮 } \widehat{\mathscr{S}}:=\{\widehat{I}:I\in\mathscr{S}\}  is  4 4 -Carleson. 
 
 
 We now restate Lemma 5.4 from  [ 31 ]  in the way adapted to our purposes. For a collection
 𝒞 \mathcal{C}  of dyadic intervals define the square function  S 1 = S 1 , 𝒞 , W S_{1}=S_{1,\mathcal{C},W}  as 
 

 
 (6.2) 
 
 S 1 g ( x ) := ( ∑ I ∈ 𝒞 ⟨ ∥ W ( x ) 1 / 2 W − 1 / 2 g ∥ ℝ d ⟩ I 2 𝟏 I ( x ) ) 1 / 2 . \displaystyle S_{1}g(x):=\left(\sum_{I\in\mathcal{C}}\left\langle\|W(x)^{1/2}W^{-1/2}g\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\right\rangle_{I}^{2}\mathbf{1}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}(x)\right)^{1/2}. 
 
 
 Here  g g  is a function with values in  ℝ d \mathbb{R}^{d}  and  W W  is a  d × d d\times d  matrix weight. 
 
 
 Lemma 6.5 . 
 
 Let  𝒞 \mathcal{C}  be a  λ \lambda -Carleson collection of dyadic intervals, and let  W ∈ 𝐀 2 dy W\in\mathbf{A}_{2}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm dy}} ,
 [ W ] 𝐀 2 dy ≤ 𝓠 [W]{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbf{A}_{2}}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm dy}}\leq\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}} . Then for  S 1 = S 1 , 𝒞 , W S_{1}=S{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{1,\mathcal{C},W}  there holds

 with an absolute constant  C C . 
 
 
 
 It is essentially Lemma 5.4 from  [ 31 ]  applied to the weights  W − 1 W^{-1}  and  W W  for  W W 
and  V V  in  [ 31 ] . Lemma 5.4 from  [ 31 ]  was stated for the operator  S ~ 1 \widetilde{S}_{1} 
that dominates  S 1 S_{1} .
The estimate there would translate to  C ​ d ​ λ ​ ( [ W − 1 ] 𝐀 2 dy ) 1 / 2 ​ ( [ W ] 𝐀 ∞ dy ) 1 / 2 Cd\lambda\left([W^{-1}]{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbf{A}_{2}}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm dy}}\right)^{1/2}\left([W]{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbf{A}_{\infty}}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm dy}}\right)^{1/2} , where
 [ W ] 𝐀 ∞ dy [W]{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbf{A}_{\infty}}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm dy}}  is the dyadic  𝐀 ∞ \mathbf{A}_{\infty}  characteristic of the weight  W W . We are not
giving the definition here, we only need to know that  [ W ] 𝐀 ∞ dy ≤ 4 ​ [ W ] 𝐀 2 dy [W]{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbf{A}_{\infty}}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm dy}}\leq 4[W]{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbf{A}_{2}}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm dy}} , see  [ 31 , Remark 4.4] .
Noticing that trivially
 [ W − 1 ] 𝐀 2 dy = [ W ] 𝐀 2 dy [W^{-1}]{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbf{A}_{2}}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm dy}}=[W]{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbf{A}_{2}}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm dy}}  we get the estimate stated in Lemma

 Proof of Lemma  6.2 . 
 
 Trivially, for an operator  T T  its norm in  L 2 ​ ( W ) L^{2}(W)  is the norm of the operator  g ↦ W 1 / 2 T W − 1 / 2 g g\mapsto W^{1/2}TW^{-1/2}g  (slightly abusing the notation we will say ‘‘of the operator  W 1 / 2 T W − 1 / 2 W^{1/2}TW^{-1/2} ’’) in the non-weighted space  L 2 L^{2} . Since the intervals  L ⁡ ( I ) L(I) ,
 I ∈ 𝒮 < I\in\mathscr{S}_{<}  are disjoint,
we can estimate 
 

 
 
 ∥ W 1 / 2 Π 3 W − 1 / 2 g ∥ L 2 2 \displaystyle\|W^{1/2}\Pi_{3}W^{-1/2}g\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}}^{2} 
 = ∑ I ∈ 𝒮 < ∫ L ⁡ ( I ) ∥ W ( x ) 1 / 2 ⟨ W − 1 / 2 g ⟩ I ∥ 2 d x \displaystyle=\sum_{I\in\mathscr{S}_{<}}\int_{L(I)}\|W(x)^{1/2}\langle W^{-1/2}g\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\|^{2}{\mathrm{d}}x 
 
 
 
 
 ≤ ∑ I ∈ 𝒮 < ∫ L ⁡ ( I ) ⟨ ∥ W ( x ) 1 / 2 W − 1 / 2 g ∥ ⟩ I 2 d x \displaystyle\leq\sum_{I\in\mathscr{S}_{<}}\int_{L(I)}\langle\|W(x)^{1/2}W^{-1/2}g\|\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{2}{\mathrm{d}}x 
 
 
 
 
 ≲ ∑ I ∈ 𝒮 < ∫ I ^ ⟨ ∥ W ( x ) 1 / 2 W − 1 / 2 g ∥ ⟩ I ^ 2 d x \displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{I\in\mathscr{S}_{<}}\int_{\widehat{I}}\langle\|W(x)^{1/2}W^{-1/2}g\|\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\widehat{I}}^{2}{\mathrm{d}}x 
 
 
 
 
 = ‖ S 1 , 𝒮 ^ < , W ​ g ‖ L 2 2 ≲ 𝓠 2 ​ ‖ g ‖ L 2 2 , \displaystyle=\left\|S{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{1,\widehat{\mathscr{S}}_{<},W}g\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\lesssim\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}^{2}\|g\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}}^{2}, 
 
 
 where  𝒮 ^ < := { I ^ : I ∈ 𝒮 < } \widehat{\mathscr{S}}_{<}:=\{\widehat{I}:I\in\mathscr{S}_{<}\} .
The crucial moment here is that the intervals  L ⁡ ( I ) L(I) ,  I ∈ 𝒮 < I\in\mathscr{S}_{<}  are disjoint, so we can
represent
the square of the norm as a sum of squares. 
 
 
 The estimate for  Π 1 \Pi_{1}  works absolutely the same way: the only difference is that in the first
and the second lines one should take the averages over  L ⁡ ( I ) L(I)  instead ot  I I . But the final
estimate will be the same. 
 
 
 As for  Π 2 \Pi_{2} , we can get the estimate by duality. Namely, replacing  W W  by  W − 1 W^{-1}  and repeating
for  Π 2 ∗ \Pi_{2}^{*}  the same calculations as above with obvious
modifications we get that 
 

 
 
 ∥ W − 1 / 2 Π 2 ∗ W 1 / 2 ∥ L 2 → L 2 2 ≲ ∥ S 1 , 𝒮 ^ < , W − 1 ∥ L 2 → L 2 2 ≲ 𝓠 2 . \displaystyle\|W^{-1/2}\Pi_{2}^{*}W^{1/2}\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}\to L^{2}}^{2}\lesssim\|S{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{1,\widehat{\mathscr{S}}_{<},W^{-1}}\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}\to L^{2}}^{2}\lesssim\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}^{2}. 
 
 
 Taking the adjoint we get that 
 

 
 
 ∥ W 1 / 2 Π 2 W − 1 / 2 ∥ L 2 → L 2 ≲ 𝓠 . \displaystyle\|W^{1/2}\Pi_{2}W^{-1/2}\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}\to L^{2}}\lesssim\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}. 
 
 
 
 
 Finally, to estimate  𝕊 𝒮 0 \mathbb{S}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathscr{S}}^{0}  we represent it as 
 

 
 
 𝕊 𝒮 0 = 𝕊 L − 𝕊 L ∗ , \displaystyle\mathbb{S}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathscr{S}}^{0}=\mathbb{S}_{\scriptstyle\text{\rm L}}-\mathbb{S}_{\scriptstyle\text{\rm L}}^{*}, 
 
 
 where  𝕊 L \mathbb{S}_{\scriptstyle\text{\rm L}}  is the  sparse left shift , 
 

 
 
 𝕊 L ​ g = ∑ I ∈ 𝒮 < ( g , h I ) L 2 ​ h L ⁡ ( I ) . \displaystyle\mathbb{S}_{\scriptstyle\text{\rm L}}g=\sum_{I\in\mathscr{S}_{<}}(g,h{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}}h{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L(I)}. 
 
 
 Since the intervals  L ⁡ ( I ) L(I) ,  I ∈ 𝒮 < I\in\mathscr{S}_{<}  are disjoint, using the same estimates as above, we get
that 
 

 
 
 ‖ 𝕊 L ‖ L 2 ​ ( W ) → L 2 ​ ( W ) ≲ 𝓠 . \displaystyle\|\mathbb{S}_{\scriptstyle\text{\rm L}}\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(W)\to L^{2}(W)}\lesssim\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}. 
 
 
 Replacing  W W  by  W − 1 W^{-1}  we get that 
 

 
 
 ‖ 𝕊 L ‖ L 2 ​ ( W − 1 ) → L 2 ​ ( W − 1 ) ≲ 𝓠 , \displaystyle\|\mathbb{S}_{\scriptstyle\text{\rm L}}\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(W^{-1})\to L^{2}(W^{-1})}\lesssim\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}, 
 
 
 which by duality implies that  ‖ 𝕊 L ∗ ‖ L 2 ​ ( W ) → L 2 ​ ( W ) ≲ 𝓠 \|\mathbb{S}_{\scriptstyle\text{\rm L}}^{*}\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(W)\to L^{2}(W)}\lesssim\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}} .
∎ 
 
 
 
 As it was discussed above, the lower bound for  ℋ 𝒮 dy ​ f \mathcal{H}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm dy}}_{\mathscr{S}}f  follows from the lower bound for 
 

 
 
 ‖ ( Π − Π ∗ ) ​ f ‖ L 2 ​ ( W ) . \|(\Pi-\Pi^{*})f\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(W)}. 
 
 
 
 
 We have the correct lower bound for  ‖ Π ​ f ‖ L 2 ​ ( W ) \|\Pi f\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(W)} .
To see that  ‖ ( Π − Π ⋆ ) ​ f ‖ L 2 ​ ( W ) \|(\Pi-\Pi^{\star})f\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(W)}  also has the correct lower bound, it suffices
to show that for any sufficiently large  𝓠 \boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}} 
 

 
 
 ( Π ​ f , Π ∗ ​ f ) L 2 ​ ( W ) ≤ 0 \left(\Pi f,\Pi^{*}f\right)_{L^{2}(W)}\leq 0 
 
 
 for all sufficiently small  δ 0 \delta_{0} . 
 
 
 We calculate 
 

 
 (6.3) 
 
 − ( Π ​ f , Π ∗ ​ f ) L 2 ​ ( W ) = \displaystyle-\left(\Pi f,\Pi^{*}f\right)_{L^{2}(W)}= 
 − ∑ I ∈ 𝒮 < ( ⟨ W h ^ I ⟩ I ⟨ W − 1 h ^ I ⟩ I 𝐞 , ⟨ W − 1 ⟩ I 𝐞 ) ℝ 2 | I | \displaystyle-\sum_{I\in\mathscr{S}_{<}}\left(\langle W\widehat{h}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\langle W^{-1}\widehat{h}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\mathbf{e},\langle W^{-1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\mathbf{e}\right){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|I|

 Here we expanded  Π ​ f \Pi f  indexing by  I I  and  Π ∗ ​ f \Pi^{*}f  by  J J : the first term is the diagonal

compute for  J ∈ 𝒮 k J\in\mathscr{S}_{k}  (compare with ( 5.5 ))

 ⟨ W − 1 ​ h ^ J ⟩ J = δ k ​ α ~ k − β ~ k 1 + δ k 2 ​ ( 𝐚 J ​ 𝐛 J ∗ + 𝐛 J ​ 𝐚 J ∗ ) ; \displaystyle\langle W^{-1}\widehat{h}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\!J}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\!J}=\delta_{k}\frac{\widetilde{\alpha}_{k}-\widetilde{\beta}_{k}}{1+\delta_{k}^{2}}\left(\mathbf{a}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\!J}\mathbf{b}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\!J}^{*}+\mathbf{b}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\!J}\mathbf{a}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\!J}^{*}\right);

 ( with implied constants not depending on  n n ,  k k ,  I I ,  J J ,  𝓠 \boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}} ) . 
 
 
 
 The proof is exactly the same as the proof of Lemma  5.5 , so we skip it.

 ( ⟨ W − 1 ​ h ^ I ⟩ I ​ 𝐞 , ⟨ W ​ h ^ J ⟩ J ​ ⟨ W − 1 ⟩ J ​ 𝐞 ) ℝ 2 \displaystyle\left(\langle W^{-1}\widehat{h}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\mathbf{e},\langle W\widehat{h}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\!J}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\!J}\langle W^{-1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\!J}\mathbf{e}\right){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}

 = σ I ′′ ​ s J ′′ ​ ( 𝐚 I , 𝐚 J ) ℝ 2 + σ I ′′ ​ t J ′′ ​ ( 𝐚 I , 𝐛 J ) ℝ 2 + τ I ′′ ​ t J ′′ ​ ( 𝐛 I , 𝐛 J ) ℝ 2 + τ I ′′ ​ s J ′′ ​ ( 𝐛 I , 𝐚 J ) ℝ 2 . \displaystyle\qquad\qquad=\sigma{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{\prime\prime}s{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\!J}^{\prime\prime}(\mathbf{a}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I},\mathbf{a}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}+\sigma{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{\prime\prime}t{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\!J}^{\prime\prime}(\mathbf{a}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I},\mathbf{b}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}+\tau{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{\prime\prime}t{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}^{\prime\prime}(\mathbf{b}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I},\mathbf{b}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}+\tau{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{\prime\prime}s{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}^{\prime\prime}(\mathbf{b}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I},\mathbf{a}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}. 
 
 
 
 
 We observe that  ( 𝐚 I , 𝐚 J ) ℝ 2 > 0 (\mathbf{a}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I},\mathbf{a}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}>0  and  ( 𝐛 I , 𝐛 J ) ℝ 2 > 0 (\mathbf{b}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I},\mathbf{b}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}>0  and
recall that  ( 𝐚 J , 𝐞 ) ℝ 2 ​ ( 𝐚 I , 𝐞 ) ℝ 2 > 0 (\mathbf{a}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J},\mathbf{e}){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(\mathbf{a}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I},\mathbf{e}){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}>0  and  ( 𝐛 J , 𝐞 ) ℝ 2 ​ ( 𝐛 I , 𝐞 ) ℝ 2 > 0 (\mathbf{b}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J},\mathbf{e}){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(\mathbf{b}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I},\mathbf{e}){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}>0 . Then,  α ~ k − β ~ k > 0 \widetilde{\alpha}_{k}-\widetilde{\beta}_{k}>0  and  α ~ k # − β ~ k # > 0 \widetilde{\alpha}_{k}^{\#}-\widetilde{\beta}_{k}^{\#}>0 
gives 
 

 
 
 σ I ′′ ​ s J ′′ ​ ( 𝐚 I , 𝐚 J ) ℝ 2 < 0 , τ I ′′ ​ t J ′′ ​ ( 𝐛 I , 𝐛 J ) ℝ 2 < 0 . \sigma{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{\prime\prime}s{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}^{\prime\prime}(\mathbf{a}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I},\mathbf{a}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}<0,\qquad\tau{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{\prime\prime}t{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}^{\prime\prime}(\mathbf{b}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I},\mathbf{b}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}<0. 
 
 
 
 
 We roughly estimate the remaining two terms in ( 6.10 ), noticing that

 implying that for sufficiently small  δ 0 \delta_{0}  the sum ( 6.10 ) is negative.
The minus sign in front of the sum ( 6.6 ) gives us a positive contribution there. 
 
 
 For the diagonal terms ( 6.3 ), let us figure out the sign of  ( ⟨ W ​ h ^ I ⟩ I ​ ⟨ W − 1 ​ h ^ I ⟩ I ​ 𝐞 , ⟨ W − 1 ⟩ I ​ 𝐞 ) ℝ 2 \left(\langle W\widehat{h}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\langle W^{-1}\widehat{h}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\mathbf{e},\langle W^{-1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\mathbf{e}\right){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbb{R}^{2}} .
The operators  ⟨ W ​ h ^ I ⟩ I \langle W\widehat{h}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}  and  ⟨ W − 1 ​ h ^ I ⟩ I \langle W^{-1}\widehat{h}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}  are given by
( 5.5 ) and ( 6.7 ) respectively, so their product is a negative multiple
of the identity  𝐈 \mathbf{I} . Therefore the sign of  ( ⟨ W ​ h ^ I ⟩ I ​ ⟨ W − 1 ​ h ^ I ⟩ I ​ 𝐞 , ⟨ W − 1 ⟩ I ​ 𝐞 ) ℝ 2 \left(\langle W\widehat{h}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\langle W^{-1}\widehat{h}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\mathbf{e},\langle W^{-1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\mathbf{e}\right){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbb{R}^{2}} 
is the same as the sign of 
 

 
 
 ( 𝐞 , ⟨ W − 1 ⟩ I ​ 𝐞 ) ℝ 2 < 0 , \displaystyle\left(\mathbf{e},\langle W^{-1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\mathbf{e}\right){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}<0, 
 
 
 which implies a positive contribution of the diagonal term.
 ∎ 
 
 
 
 
 6.2.  The lower bound for the classical Haar Shift  Ш \mathord{\mathchoice{\mbox{Ш}}{\mbox{Ш}}{\mbox{Ш}}{\mbox{Ш}}} 

 
 Even though we will not need the lower estimate for the full Haar Shift for our main result, it is
of independent interest and it is not hard to complete the proof. 
 
 
 Theorem 6.7 . 
 
 For the weight  W W  constructed above, and for the function  f = 𝟏 I 0 ​ W − 1 ​ 𝐞 f=\mathbf{1}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}}W^{-1}\mathbf{e} 
 ( 𝐞 = 𝐚 I 0 \mathbf{e}=\mathbf{a}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}} , or  𝐞 = 𝐚 I 0 + 𝐛 I 0 \mathbf{e}=\mathbf{a}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}}+\mathbf{b}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}} ) 
 

 
 
 ‖ Ш ​ f ‖ L 2 ​ ( W ) ≥ c ​ 𝓠 3 / 2 ​ ‖ f ‖ L 2 ​ ( W ) . \|\mathord{\mathchoice{\mbox{Ш}}{\mbox{Ш}}{\mbox{Ш}}{\mbox{Ш}}}f\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(W)}\geq c\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}^{3/2}\|f\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(W)}. 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 It was proved above, see Remark  6.3 , that for the odd Haar Shift  𝕊 \mathbb{S}  and
 f = 𝟏 I 0 ​ W − 1 ​ 𝐞 f=\mathbf{1}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}}W^{-1}\mathbf{e}  we have the desired estimate  ‖ 𝕊 ​ f ‖ L 2 ​ ( W ) ≳ 𝓠 3 / 2 \|\mathbb{S}f\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(W)}\gtrsim\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}^{3/2} .
Therefore, to prove Theorem  6.7 
it suffices to show that the contribution of the  even  Haar Shift  𝕊 ′ \mathbb{S}^{\prime}  (where summation is
over the intervals in
 𝒟 even = ( 𝒟 2 ​ k ) k ≥ 0 \mathscr{D}_{\scriptstyle\text{\rm even}}=(\mathscr{D}_{2k})_{k\geq 0} ), 
 

 
 
 𝕊 ′ ​ f = ∑ I ∈ 𝒟 even ( f , h I ) L 2 ​ [ h I + − h I − ] , \displaystyle\mathbb{S}^{\prime}f=\sum_{I\in\mathscr{D}_{\scriptstyle\text{\rm even}}}(f,h{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}}\left[h{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I_{+}}-h{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I_{-}}\right], 
 
 
 is small in comparison.
Below we will show that for
the weight  W W  and
our test function  f = 𝟏 I 0 ​ W − 1 ​ 𝐞 f=\mathbf{1}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}}W^{-1}\mathbf{e} 
 

 
 (6.11) 
 
 ‖ 𝕊 ′ ​ f ‖ L 2 ​ ( W ) ≲ 𝓠 ​ ‖ f ‖ L 2 ​ ( W ) , \displaystyle\|\mathbb{S}^{\prime}f\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(W)}\lesssim\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}\|f\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(W)}, 
 
 
 which will prove the theorem. 
 
 
 The weight  W − 1 W^{-1} , and so the test function  f = 𝟏 I 0 ​ W − 1 ​ 𝐞 f=\mathbf{1}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}}W^{-1}\mathbf{e} , are constant on children of
terminal intervals, see Section  4.5.2 , so the descendants of terminal intervals do
not contribute to  𝕊 ′ ​ f \mathbb{S}^{\prime}f .
The terminal intervals do contribute to  𝕊 ′ ​ f \mathbb{S}^{\prime}f , so expanding  ‖ 𝕊 ′ ​ f ‖ L 2 ​ ( W ) 2 \|\mathbb{S}^{\prime}f\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(W)}^{2}  we get 
 

 
 
 
 ‖ 𝕊 ′ ​ f ‖ L 2 ​ ( W ) 2 \displaystyle\|\mathbb{S}^{\prime}f\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(W)}^{2} 
 
 
 (6.12) 
 
 
 = ∑ I ∈ 𝒮 ∪ 𝒯 ∑ J ∈ 𝒮 ∪ 𝒯 ( [ ∫ W ⁡ ( h ^ I + − h ^ I − ) ​ ( h ^ J + − h ^ J − ) ] ​ ⟨ W − 1 ​ h ^ I ⟩ I ​ 𝐞 , ⟨ W − 1 ​ h ^ J ⟩ J ​ 𝐞 ) ℝ 2 , \displaystyle=\sum_{I\in\mathscr{S}\cup\mathcal{T}}\sum_{J\in\mathscr{S}\cup\mathcal{T}}\left(\left[\int W(\widehat{h}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I_{+}}-\widehat{h}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I_{-}})(\widehat{h}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J_{+}}-\widehat{h}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J_{-}})\right]\langle W^{-1}\widehat{h}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\mathbf{e},\langle W^{-1}\widehat{h}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}\mathbf{e}\right){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}, 
 
 
 where, recall,

terminal intervals.

 = ∫ ℝ ∑ I ∈ 𝒮 ∪ 𝒯 ⟨ ∥ W ( x ) 1 / 2 W − 1 / 2 W 1 / 2 f ∥ ℝ 2 ⟩ I 2 d x \displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\sum_{I\in\mathscr{S}\cup\mathcal{T}}\left\langle\|W(x)^{1/2}W^{-1/2}W^{1/2}f\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\right\rangle_{I}^{2}{\mathrm{d}}x 
 
 
 
 
 = ‖ S 1 ​ W 1 / 2 ​ f ‖ L 2 2 , \displaystyle=\|S_{1}W^{1/2}f\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}}^{2},

 To estimate the rest of the sum ( 6.12 ), let us thus consider one of the (symmetric) off
diagonal terms
with  J ⊊ I J\subsetneq I . If  I ∈ 𝒯 I\in\mathcal{T} 
then there is no contribution.  J ± J_{\pm} , so again there is no contribution. Thus we only need to consider the case  I , J ∈ 𝒮 I,J\in\mathscr{S} .
Let us assume that  I ∈ 𝒮 n I\in\mathscr{S}_{n} ,  J ∈ 𝒮 k J\in\mathscr{S}_{k} ,  k > n k>n .

 Let us regroup the terms into differences corresponding to ‘‘neighboring’’ stopping and terminal

 1 4 ​ [ ⟨ W ⟩ J + ⁣ + − ⟨ W ⟩ J − ⁣ + ] = 1 2 ⋅ δ k 1 + δ k 2 ​ ( β k + 1 # − α k + 1 # ) ​ ( 𝐚 J ​ 𝐛 J ∗ + 𝐛 J ​ 𝐚 J ∗ ) ; \displaystyle\frac{1}{4}\left[\langle W\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J_{++}}-\langle W\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J_{-+}}\right]=\frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{\delta_{k}}{1+\delta_{k}^{2}}(\beta_{k+1}^{\#}-\alpha_{k+1}^{\#})(\mathbf{a}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}\mathbf{b}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}^{*}+\mathbf{b}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}\mathbf{a}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}^{*}); 
 
 
 we use the same calculation as for ( 5.5 ) with  α ~ k # \widetilde{\alpha}_{k}^{\#} ,  β ~ k # \widetilde{\beta}_{k}^{\#} 
replaced by
their ‘‘stretched’’ counterparts  α k + 1 # = r ​ α ~ k # \alpha_{k+1}^{\#}=r\widetilde{\alpha}_{k}^{\#} ,  β k + 1 # = β ~ k # / ( s k ​ r ) \beta_{k+1}^{\#}=\widetilde{\beta}_{k}^{\#}/(s_{k}r) . 
 
 
 As for the terminal
intervals we get using the formulas ( 4.6 ) that

 By picking sufficiently small  δ 0 \delta_{0}  we can make the sum of off diagonal terms as small as we
want, so it can be ignored. Thus ( 6.11 ) holds, and Theorem  6.7  is proved.

 7.  Towards the lower bound for the Hilbert Transform: remodeling 

 
 Above we constructed a dyadic  𝐀 2 \mathbf{A}_{2}  weight  W = W 𝓠 W=W{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\!\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}}  on  I 0 = [ 0 , 1 ) I^{0}=[0,1) ,
 [ W ] 𝐀 2 dy = 𝓠 [W]{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbf{A}_{2}}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm dy}}=\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}  such that the dyadic operators, namely the paraproduct  Π \Pi , the
 odd  Haar Shift  𝕊 \mathbb{S}  and the linear combination  ℋ dy = c 1 ​ ( 𝕊 − 𝕊 ∗ ) + c 2 ​ 𝕊 0 \mathcal{H}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm dy}}=c_{1}(\mathbb{S}-\mathbb{S}^{*})+c_{2}\mathbb{S}_{0}  had lower
bounds  c ​ 𝓠 3 / 2 c\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}^{3/2} . 
 
 
 In this section we will use the weight  W W  to construct a weight  W ~ \widetilde{W}  such that  [ W ~ ] 𝐀 2 ≍ [ W ] 𝐀 2 dy = 𝓠 [\widetilde{W}]{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbf{A}_{2}}\asymp[W]{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbf{A}_{2}}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm dy}}=\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}  to prove later in Section  8 
that the norm of the Hilbert
Transform  ℋ \mathcal{H}  in  L 2 ​ ( W ~ ) L^{2}(\widetilde{W})  is bounded below by  c ​ 𝓠 3 / 2 c\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}^{3/2} . 
 
 
 Since from Lemma  6.1  we have a lower bound for the operator  ℋ dy \mathcal{H}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm dy}} ,
 ℋ dy = c 1 ​ ( 𝕊 − 𝕊 ∗ ) + c 2 ​ 𝕊 0 \mathcal{H}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm dy}}=c_{1}(\mathbb{S}-\mathbb{S}^{*})+c_{2}\mathbb{S}_{0} , there exist functions

 (7.1)

 In our construction the functions  f f ,  g g  were the normalized in  L 2 ​ ( W ) L^{2}(W) 
functions

Denoting  𝐠 := W ​ g \mathbf{g}:=Wg , and using  𝐟 = f \mathbf{f}=f  for consistency sake, we can see that  ‖ 𝐟 ‖ L 2 ​ ( W ) = ‖ 𝐠 ‖ L 2 ​ ( W − 1 ) = 1 \|\mathbf{f}\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(W)}=\|\mathbf{g}\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(W^{-1})}=1 , and ( 7.1 ) can be rewritten as

 And using the construction presented below, we will show that ( 7.2 ) implies the lower
bound for the Hilbert Transform  ℋ \mathcal{H}  in  L 2 ​ ( W ~ ) L^{2}(\widetilde{W})  for a weight  W ~ \widetilde{W}  remodeled from  W W . 
 
 
 Throughout this section we denote for intervals  I I ,  J J  by  ψ I , J \psi{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I,J}  the unique
orientation preserving affine bijection from  J J  to  I I . 
 
 
 7.1.  (Iterated) periodization 

 
 Given a function  f f  on an interval  I I  define its  periodization   𝒫 I N ​ f \mathcal{P}_{I}^{N}f  with
‘‘period’’  N N  as the unique function on  I I  with period  2 − N ​ | I | 2^{-N}|I| , consisting of repeated  2 N 2^{N} 
‘‘shrunken copies’’ of the function  f f , i.e.  𝒫 I N ​ f = f ∘ ψ I N \mathcal{P}_{I}^{N}f=f\circ\psi{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{N} , where the
restriction of  ψ I N \psi{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{N}  onto each  J ∈ ch N ⁡ ( I ) J\in\operatorname{ch}^{N}(I)  coincides with  ψ I , J \psi{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I,J} . Note that
 ψ I N \psi{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{N}  is a measure preserving transformation of the interval  I I . 
 
 
 Given a sequence  N → = ( N 1 , N 2 , N 3 , … ) \vec{N}=(N_{1},N_{2},N_{3},\ldots)  of frequencies  N k N_{k}  and a function  f f  on
 I 0 I^{0}  let us define the  iterated periodization   𝒫 N → ​ f \mathcal{P}^{\vec{N}}f  by induction as follows. 
 
 
 
 (i)  
 
 We start with the children  I ± 0 I^{0}_{\pm}  of  I 0 I^{0}  as the  starting intervals  of the first
order,  Start 1 \operatorname{Start}_{1}  and define  f 0 := f f_{0}:=f . 
 
 (ii)  
 
 Knowing the function  f k − 1 f_{k-1}  define the function  f k f_{k}  by replacing the function
 f k − 1 | I f_{k-1}|_{I} 
on every starting interval  I I  of order  k k  by its periodization  𝒫 I N k ​ ( f k − 1 | I ) \mathcal{P}_{I}^{N_{k}}(f_{k-1}|_{I}) . We will call the descendants of order  N k N_{k}  of the starting intervals of order  k k 
the  stopping intervals  of order  k k . 
 
 (iii)  
 
 Define the starting intervals of order  k + 1 k+1  as the grandchildren ( 4 4  intervals in
 ch 2 ⁡ ( I ) \operatorname{ch}^{2}(I) ) of the stopping intervals of order  k k , and return to step (ii). 
 
 
 
 
 To avoid technical details with convergence we will only consider functions  f ∈ L 1 ​ ( I 0 ) f\in L^{1}(I^{0})  with
finite Haar expansion; in this case the process stabilizes after finitely many steps. 
 
 
 Since a periodization is obtained by composing a function with a measure preserving
transformation, the iterated periodization  𝒫 N → ​ f \mathcal{P}^{\vec{N}}f  is also given by  𝒫 N → ​ f = f ∘ Ψ N → \mathcal{P}^{\vec{N}}f=f\circ\Psi^{\vec{N}} , where  Ψ N → \Psi^{\vec{N}}  is a measure preserving transformation. 
 
 
 7.1.1.  An alternative description of iterated periodization 

 
 Analyzing the process described above, we can see that the iterated periodization associates with a
starting interval  I I  of order  k k 
a unique interval  J = ℱ ⁡ ( I ) ∈ 𝒟 2 ​ k − 1 J=\mathcal{F}(I)\in\mathscr{D}_{2k-1} , such that  f k − 1 | I = f ∘ ψ J , I f_{k-1}|_{I}=f\circ\psi{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J,I} . Then, on
 I I  we perform the periodization of the
function  f ∘ ψ J , I f\circ\psi{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J,I} . We will also associate the same interval  J = ℱ ⁡ ( I ) ∈ 𝒟 2 ​ k − 1 J=\mathcal{F}(I)\in\mathscr{D}_{2k-1} 
with all
stopping subintervals  I ′ ⊂ I I^{\prime}\subset I  of order  k k , so  ℱ ⁡ ( I ′ ) = ℱ ⁡ ( I ) \mathcal{F}(I^{\prime})=\mathcal{F}(I)  for such intervals. 
 
 
 The function  ℱ \mathcal{F}  maps each starting (and stopping) interval  I I  of order  k k  to a
unique interval  ℱ ⁡ ( I ) ∈ 𝒟 2 ​ k − 1 \mathcal{F}(I)\in\mathscr{D}_{2k-1} . Note that the function  ℱ \mathcal{F}  depends (only) on the
sequence  N → \vec{N}  of indices, so  ℱ = ℱ N → \mathcal{F}=\mathcal{F}^{\vec{N}} ; however, to simplify our typography, we
will
often omit index  N → \vec{N} . 
 
 
 Now let us see what happens when we apply the periodization to the martingale difference
decomposition of  f f , 
 

 
 (7.3) 
 
 f = 𝐄 1 ​ f + ∑ J ∈ 𝒟 odd Δ J 2 ​ f . \displaystyle f=\mathbf{E}_{1}f+\sum_{J\in\mathscr{D}_{\scriptstyle\text{\rm odd}}}\Delta{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}^{2}f. 
 
 
 Since the periodization does not change constant functions, we can see that  𝒫 N → ​ 𝐄 1 ​ f = 𝐄 1 ​ f \mathcal{P}^{\vec{N}}\mathbf{E}_{1}f=\mathbf{E}_{1}f , and the periodization  𝒫 N → ​ Δ J 2 ​ f \mathcal{P}^{\vec{N}}\Delta{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}^{2}f  with  J ∈ 𝒟 2 ​ k − 1 J\in\mathscr{D}_{2k-1}  depends
only on indices  N j N_{j} ,  j ≤ k j\leq k . It is also easy to see that the starting intervals of order  k k ,
and action of the function  ℱ \mathcal{F}  on these intervals depends only on indices  N j N_{j} ,  j < k j<k . Thus
for  J ∈ 𝒟 2 ​ k − 1 J\in\mathscr{D}_{2k-1}  we can write 
 

 
 (7.4) 
 
 𝒫 N → Δ J 2 f = ∑ I ∈ Start k : ℱ ⁡ ( I ) = J 𝒫 I N k ( ( Δ J 2 f ) ∘ ψ J , I ) . \displaystyle\mathcal{P}^{\vec{N}}\Delta{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}^{2}f=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}I\in\operatorname{Start}_{k}:\\
\mathcal{F}(I)=J\end{subarray}}\mathcal{P}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{N_{k}}\left(\left(\Delta{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}^{2}f\right)\circ\psi{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J,I}\right). 
 
 
 
 
 Note that the position of the starting intervals  I ∈ Start k I\in\operatorname{Start}_{k} ,  ℱ ⁡ ( I ) = J \mathcal{F}(I)=J  depends only on
indices  N j N_{j} ,  j < k j<k . 
 
 
 Also, since the iterated periodization is given by a composition with a measure preserving
transformation, we can see that 
 

 
 (7.5) 
 
 ∑ I ∈ Start : ℱ ⁡ ( I ) = J | I | = | J | . \displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}I\in\operatorname{Start}:\\
\mathcal{F}(I)=J\end{subarray}}|I|=|J|. 
 
 
 
 
 Figure 4.  The first intervals on which periodisation is applied are
 Start 1 = { I − 0 , I + 0 } \operatorname{Start}_{1}=\{I^{0}_{-},I^{0}_{+}\} . The function  f | I − 0 f|_{I^{0}_{-}}  is copied  2 N 1 = 8 2^{N_{1}}=8  times onto the  8 8  intervals in  𝒟 4 ∩ I − 0 \mathcal{D}_{4}\cap{I^{0}_{-}} . The grandchildren  𝒟 6 ∩ I − 0 \mathcal{D}_{6}\cap{I^{0}_{-}}  of those  8 8  intervals constitute the new starting intervals  Start 2 ∩ I − 0 \operatorname{Start}_{2}\cap{I^{0}_{-}}  below  I − 0 I^{0}_{-} . Each of these grandchildren carries a copy of one of the grandchildren of  I − 0 I^{0}_{-} . For example the interval  J ∈ 𝒟 3 J\in\mathcal{D_{3}}  marked in red is the second grandchild (counting from the left) of  I − 0 I^{0}_{-} . Correspondingly the intervals  I ∈ Start 2 ∩ I − 0 I\in\operatorname{Start}_{2}\cap{I^{0}_{-}}  marked in red are the second grandchildren of intervals in  𝒟 4 ∩ I − 0 \mathcal{D}_{4}\cap{I^{0}_{-}} . As such, the second difference  Δ I 2 ​ f \Delta^{2}_{I}f  on those intervals is a compressed version of the second difference  Δ J 2 ​ f \Delta^{2}_{J}f  through the map  Ψ J , I \Psi_{J,I} , with  J = ℱ ⁡ ( I ) J=\mathcal{F}(I) . 
 
 
 Finally, the measure preserving property implies the preservation of products, norms, and inner
products: 
 
 
 Lemma 7.1 . 
 
 For  f , g ∈ L 2 ​ ( ℝ ) f,\,g\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})  with support on  I 0 I^{0}  we have 
 

 
 
 𝒫 N → ​ ( f ​ g ) = ( 𝒫 N → ​ f ) ​ ( 𝒫 N → ​ g ) , ( 𝒫 N → ​ f , 𝒫 N → ​ g ) L 2 ​ ( ℝ ) = ( f , g ) L 2 ​ ( ℝ ) , ‖ 𝒫 N → ​ f ‖ L 2 ​ ( ℝ ) = ‖ f ‖ L 2 ​ ( ℝ ) . \mathcal{P}^{\vec{N}}(fg)=(\mathcal{P}^{\vec{N}}f)(\mathcal{P}^{\vec{N}}g),\quad\left(\mathcal{P}^{\vec{N}}f,\mathcal{P}^{\vec{N}}g\right){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}=\left(f,g\right){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})},\quad\|\mathcal{P}^{\vec{N}}f\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}=\|f\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 7.2.  Remodeling 

 
 The iterated periodization will give us the desired lower bound for
 ( ℋ ℝ ​ 𝒫 N → ​ 𝐟 , 𝒫 N → ​ 𝐠 ) L 2 ​ ( ℝ ) \left(\mathcal{H}^{\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{P}^{\vec{N}}\mathbf{f},\mathcal{P}^{\vec{N}}\mathbf{g}\right){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})} . Unfortunately,
while the weight  𝒫 N → ​ W \mathcal{P}^{\vec{N}}W  has the same dyadic  𝐀 2 \mathbf{A}_{2}  characteristic as  W W , its  𝐀 2 \mathbf{A}_{2} 
characteristic can blow up. 
 
 
 Fortunately, a simple idea, introduced
by F. Nazarov in  [ 30 ]  and further
developed later in  [ 23 ] , allows one to pass from dyadic characteristics to the classical
ones. 
 
 
 7.2.1.  (Iterated) quasi-periodizations 

 
 We start with a dyadic  𝐀 2 \mathbf{A}_{2}  weight, and we want to get a
regular  𝐀 2 \mathbf{A}_{2}  weight (with comparable constant). Our weight  W W  is generally not an  𝐀 2 \mathbf{A}_{2} 
weight, it is only a dyadic  𝐀 2 \mathbf{A}_{2}  weight, and the periodization does not make the  𝐀 2 \mathbf{A}_{2} 
characteristic better. Fortunately, a slight modification of the iterated periodization, which we
will call the  quasi-periodization  allows us to get the  𝐀 2 \mathbf{A}_{2}  condition on all intervals. 
 
 
 Namely, for a function  f f  on  I 0 I^{0}  with finite Haar expansion we get its quasi-periodization as
follows. 
 
 
 Recall that in the periodization a starting interval  I I  of order  k k  is divided into  2 N k 2^{N_{k}} 
dyadic subintervals, which we called  stopping intervals  of order  k k . Let us call the
stopping intervals touching the boundary of  I I  the  exceptional  stopping intervals (there
are  2 2  of them), and the rest are the  regular  stopping intervals. We use the notation
 ℰ ⁡ ( I ) \mathcal{E}(I)  and  ℛ ⁡ ( I ) \mathcal{R}(I)  for exceptional and regular stopping subintervals respectively. 
 
 
 To get the function  f k f_{k}  from  f k − 1 f_{k-1}  on the starting interval  I I , we, as in the case of
iterated periodization, compute  𝒫 I N k ​ f k − 1 \mathcal{P}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{N_{k}}f_{k-1} ,
but on the exceptional stopping subintervals  I ′ ∈ ℰ ⁡ ( I ) I^{\prime}\in\mathcal{E}(I)  we put the values of the function  f k f_{k} 
to
be  ⟨ f k − 1 ⟩ I = ⟨ 𝒫 I N k ​ f k − 1 ⟩ I ′ \langle f_{k-1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}=\langle\mathcal{P}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{N_{k}}f_{k-1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{\prime}} ; on the regular stopping
intervals
 I ′ ∈ ℛ ⁡ ( I ) I^{\prime}\in\mathcal{R}(I)  we still have  f k = 𝒫 I N k ​ f k − 1 f_{k}=\mathcal{P}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{N_{k}}f_{k-1} . The result on the interval  I I  will
be denoted by  𝒬 ​ 𝒫 I N k ​ f k − 1 \mathcal{Q}\mathcal{P}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{N_{k}}f_{k-1} ; note that it differs from  𝒫 I N k ​ f k − 1 \mathcal{P}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{N_{k}}f_{k-1} 
only on two exceptional
stopping subintervals of  I I . 
 
 
 We then will do nothing on the exceptional stopping intervals, and continue on the regular
ones: take their grandchildren as the starting intervals, and so on…. 
 
 
 The process will stop after finitely many steps, and we denote the result as  𝒬 ​ 𝒫 N → ​ f \mathcal{Q}\mathcal{P}^{\vec{N}}f . 
 
 
 Note that the quasi-periodization is not given by a composition of the original function with a
measure
preserving transformation.
However, exactly as in the case of iterated periodization, we will assign to each starting and
each stopping interval  I I  of order  k k  a unique interval  ℱ ⁡ ( I ) ∈ 𝒟 2 ​ k − 1 \mathcal{F}(I)\in\mathscr{D}_{2k-1} , see end of
Section  7.1 . Formally, the function  ℱ \mathcal{F}  is the restriction of the function
described above in Section  7.1 , because it is not defined on any of the descendants of
the exceptional stopping intervals. However, to simplify typography again (and because we will not
be using this function for periodization anymore) we will be using the same
symbol  ℱ \mathcal{F}  for it. 
 
 
 We can compare the iterated periodization and the iterated quasi-periodization quantitatively. 
 
 
 Lemma 7.2 . 
 
 Let  f f  be a function with finite Haar expansion, we have 
 

 
 
 ‖ 𝒫 N → ​ f − 𝒬 ​ 𝒫 N → ​ f ‖ L 2 ​ ( ℝ ) ⟶ 0 as  ​ N → → ∞ . \displaystyle\left\|\mathcal{P}^{\vec{N}}f-\mathcal{Q}\mathcal{P}^{\vec{N}}f\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\longrightarrow 0\qquad\text{as }\vec{N}\to\infty. 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Since  f f  with finite Haar expansion are bounded, the difference above is controlled by the
total size of the exceptional intervals, which is arbitrarily small as  N → → ∞ \vec{N}\to\infty .
∎ 
 
 
 
 
 7.2.2.  An alternative description of the iterated quasi-periodization 

 
 Let us analyze the action of the quasi-periodization on martingale differences and write an
analogue of
formula ( 7.4 ) for the (iterated) quasi-periodization. Applying the iterated
quasi-periodization to the martingale difference decomposition ( 7.3 ), we get
almost the same formula as ( 7.4 ) with a slight modification. Namely, denote by
 Start ′ ⊂ Start \operatorname{Start}^{\prime}\subset\operatorname{Start}  the collection of all starting intervals that are not a subset of any
exceptional stopping interval. Then it is not hard to see that for  J ∈ 𝒟 2 ​ k − 1 J\in\mathscr{D}_{2k-1} 
 

 
 (7.6) 
 
 𝒬 𝒫 N → Δ J 2 f = ∑ I ∈ Start ′ : ℱ ⁡ ( I ) = J 𝒬 𝒫 I N k ( ( Δ J 2 f ) ∘ ψ J , I ) . \displaystyle\mathcal{Q}\mathcal{P}^{\vec{N}}\Delta{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}^{2}f=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}I\in\operatorname{Start}^{\prime}:\\
\mathcal{F}(I)=J\end{subarray}}\mathcal{Q}\mathcal{P}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{N_{k}}\left(\left(\Delta{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}^{2}f\right)\circ\psi{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J,I}\right). 
 
 
 
 
 Looking at this formula and the martingale difference decomposition ( 7.3 ), we
can see that for a function  f f  with finite Haar expansion its quasi-periodization  𝒬 ​ 𝒫 N → ​ f \mathcal{Q}\mathcal{P}^{\vec{N}}f  can be obtained by starting with the function  𝐄 1 ​ f \mathbf{E}_{1}f  (which is constant on  I ± 0 I^{0}_{\pm} ) and then
applying recursively on starting intervals the following procedure finitely many times: 
 
 
 Procedure 7.3 . 
 
 On an interval  I I  where the previously constructed function is constant, add to this
function a quasi-periodization  𝒬 ​ 𝒫 N ⁡ ( I ) ​ Δ I 2 ​ φ I \mathcal{Q}\mathcal{P}^{N(I)}\Delta{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{2}\varphi{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}  of an appropriate second
order martingale difference. 
 
 
 
 The order of starting intervals where we apply the above procedure is not really important: it
just needs to agree with the ordering by inclusion of the intervals. Namely, we can apply the
procedure to a starting interval only after we are done with all starting intervals containing it. 
 
 
 
 7.2.3.  Remodeling 

 
 In our case we apply the
quasi-periodization to the weights  W W ,  V = W − 1 V=W^{-1}  and the functions  𝐟 \mathbf{f} ,  𝐠 \mathbf{g} ; recall that we
assumed that all the objects have finite Haar expansions. We get the quasi-periodization of all
objects,
but while we do not need to worry about  𝐟 ~ := 𝒬 ​ 𝒫 N → ​ 𝐟 \widetilde{\mathbf{f}}:=\mathcal{Q}\mathcal{P}^{\vec{N}}\mathbf{f} ,  𝐠 ~ := 𝒬 ​ 𝒫 N → ​ 𝐠 \widetilde{\mathbf{g}}:=\mathcal{Q}\mathcal{P}^{\vec{N}}\mathbf{g} , we have a small problem with the quasi-periodizations of the weights: on the exceptional
stopping
intervals the weights  𝒬 ​ 𝒫 N → ​ V \mathcal{Q}\mathcal{P}^{\vec{N}}V  and  𝒬 ​ 𝒫 N → ​ W \mathcal{Q}\mathcal{P}^{\vec{N}}W  are not inverses of each other
(but they are everywhere else). 
 
 
 Fortunately, this problem is very easy to fix. After performing the (iterated) quasi-periodization,
we got a
disjoint collection of exceptional stopping intervals, where we have a problem. So, on each such
exceptional stopping interval
 I I  of order  k k  we replace constants by appropriately chosen weights
and perform the iterated quasi-periodization of these
weights.
We will get a new collection of exceptional stopping intervals, perform iterated
quasi-periodization of appropriate weights on these intervals, and so on… 
 
 
 To write it down formally, recall that during the quasi-periodization,
as it was discussed above at the end of Section  7.2.1 , we associate with each stopping
interval  I I  of order  k k  a unique interval  J = ℱ ⁡ ( I ) ∈ 𝒟 2 ​ k − 1 J=\mathcal{F}(I)\in\mathscr{D}_{2k-1} . And on the exceptional
stopping intervals we perform
the iterated quasi-periodization  𝒬 ​ 𝒫 2 → \mathcal{Q}\mathcal{P}^{\vec{2}}  of the weights  V ∘ ψ J , I V\circ\psi{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J,I} ,
 W ∘ ψ J , I W\circ\psi{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J,I} ,  J = ℱ ⁡ ( I ) J=\mathcal{F}(I) ; here  2 → := ( 2 , 2 , 2 , … ) \vec{2}:=(2,2,2,\ldots) . 
 
 
 We get a new set of exceptional stopping intervals, and their total length will be  1 / 2 1/2  of the
total length of the previous stopping intervals. On these new stopping intervals 3 3 
                 3 
                
                
                
              the map  ℱ \mathcal{F}  is naturally extended to new stopping and starting intervals. 
we again perform
the iterated quasi-periodization  𝒬 ​ 𝒫 2 → \mathcal{Q}\mathcal{P}^{\vec{2}}  of the corresponding weights, again treating
these stopping intervals, and not their grandchildren, as starting ones (of the same order), get
new stopping intervals, and so on. 
 
 
 In every step of the remodeling iterations, we will get new
exceptional stopping intervals, but their total measure will be half of the measure of the
previous stopping intervals. 
 
 
 Therefore, inductively repeating this procedure
and taking the limit, 4 4 
                 4 
                
                
                
              By the construction the limit exists a.e., and since  W W  and  V = W − 1 V=W^{-1}  have finite Haar
expansion, the limit exists in (matrix-valued)  L 1 L^{1} . 
we will end up with the weights  V ~ \widetilde{V} ,  W ~ \widetilde{W}  such that  V ~ = W ~ − 1 \widetilde{V}=\widetilde{W}^{-1}  a.e. 
 
 
 We will denote the new remodeled weights as  V ~ := ϱ N → ​ V \widetilde{V}:=\varrho^{\vec{N}}V ,  W ~ := ϱ N → ​ W \widetilde{W}:=\varrho^{\vec{N}}W . 
 
 
 Remark 7.4 . 
 
 We would like to emphasize that in the remodeling we only change the weights; the functions
 𝐟 ~ \widetilde{\mathbf{f}} ,  𝐠 ~ \widetilde{\mathbf{g}}  will be the quasi-periodizations  𝐟 ~ = 𝒬 ​ 𝒫 N → ​ 𝐟 \widetilde{\mathbf{f}}=\mathcal{Q}\mathcal{P}^{\vec{N}}\mathbf{f} ,
 𝐠 ~ = 𝒬 ​ 𝒫 N → ​ 𝐠 \widetilde{\mathbf{g}}=\mathcal{Q}\mathcal{P}^{\vec{N}}\mathbf{g} , and will not be changed any further. We will use Lemma
 7.9  below to estimate  ( ℋ ​ 𝐟 ~ , 𝐠 ~ ) L 2 \left(\mathcal{H}\widetilde{\mathbf{f}},\widetilde{\mathbf{g}}\right){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}} , so we will need to
pick appropriately large frequencies  N k N_{k}  later.
As for the behavior of the weight, all the modifications after the first quasi-periodization do not
influence estimates of  ( ℋ ​ 𝐟 ~ , 𝐠 ~ ) L 2 \left(\mathcal{H}\widetilde{\mathbf{f}},\widetilde{\mathbf{g}}\right){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}} , and we will not need arbitrarily
large frequencies. We are performing the remodeling to make the weights  V ~ \widetilde{V}  and  W ~ \widetilde{W}  to be
the inverses of each other, and for this purpose any frequency  N ≥ 2 N\geq 2  (the same for all
exceptional intervals) will work. So above we just picked  N = 2 N=2 . 
 
 
 
 Remark 7.5 . 
 
 As we discussed above, the remodeling in our construction was done by performing consecutively
quasi-periodizations on some intervals on some functions with finite Haar expansion, and
quasi-periodization is done applying the above Procedure  7.3  finitely many times. 
 
 
 Further analyzing the remodeling, we can see that the remodeling of the function  f f  can be
obtained by
starting with the function  𝐄 1 ​ f := ⟨ f ⟩ I + 0 ​ 𝟏 I + 0 + ⟨ f ⟩ I − 0 ​ 𝟏 I − 0 \mathbf{E}_{1}f:=\langle f\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}_{+}}\mathbf{1}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}_{+}}+\langle f\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}_{-}}\mathbf{1}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}_{-}}  (which is constant on intervals  I ± 0 I_{\pm}^{0} ), then
applying inductively Procedure  7.3  and taking the limit (a.e. and/or in  L 1 L^{1} ). 
 
 
 Again, the order of starting intervals, where we apply the above procedure is not really
important and it
only needs to agree with the ordering by inclusion of the intervals. Namely, we can apply the
procedure to a starting interval only after we are done with all starting intervals containing it. 
 
 
 
 The picture below illustrates the iterated quasi-periodisation process and should be compared with the iterated periodisation process from Fig.  4 .

The first intervals on which quasi-periodisation is applied are again
 Start 1 = { I − 0 , I + 0 } \operatorname{Start}_{1}=\{I^{0}_{-},I^{0}_{+}\} .
The function  f | I − 0 f|_{I^{0}_{-}}  is copied  2 N 1 − 2 = 6 2^{N_{1}}-2=6  times on the  6 6  regular stopping intervals  ℛ ⁡ ( I − 0 ) \mathcal{R}(I^{0}_{-})  in  𝒟 4 ∩ I − 0 \mathcal{D}_{4}\cap{I^{0}_{-}}  that are  not  touching the boundary of  I − 0 I^{0}_{-} . are the two exceptional stopping intervals  ℰ ⁡ ( I − 0 ) \mathcal{E}(I^{0}_{-})  below  I − 0 I^{0}_{-} ,
the red intervals are  ℰ ⁡ ( I + 0 ) \mathcal{E}(I^{0}_{+}) .
 
 
 
 Lemma 7.6 . 
 
 Let  𝐟 \mathbf{f} ,  𝐠 \mathbf{g} ,  W W ,  W − 1 W^{-1}  be functions with finite Haar expansions, and let  W N → := ϱ N → ​ W W{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\!\!\vec{N}}:=\varrho^{\vec{N}}W  be the remodeling of the weight  W W . Then 
 

 
 
 ‖ 𝒬 ​ 𝒫 N → ​ 𝐟 ‖ L 2 ​ ( ϱ N → ​ W ) ⟶ ‖ 𝐟 ‖ L 2 ​ ( W ) , ‖ 𝒬 ​ 𝒫 N → ​ 𝐠 ‖ L 2 ​ ( ( ϱ N → ​ W ) − 1 ) ⟶ ‖ 𝐠 ‖ L 2 ​ ( W − 1 ) . \displaystyle\left\|\mathcal{Q}\mathcal{P}^{\vec{N}}\mathbf{f}\right\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(\varrho^{\vec{N}}W)}\longrightarrow\|\mathbf{f}\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(W)},\qquad\left\|\mathcal{Q}\mathcal{P}^{\vec{N}}\mathbf{g}\right\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}((\varrho^{\vec{N}}W)^{-1})}\longrightarrow\|\mathbf{g}\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(W^{-1})}. 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Since the function  𝒬 ​ 𝒫 N → ​ 𝐟 \mathcal{Q}\mathcal{P}^{\vec{N}}\mathbf{f}  is constant on the exceptional stopping intervals, the
contribution of these intervals to the norm  ‖ 𝒬 ​ 𝒫 N → ​ 𝐟 ‖ L 2 ​ ( ϱ N → ​ W ) \left\|\mathcal{Q}\mathcal{P}^{\vec{N}}\mathbf{f}\right\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(\varrho^{\vec{N}}W)}  depends only on the averages of the weight  ϱ N → ​ W \varrho^{\vec{N}}W  there. Therefore 
 

 
 
 ‖ 𝒬 ​ 𝒫 N → ​ 𝐟 ‖ L 2 ​ ( ϱ N → ​ W ) = ‖ 𝒬 ​ 𝒫 N → ​ 𝐟 ‖ L 2 ​ ( 𝒬 ​ 𝒫 N → ​ W ) = ‖ 𝒬 ​ 𝒫 N → ​ 𝐟 ‖ L 2 ​ ( 𝒫 N → ​ W ) . \displaystyle\left\|\mathcal{Q}\mathcal{P}^{\vec{N}}\mathbf{f}\right\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(\varrho^{\vec{N}}W)}=\left\|\mathcal{Q}\mathcal{P}^{\vec{N}}\mathbf{f}\right\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(\mathcal{Q}\mathcal{P}^{\vec{N}}W)}=\left\|\mathcal{Q}\mathcal{P}^{\vec{N}}\mathbf{f}\right\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(\mathcal{P}^{\vec{N}}W)}. 
 
 
 We also trivially have  ‖ 𝒫 N → ​ 𝐟 ‖ L 2 ​ ( 𝒫 N → ​ W ) = ‖ 𝐟 ‖ L 2 ​ ( W ) \left\|\mathcal{P}^{\vec{N}}\mathbf{f}\right\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(\mathcal{P}^{\vec{N}}W)}=\|\mathbf{f}\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(W)} . Because of the finite Haar expansion the weight  W W  is bounded, and applying
Lemma  7.2  we get the first limit. 
 
 
 Noticing that  ( ϱ N → ​ W ) − 1 = ϱ N → ​ ( W − 1 ) (\varrho^{\vec{N}}W)^{-1}=\varrho^{\vec{N}}(W^{-1})  we immediately get the second limit.
∎ 
 
 
 
 
 
 7.3.  Hilbert Transforms and periodization 

 
 Recall that the Hilbert Transform on the real line  ℋ = ℋ ℝ \mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}^{\mathbb{R}}  is defined as 
 

 
 
 ℋ ℝ ​ f ​ ( s ) = 1 π ​ p.v. ​ ∫ ℝ f ⁡ ( t ) s − t ​ 𝑑 t \displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{\mathbb{R}}f(s)=\frac{1}{\pi}\text{p.v.}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{f(t)}{s-t}{\mathrm{d}}t 
 
 
 and the Hilbert Transform  ℋ 𝕋 \mathcal{H}^{\mathbb{T}}  on the torus  𝕋 := ℝ / ℤ \mathbb{T}:=\mathbb{R}/\penalty\mathbb{Z}  (the periodic Hilbert Transform)
is given by 
 

 
 
 ℋ 𝕋 ​ f ​ ( s ) = p.v. ​ ∫ 0 1 f ⁡ ( t ) ​ cot ⁡ ( π ⋅ ( s − t ) ) ​ 𝑑 t ; \displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{\mathbb{T}}f(s)=\text{p.v.}\int_{0}^{1}f(t)\cot\left(\pi\cdot(s-t)\right){\mathrm{d}}t; 
 
 
 here we naturally identify  𝕋 \mathbb{T}  with  I 0 = [ 0 , 1 ) I^{0}=[0,1)  with the Lebesgue measure  d ​ x {\mathrm{d}}x . We can also
identify  𝕋 \mathbb{T}  with an arbitrary interval  I I  with the normalized Lebesgue measure  | I | − 1 ​ d ​ x |I|^{-1}{\mathrm{d}}x .
In this case the Hilbert Transform on the torus  𝕋 \mathbb{T}  is represented as 
 

 
 
 ℋ I 𝕋 ​ f ​ ( s ) = | I | − 1 ​ p.v. ​ ∫ I f ⁡ ( t ) ​ cot ⁡ ( | I | − 1 ​ π ⋅ ( s − t ) ) ​ 𝑑 t , s ∈ I . \displaystyle\mathcal{H}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{\mathbb{T}}f(s)=|I|^{-1}\text{p.v.}\int_{I}f(t)\cot\left(|I|^{-1}\pi\cdot(s-t)\right){\mathrm{d}}t,\qquad s\in I. 
 
 
 
 
 If we denote  L 2 ​ ( I ) = L 2 ​ ( I , | I | − 1 ​ d ​ x ) L^{2}(I)=L^{2}(I,|I|^{-1}{\mathrm{d}}x) , then the representations  ℋ I 𝕋 \mathcal{H}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{\mathbb{T}}  in  L 2 ​ ( I ) L^{2}(I) 
and  ℋ J 𝕋 \mathcal{H}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}^{\mathbb{T}}  in  L 2 ​ ( J ) L^{2}(J)  are unitarily equivalent, 
 

 
 (7.7) 
 
 ℋ I 𝕋 ​ U I , J = U I , J ​ ℋ J 𝕋 , \displaystyle\mathcal{H}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{\mathbb{T}}U{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I,J}=U{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I,J}\mathcal{H}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}^{\mathbb{T}}, 
 
 where the unitary operator  U I , J : L 2 ​ ( J ) → L 2 ​ ( I ) U{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I,J}:L^{2}(J)\to L^{2}(I)  is given by 
 
 (7.8)

 Define 
 

 
 (7.9)

 Recall, that extending a function  f ∈ L 2 ​ ( I 0 ) f\in L^{2}(I^{0})  periodically to the
real line  ℝ \mathbb{R} , we naturally identify  I 0 I^{0}  with the torus  𝕋 = ℝ / ℤ \mathbb{T}=\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}  and the space
 L 2 ​ ( I 0 ) L^{2}(I^{0})  with  L 2 ​ ( 𝕋 ) L^{2}(\mathbb{T}) .
The rotation of the torus  𝕋 \mathbb{T}  through  π \pi , i.e. map  f ( ⋅ ) ↦ f ( ⋅ + 1 / 2 ) f(\,\cdot\,)\mapsto f(\,\cdot\,+1/2) 
in  OPEN L 2 ​ ( ℝ / ℤ ) ) = L 2 ​ ( I 0 ) L^{2}(\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}))=L^{2}(I^{0})  is a unitary transformation in  L 2 ​ ( I 0 ) L^{2}(I^{0}) , mapping  h I 0 h{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}}  to
 − h I 0 -h{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}} , and  h I + 0 h{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}_{+}}  to  h I − 0 h{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}_{-}} , so we can write 
 

 
 (7.10)

 (7.11)

 (7.12)

 Lemma 7.7 . 
 
 Let  𝐟 \mathbf{f} ,  𝐠 \mathbf{g}  be functions on  I 0 = [ 0 , 1 ) I^{0}=[0,1)  with finite Haar expansion. Then

 along some family of  N → → ∞ \vec{N}\to\infty   ( all  N k → ∞ N_{k}\to\infty ) . 
 
 
 
 Remark 7.8 . 
 
 It is possible to show that in fact we have the convergence as  N → → ∞ \vec{N}\to\infty , but for our
purposes convergence along some family is sufficient. 
 
 
 
 Using Lemma  7.2  we can trivially get from
the above Lemma  7.7 
 
 
 Lemma 7.9 . 
 
 Let  𝐟 \mathbf{f} ,  𝐠 \mathbf{g}  be functions on  I 0 = [ 0 , 1 ) I^{0}=[0,1)  with finite Haar expansion. Then 
 

 
 
 ( ℋ ℝ ​ 𝒬 ​ 𝒫 N → ​ 𝐟 , 𝒬 ​ 𝒫 N → ​ 𝐠 ) L 2 ​ ( ℝ ) ⟶ ( ℋ dy ​ 𝐟 , 𝐠 ) L 2 ​ ( I 0 ) + c 0 ​ [ ( ⟨ 𝐟 ⟩ I + 0 , ⟨ 𝐠 ⟩ I − 0 ) ℝ 2 − ( ⟨ 𝐟 ⟩ I − 0 , ⟨ 𝐠 ⟩ I + 0 ) ℝ 2 ] . \displaystyle\left(\mathcal{H}^{\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{Q}\mathcal{P}^{\vec{N}}\mathbf{f},\mathcal{Q}\mathcal{P}^{\vec{N}}\mathbf{g}\right)_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\longrightarrow\left(\mathcal{H}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm dy}}\mathbf{f},\mathbf{g}\right){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(I^{0})}+c_{0}\left[\left(\langle\mathbf{f}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}_{+}},\langle\mathbf{g}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}_{-}}\right){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}-\left(\langle\mathbf{f}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}_{-}},\langle\mathbf{g}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}_{+}}\right){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\right]. 
 
 
 along some family of  N → → ∞ \vec{N}\to\infty   ( all  N k → ∞ N_{k}\to\infty ) . 
 
 
 
 In both lemmas above the first term  ( ℋ dy ​ 𝐟 , 𝐠 ) L 2 ​ ( I 0 ) \left(\mathcal{H}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm dy}}\mathbf{f},\mathbf{g}\right){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(I^{0})}  is the essential one
that will be used, and the second term is small in comparison. 
 
 
 To prove Lemma  7.7  we will need some additional results. The first one explains the
connection between the operator  ℋ dy \mathcal{H}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm dy}}  and the periodic Hilbert Transform  ℋ I 𝕋 \mathcal{H}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{\mathbb{T}} .
