 Lemma 7.11 . 
 
 Let  f f  be a function on an interval  I I ,  ⟨ f ⟩ I = 0 \langle f\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}=0  with finite Haar expansion on  I I ,
extended by  0 0  outside of  I I . Then 
 

 
 (7.16) 
 
 lim N → ∞ ‖ ℋ ℝ ​ 𝒫 I N ​ f − 𝒫 I N ​ ℋ I 𝕋 ​ f ‖ L 2 ​ ( I ) = 0 , lim N → ∞ ‖ 𝟏 ℝ ∖ I ​ ℋ ℝ ​ 𝒫 I N ​ f ‖ L 2 ​ ( ℝ ) = 0 . \displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\mathcal{H}^{\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{P}^{N}_{I}f-\mathcal{P}^{N}_{I}\mathcal{H}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{\mathbb{T}}f\right\|_{L^{2}(I)}=0,\qquad\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\mathbf{1}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbb{R}\setminus I}\mathcal{H}^{\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{P}^{N}_{I}f\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}=0. 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Since the Hilbert Transforms  ℋ ℝ \mathcal{H}^{\mathbb{R}}  and  ℋ I 𝕋 \mathcal{H}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{\mathbb{T}}  commute with rescaling and translations,
it is sufficient to prove the lemma for  I = I 0 = [ 0 , 1 ) I=I^{0}=[0,1) . 
 
 
 Let  g N g^{N}  be the function  𝒫 I 0 N ​ f \mathcal{P}^{N}_{I^{0}}f  extended by  1 1 -periodicity to  ℝ \mathbb{R} . Then for any
 f ∈ L 2 ​ ( I 0 ) f\in L^{2}(I^{0})  we have on  I 0 I^{0} 
 

 
 
 ℋ ℝ ​ g N = 𝒫 I 0 N ​ ℋ 𝕋 ​ f ; \displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{\mathbb{R}}g^{N}=\mathcal{P}^{N}_{I^{0}}\mathcal{H}^{\mathbb{T}}f; 
 
 
 the identity is trivial for the exponentials  e 2 ​ π ​ i ​ k ​ x e^{2\pi ikx} ,  k ∈ ℤ k\in\mathbb{Z} , and extends to
 L 2 ​ ( I 0 ) L^{2}(I^{0})  by continuity. We then need to show that 
 

 
 (7.17) 
 
 ‖ 𝟏 I 0 ​ ℋ ℝ ​ ( 𝟏 ℝ ∖ I 0 ) ‖ → 0 as  ​ N → ∞ . \displaystyle\left\|\mathbf{1}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}}\mathcal{H}^{\mathbb{R}}(\mathbf{1}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbb{R}\setminus I^{0}})\right\|\to 0\qquad\text{as }N\to\infty. 
 
 
 But this is an easy exercise in basic analysis. Namely, denote  I m 0 := [ − 2 − m , 1 + 2 − m ] I^{0}_{m}:=[-2^{-m},1+2^{-m}] . Then
using the fact that  ⟨ g N ⟩ I = 0 \langle g^{N}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}=0  for any interval  I I  of length  2 − N 2^{-N} , we can easily
see that for a fixed  m ∈ ℕ m\in\mathbb{N} 
 

 
 
 ℋ ℝ ​ ( 𝟏 ℝ ∖ I m 0 ) ⇉ 0 on  ​ I 0 , as  ​ N → ∞ . \displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{\mathbb{R}}(\mathbf{1}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbb{R}\setminus I^{0}_{m}})\rightrightarrows 0\quad\text{on }I^{0},\qquad\text{as }N\to\infty. 
 
 
 But  ‖ 1 I m 0 ∖ I 0 ​ f ‖ L 2 \|1{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}_{m}\setminus I^{0}}f\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}}  can be made arbitrarily small, and together with the
boundedness of  ℋ ℝ \mathcal{H}^{\mathbb{R}}  this implies ( 7.17 ). Thus, the first identity in ( 7.16 ) is proved. 
 
 
 The second identity in ( 7.16 ) is proved similarly to ( 7.17 ).
∎ 
 
 
 
 Proof of Lemma  7.7 . 
 
 Let  Start = Start N → \operatorname{Start}=\operatorname{Start}^{\vec{N}}  be the collection of all starting intervals appearing in the
iterated periodization, and let  Start k = Start k N → \operatorname{Start}_{k}=\operatorname{Start}^{\vec{N}}_{k}  be the collection of all starting
intervals of order  k k . Since the periodization does not change constant functions, using the
alternative description of the periodization in Section  7.1.1  we can write 
 

 
 
 𝒫 N → ​ 𝐟 \displaystyle\mathcal{P}^{\vec{N}}\mathbf{f}

 so if we can make the sum of the cross terms as small as we want, the lemma is proved. 
 
 
 To complete the proof, let us show that asymptotically, we can ignore the cross terms.
Since we have finitely many cross terms, it is sufficient to show that given small  ε > 0 \varepsilon>0 
we can make each cross term bounded by  ε \varepsilon . 
 
 
 First, using the second limit in Lemma  7.11 , we can see that for all starting
intervals
 I I ,  J J  of order  1 1 ,  I ≠ J I\neq J , and for all sufficiently large  N 1 N_{1} , 
 

 
 (7.19)

 We know that functions  D I ​ 𝐟 D{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\mathbf{f} ,  D I ​ 𝐠 D{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\mathbf{g}  are just periodizations of appropriate

 | ( ℋ ℝ ​ D I ​ 𝐟 , 𝐄 1 ​ 𝐠 ) L 2 ​ ( I 0 ) | < ε , | ( ℋ ℝ ​ 𝐄 1 ​ 𝐟 , D I ​ 𝐠 ) L 2 ​ ( I 0 ) | < ε \left|\left(\mathcal{H}^{\mathbb{R}}D{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\mathbf{f},\mathbf{E}_{1}\mathbf{g}\right){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(I^{0})}\right|<\varepsilon,\qquad\left|\left(\mathcal{H}^{\mathbb{R}}\mathbf{E}_{1}\mathbf{f},D{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\mathbf{g}\right){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(I^{0})}\right|<\varepsilon 
 
 
 for all starting intervals  I I  of order  1 1 .

 (7.20)

 8.  Lower bound for the Hilbert Transform 

 
 Proof of Theorem  1.1 . 
 
 The proof of the main theorem is now in order. We want to find functions  f ~ ∈ L 2 ​ ( W ) \widetilde{f}\in L^{2}(W) ,  g ~ ∈ L 2 ​ ( W ) \widetilde{g}\in L^{2}(W) ,  ‖ f ~ ‖ L 2 ​ ( W ) = ‖ g ~ ‖ L 2 ​ ( W ) = 1 \|\widetilde{f}\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(W)}=\|\widetilde{g}\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(W)}=1  such that for
 ℋ = ℋ ℝ \mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}^{\mathbb{R}} 
 

 
 
 ( ℋ ​ f ~ , g ~ ) L 2 ​ ( W ) ≳ 𝓠 3 / 2 . \displaystyle\left(\mathcal{H}\widetilde{f},\widetilde{g}\right){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(W)}\gtrsim\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}^{3/2}. 
 
 
 Denoting  𝐠 ~ := W ​ g ~ \widetilde{\mathbf{g}}:=W\widetilde{g}  (and  𝐟 ~ := f ~ \widetilde{\mathbf{f}}:=\widetilde{f}  for
consistency of notation), we can see that
the above estimate is
equivalent to 
 

 
 (8.1) 
 
 ( ℋ ​ 𝐟 ~ , 𝐠 ~ ) L 2 ​ ( I 0 ) ≳ 𝓠 3 / 2 , ‖ 𝐟 ~ ‖ L 2 ​ ( W ) = ‖ 𝐠 ~ ‖ L 2 ​ ( W − 1 ) = 1 \displaystyle\left(\mathcal{H}\widetilde{\mathbf{f}},\widetilde{\mathbf{g}}\right){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(I^{0})}\gtrsim\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}^{3/2},\qquad\|\widetilde{\mathbf{f}}\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(W)}=\|\widetilde{\mathbf{g}}\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(W^{-1})}=1 
 
 
 (with the same implied constant). 
 
 
 Recall that from Lemma  7.9 , we have
for any  𝐟 \mathbf{f} ,  𝐠 \mathbf{g}  with finite Haar expansion 
 

 
 (8.2) 
 
 ( ℋ ℝ ​ 𝒬 ​ 𝒫 N → ​ 𝐟 , 𝒬 ​ 𝒫 N → ​ 𝐠 ) L 2 ​ ( ℝ ) ⟶ ( ℋ dy ​ 𝐟 , 𝐠 ) L 2 ​ ( I 0 ) + c 0 ​ [ ( ⟨ 𝐟 ⟩ I + 0 , ⟨ 𝐠 ⟩ I − 0 ) ℝ 2 − ( ⟨ 𝐟 ⟩ I − 0 , ⟨ 𝐠 ⟩ I + 0 ) ℝ 2 ] . \displaystyle\left(\mathcal{H}^{\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{Q}\mathcal{P}^{\vec{N}}\mathbf{f},\mathcal{Q}\mathcal{P}^{\vec{N}}\mathbf{g}\right)_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\longrightarrow\left(\mathcal{H}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm dy}}\mathbf{f},\mathbf{g}\right){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(I^{0})}+c_{0}\left[\left(\langle\mathbf{f}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}_{+}},\langle\mathbf{g}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}_{-}}\right){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}-\left(\langle\mathbf{f}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}_{-}},\langle\mathbf{g}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}_{+}}\right){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\right]. 
 
 
 We also know, see ( 7.2 ), that there exist  𝐟 \mathbf{f} ,  𝐠 \mathbf{g} ,
 ‖ 𝐟 ‖ L 2 ​ ( W ) = ‖ 𝐠 ‖ L 2 ​ ( W − 1 ) = 1 \|\mathbf{f}\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(W)}=\|\mathbf{g}\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(W^{-1})}=1 , such that 
 

 
 (8.3) 
 
 ( ℋ dy ​ 𝐟 , 𝐠 ) L 2 ​ ( I 0 ) ≳ 𝓠 3 / 2 . \displaystyle\left(\mathcal{H}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm dy}}\mathbf{f},\mathbf{g}\right){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(I^{0})}\gtrsim\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}^{3/2}. 
 
 
 
 
 In our example the functions  𝐟 \mathbf{f}  and  𝐠 \mathbf{g}  were explicitly constructed, but in what follows
we only need the estimate ( 8.3 ). 
 
 
 Trivially, by taking a slightly smaller implicit constant in the estimate ( 8.3 ) we can
always assume that  𝐟 \mathbf{f}  and  𝐠 \mathbf{g}  have finite Haar expansions (so ( 8.2 )
also holds). 
 
 
 Let us show that the second term in ( 8.2 ) is negligible. For that it is enough to
show that for any  𝐟 \mathbf{f} ,  𝐠 \mathbf{g} ,  ‖ 𝐟 ‖ L 2 ​ ( W ) = ‖ 𝐠 ‖ L 2 ​ ( W − 1 ) = 1 \|\mathbf{f}\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(W)}=\|\mathbf{g}\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(W^{-1})}=1  we have 
 

 
 (8.4) 
 
 | ( ⟨ 𝐟 ⟩ I + 0 , ⟨ 𝐠 ⟩ I − 0 ) ℝ 2 | , | ( ⟨ 𝐟 ⟩ I − 0 , ⟨ 𝐠 ⟩ I + 0 ) ℝ 2 | ≤ 4 ​ 𝓠 1 / 2 . \displaystyle\left|\left(\langle\mathbf{f}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}_{+}},\langle\mathbf{g}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}_{-}}\right)_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\right|,\ \left|\left(\langle\mathbf{f}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}_{-}},\langle\mathbf{g}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}_{+}}\right)_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\right|\leq 4\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}^{1/2}. 
 
 
 But the quantity  ‖ ⟨ W ⟩ I 1 / 2 ​ ⟨ W − 1 ⟩ I 1 / 2 ‖ \left\|\langle W\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{1/2}\langle W^{-1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{1/2}\right\|  is exactly
the norm of the averaging operator  f ↦ 𝐄 I ​ f = 𝟏 I ​ ⟨ f ⟩ I f\mapsto\mathbf{E}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}f=\mathbf{1}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\langle f\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I} , so for any
 𝐟 ^ \hat{\mathbf{f}} ,  𝐠 ^ \hat{\mathbf{g}} 
 

 
 
 | ( ⟨ 𝐟 ^ ⟩ I 0 , ⟨ 𝐠 ^ ⟩ I 0 ) ℝ 2 | ≤ 𝓠 1 / 2 ​ ‖ 𝐟 ^ ‖ L 2 ​ ( W ) ​ ‖ 𝐠 ^ ‖ L 2 ​ ( W − 1 ) . \displaystyle\left|\left(\langle\hat{\mathbf{f}}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}},\langle\hat{\mathbf{g}}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}}\right)_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\right|\leq\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}^{1/2}\|\hat{\mathbf{f}}\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(W)}\|\hat{\mathbf{g}}\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(W^{-1})}. 
 
 
 Taking  𝐟 ^ := 𝟏 I + 0 ​ 𝐟 \hat{\mathbf{f}}:=\mathbf{1}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}_{+}}\mathbf{f} ,  𝐠 ^ := 𝟏 I − 0 ​ 𝐠 \hat{\mathbf{g}}:=\mathbf{1}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}_{-}}\mathbf{g}  we see that 
 

 
 
 ⟨ 𝐟 ⟩ I + 0 = 2 ​ ⟨ 𝐟 ^ ⟩ I 0 , ⟨ 𝐠 ⟩ I − 0 = 2 ​ ⟨ 𝐟 ^ ⟩ I 0 . \displaystyle\langle\mathbf{f}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}_{+}}=2\langle\hat{\mathbf{f}}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}},\qquad\langle\mathbf{g}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}_{-}}=2\langle\hat{\mathbf{f}}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}}. 
 
 
 Noticing that  ‖ 𝐟 ^ ‖ L 2 ​ ( W ) ≤ ‖ 𝐟 ‖ L 2 ​ ( W ) \|\hat{\mathbf{f}}\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(W)}\leq\|\mathbf{f}\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(W)} ,  ‖ 𝐠 ^ ‖ L 2 ​ ( W − 1 ) ≤ ‖ 𝐠 ‖ L 2 ​ ( W − 1 ) \|\hat{\mathbf{g}}\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(W^{-1})}\leq\|\mathbf{g}\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(W^{-1})}  we immediately get the first inequality in ( 8.4 ). Taking
 𝐟 ^ := 𝟏 I − 0 ​ 𝐟 \hat{\mathbf{f}}:=\mathbf{1}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}_{-}}\mathbf{f} ,  𝐠 ^ := 𝟏 I + 0 ​ 𝐠 \hat{\mathbf{g}}:=\mathbf{1}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}_{+}}\mathbf{g}  we get the second one. 
 
 
 Therefore the last term in ( 8.2 ) is bounded by  4 ​ 𝓠 1 / 2 ≪ 𝓠 3 / 2 4\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}^{1/2}\ll\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}^{3/2} 
so for large  𝓠 \boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}  it is negligible. This means that for sufficiently large  𝓠 \boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}} 
 

 
 
 ( ℋ ℝ ​ 𝒬 ​ 𝒫 N → ​ 𝐟 , 𝒬 ​ 𝒫 N → ​ 𝐠 ) L 2 ​ ( ℝ ) ≳ 𝓠 3 / 2 . \displaystyle\left(\mathcal{H}^{\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{Q}\mathcal{P}^{\vec{N}}\mathbf{f},\mathcal{Q}\mathcal{P}^{\vec{N}}\mathbf{g}\right)_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\gtrsim\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}^{3/2}. 
 
 
 But by Lemma  7.6 
 

 
 
 ‖ 𝒬 ​ 𝒫 N → ​ 𝐟 ‖ L 2 ​ ( ϱ N → ​ W ) ⟶ ‖ 𝐟 ‖ L 2 ​ ( W ) = 1 , ‖ 𝒬 ​ 𝒫 N → ​ 𝐠 ‖ L 2 ​ ( ( ϱ N → ​ W ) − 1 ) ⟶ ‖ 𝐠 ‖ L 2 ​ ( W − 1 ) = 1 , \displaystyle\left\|\mathcal{Q}\mathcal{P}^{\vec{N}}\mathbf{f}\right\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(\varrho^{\vec{N}}W)}\longrightarrow\|\mathbf{f}\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(W)}=1,\qquad\left\|\mathcal{Q}\mathcal{P}^{\vec{N}}\mathbf{g}\right\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}((\varrho^{\vec{N}}W)^{-1})}\longrightarrow\|\mathbf{g}\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(W^{-1})}=1, 
 
 
 so we got the desired lower bound ( 8.1 ) (with  𝐟 ~ \widetilde{\mathbf{f}} ,  𝐠 ~ \widetilde{\mathbf{g}}  being the
functions  𝒬 ​ 𝒫 N → ​ 𝐟 \mathcal{Q}\mathcal{P}^{\vec{N}}\mathbf{f}  and
 𝒬 ​ 𝒫 N → ​ 𝐠 \mathcal{Q}\mathcal{P}^{\vec{N}}\mathbf{g}  normalized in  L 2 ​ ( W ) L^{2}(W)  and  L 2 ​ ( W − 1 ) L^{2}(W^{-1})  respectively).
∎ 
 
 
 
 
 9.  Tracking the  𝐀 2 \mathbf{A}_{2}  constant 

 
 In the above sections we constructed (for a sufficiently large  𝓠 \boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}} ) the weight  W ~ \widetilde{W}  on
 I 0 := [ 0 , 1 ) I^{0}:=[0,1) ,  [ W ~ ] 𝐀 2 dy ≤ 𝓠 [\widetilde{W}]{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbf{A}_{2}}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm dy}}\leq\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}} , and a function  𝐟 ∈ L 2 ​ ( W ~ ) \mathbf{f}\in L^{2}(\widetilde{W})  (supported on
 I 0 I^{0} ),  ‖ f ‖ L 2 ​ ( W ) ≠ 0 \|f\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(W)}\neq 0  such
that 
 

 
 
 ‖ ℋ dy ​ 𝐟 ‖ L 2 ​ ( W ~ ) ≳ 𝓠 3 / 2 ​ ‖ f ‖ L 2 ​ ( W ~ ) . \displaystyle\|\mathcal{H}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm dy}}\mathbf{f}\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(\widetilde{W})}\gtrsim\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}^{3/2}\|f\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(\widetilde{W})}. 
 
 
 Extending this weight periodically to the real line, and slightly abusing notation using the same
symbol for it, it is trivial that the above inequality holds for the same function  𝐟 \mathbf{f} 
(supported on  I 0 I^{0} , not extended periodically). 
 
 
 It is trivial that after extending periodically, the weight  W ~ \widetilde{W}  still satisfies the dyadic
 𝐀 2 \mathbf{A}_{2} 
condition and that  [ W ~ ] 𝐀 2 dy ≤ 𝓠 [\widetilde{W}]{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbf{A}_{2}}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm dy}}\leq\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}} . Here by the dyadic  𝐀 2 \mathbf{A}_{2}  condition we mean
the condition ( 3.1 ) with  𝒟 \mathscr{D}  being the collection of all dyadic subintervals of  ℝ \mathbb{R} ,
not just  𝒟 ⁡ ( I 0 ) \mathscr{D}(I^{0}) . 
 
 
 In the rest of this section we will follow this agreement and use  𝒟 \mathscr{D}  for the collection of all
dyadic subintervals of  ℝ \mathbb{R} . 
 
 
 We claim that our weight  W ~ \widetilde{W}  (periodically extended) satisfies, in fact, the standard  𝐀 2 \mathbf{A}_{2} 
condition, and that  [ W ~ ] 𝐀 2 ≤ 16 2 ​ 𝓠 [\widetilde{W}]{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbf{A}_{2}}\leq 16^{2}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}} . 
 
 
 9.1.  The strong dyadic  𝐀 2 \mathbf{A}_{2}  condition 

 
 We will need the following definition. 
 
 
 Definition 9.1 . 
 
 We say that a weight  W W  satisfies the  strong dyadic   𝐀 2 \mathbf{A}_{2}  condition, denoted
 W ∈ 𝐀 2 sd W\in\mathbf{A}_{2}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm sd}} , if the estimate ( 1.1 ) is satisfied for all intervals  I I  that are a
union of 2 adjacent dyadic intervals of equal length. The corresponding supremum, the strong dyadic
 𝐀 2 \mathbf{A}_{2} 
characteristic, will be denoted as  [ W ] 𝐀 2 sd [W]{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbf{A}_{2}}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm sd}} . 
 
 
 
 Note that any dyadic interval is a union of its children, so
trivially  [ W ] 𝐀 2 dy ≤ [ W ] 𝐀 2 sd ≤ [ W ] 𝐀 2 [W]{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbf{A}_{2}}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm dy}}\leq[W]{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbf{A}_{2}}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm sd}}\leq[W]{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbf{A}_{2}} . The lemma below shows
that the classes  𝐀 2 \mathbf{A}_{2}  and  𝐀 2 sd \mathbf{A}_{2}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm sd}}  coincide, and that the corresponding characteristics
are equivalent. 
 
 
 Lemma 9.2 . 
 
 For a matrix weight  W W , 
 

 
 
 [ W ] 𝐀 2 ≤ 16 ​ [ W ] 𝐀 2 sd . \displaystyle[W]{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbf{A}_{2}}\leq 16[W]{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbf{A}_{2}}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm sd}}. 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Take an arbitrary interval  J J  (not necessarily dyadic), and let  I 1 I_{1}  be a dyadic interval such
that 
 

 
 
 | J | ≤ | I 1 | ≤ 2 ​ | J | , I 1 ∩ J ≠ ∅ . \displaystyle|J|\leq|I_{1}|\leq 2|J|,\qquad I_{1}\cap J\neq\varnothing. 
 
 
 We can find a dyadic interval  I 2 I_{2}  adjacent to  I 1 I_{1} , with
 | I 2 | = | I 1 | |I_{2}|=|I_{1}|  such that  J ⊂ I 1 ∪ I 2 = : I J\subset I_{1}\cup I_{2}=:I . Trivially  | I | ≤ 4 ​ | J | |I|\leq 4|J| , so 
 

 
 (9.1) 
 
 ⟨ W ⟩ J \displaystyle\langle W\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\!J} 
 ≤ | J | − 1 ​ ∫ I W ⁡ ( x ) ​ 𝑑 x ≤ 4 ​ ⟨ W ⟩ I , \displaystyle\leq|J|^{-1}\int_{I}W(x){\mathrm{d}}x\leq 4\langle W\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I},\qquad 
 
 
 (9.2) 
 
 ⟨ W − 1 ⟩ J \displaystyle\langle W^{-1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\!J} 
 ≤ 4 ​ ⟨ W − 1 ⟩ I . \displaystyle\leq 4\langle W^{-1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}. 
 
 The inequality ( 9.2 ) is equivalent to 
 
 (9.3) 
 
 ⟨ W − 1 ⟩ I − 1 \displaystyle\langle W^{-1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{-1} 
 ≤ 4 ​ ⟨ W − 1 ⟩ J − 1 , \displaystyle\leq 4\langle W^{-1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\!J}^{-1}, 
 
 
 so using Lemma  3.2 
we can write 
 

 
 
 ⟨ W ⟩ J ≤ 4 ​ ⟨ W ⟩ I ≤ 4 ​ [ W ] 𝐀 2 sd ​ ⟨ W − 1 ⟩ I − 1 ≤ 16 ​ [ W ] 𝐀 2 sd ​ ⟨ W − 1 ⟩ J − 1 ; \displaystyle\langle W\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\!J}\leq 4\langle W\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\leq 4[W]{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbf{A}_{2}}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm sd}}\langle W^{-1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{-1}\leq 16[W]{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbf{A}_{2}}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm sd}}\langle W^{-1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}^{-1}\,; 
 
 
 the second inequality here follows from Lemma  3.2 , the last one from
( 9.3 ). 
 
 
 Applying Lemma  3.2  again we get the conclusion of the lemma.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Below we will prove that  [ W ~ ] 𝐀 2 sd ≤ 16 ​ 𝓠 [\widetilde{W}]{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbf{A}_{2}}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm sd}}\leq 16\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}} , which by Lemma  9.2  gives us
that  [ W ~ ] 𝐀 2 ≤ 16 2 ​ 𝓠 [\widetilde{W}]{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbf{A}_{2}}\leq 16^{2}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}} . 
 
 
 
 9.2.  Procedure  7.3  and strong dyadic  𝐀 2 \mathbf{A}_{2} 

 
 Let us recall, see
Remark  7.5 , that the remodeled weight  W ~ \widetilde{W}  was obtained from the dyadic weight  W W  by
starting with the dyadic weight  𝐄 1 ​ W := ⟨ W ⟩ I + 0 ​ 𝟏 I + 0 + ⟨ W ⟩ I − 0 ​ 𝟏 I − 0 \mathbf{E}_{1}W:=\langle W\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}_{+}}\mathbf{1}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}_{+}}+\langle W\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}_{-}}\mathbf{1}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}_{-}} , applying inductively Procedure  7.3  and taking the limit. 
 
 
 Note, that the inverse  W ~ − 1 \widetilde{W}^{-1}  of the remodeled weight is obtained exactly the same way from
the weight  W − 1 W^{-1} : start with the weight  𝐄 1 ​ ( W − 1 ) \mathbf{E}_{1}(W^{-1})  and apply Procedure  7.3 
exactly the same way. 
 
 
 That was discussed for the remodeling of a weight on the interval  I 0 I^{0} , but the same can be said
about the periodic extension of the remodeled weight. Namely, this periodic extension can be
obtained from taking the weight  𝐄 1 ​ W \mathbf{E}_{1}W , extending it periodically, then applying steps
described in Procedure  7.3  to starting intervals and taking the limit. 
 
 
 To see that it can be done, take some numbering  I k I_{k} ,  k ∈ ℕ k\in\mathbb{N}  of the dyadic intervals of length
 1 1 , and start with the periodic extension of the weight  𝐄 1 ​ W \mathbf{E}_{1}W . Let us assume, without loss of
generality, that  I 1 = I 0 I_{1}=I^{0} , and let  I 1 , k I_{1,k}  be the (ordered) starting subintervals of  I 1 I_{1}  to
which we consecutively apply Procedure  7.3  to get the remodeled weight  W ~ \widetilde{W}  on
the interval  I 1 I_{1} . 
 
 
 Let  I j , k I_{j,k}  be the translation of the starting interval  I 1 , k I_{1,k}  to the interval  I j I_{j} .
Performing Procedure  7.3  consecutively on intervals  I 1 , 1 I_{1,1} ,  I 1 , 2 I_{1,2} ,  I 2 , 1 I_{2,1} ,
 I 1 , 3 I_{1,3} ,  I 2 , 2 I_{2,2} ,  I 3 , 1 I_{3,1} ,  I 1 , 4 I_{1,4}  etc, gives us the periodic extension of the remodeled
weight. 
 
 
 Note that the inverse  W ~ − 1 \widetilde{W}^{-1}  of the (periodically extended) remodeled weight is obtained
 exactly  the same way from the weight  V = W − 1 V=W^{-1} . 
 
 
 Since we will be dealing with the averages of  W W  and  V = W − 1 V=W^{-1} , we will need an extra
definition. 
 
 
 Definition 9.3 . 
 
 We say that a pair of weights  V V ,  W W  satisfies the strong dyadic  𝐀 2 \mathbf{A}_{2}  condition (and write
 ( V , W ) ∈ 𝐀 2 sd (V,W)\in\mathbf{A}_{2}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm sd}} ) if 
 

 
 
 sup I ‖ ⟨ V ⟩ I 1 / 2 ​ ⟨ W ⟩ I 1 / 2 ‖ 2 := [ V , W ] 𝐀 2 sd < ∞ , \displaystyle\sup_{I}\|\langle V\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{1/2}\langle W\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{1/2}\|^{2}:=[V,W]{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbf{A}_{2}}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm sd}}<\infty, 
 
 
 where the supremum is taken over all intervals  I I  that are unions of two adjecent dyadic intervals
of equal length. 
 
 
 The quantity  [ V , W ] 𝐀 2 sd [V,W]{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbf{A}_{2}}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm sd}}  is called the  strong dyadic  𝐀 2 \mathbf{A}_{2}  characteristic  of
the pair  V V ,  W W . 
 
 
 
 The next lemma is crucial for controlling the  𝐀 2 \mathbf{A}_{2}  characteristic. 
 
 
 Lemma 9.4 . 
 
 Let  V V ,  W W  be matrix weights on  ℝ \mathbb{R}  such that  [ V , W ] 𝐀 2 sd ≤ 16 ​ 𝓠 [V,W]{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbf{A}_{2}}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm sd}}\leq 16\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}} . Assuming that
 V V  and  W W  are constant on an interval  J ∈ 𝒟 J\in\mathscr{D} , define new weights  V 1 V_{1} ,  W 1 W_{1}  as 
 

 
 
 V 1 := V + 𝒬 ​ 𝒫 J N ​ Δ J 2 ​ Ψ , W 1 := W + 𝒬 ​ 𝒫 J N ​ Δ J 2 ​ Φ , N ≥ 2 , \displaystyle V_{1}:=V+\mathcal{Q}\mathcal{P}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}^{N}\Delta{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}^{2}\Psi,\qquad W_{1}:=W+\mathcal{Q}\mathcal{P}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}^{N}\Delta{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}^{2}\Phi,\qquad N\geq 2, 
 
 
 where  Ψ = Ψ ∗ \Psi=\Psi^{*} ,  Φ = Φ ∗ \Phi=\Phi^{*}  are some matrix-valued functions 5 5 
                   5 
                  
                  
                  
                 Here we are implicitly assuming  V 1 V_{1} ,  W 1 W_{1}  are weights, i.e. that  ⟨ V ⟩ J + Δ J 2 ​ Ψ ≥ 0 \langle V\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}+\Delta{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}^{2}\Psi\geq 0 ,  ⟨ W ⟩ J + Δ J 2 ​ Φ ≥ 0 \langle W\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}+\Delta{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}^{2}\Phi\geq 0  on  J J . . 
 
 
 If 
 
 
 

 
 (9.4) 
 
 ‖ ⟨ V ⟩ J 1 / 2 ​ ⟨ W ⟩ J 1 / 2 ‖ 2 ≤ 𝓠 , \displaystyle\|\langle V\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\!J}^{1/2}\langle W\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\!J}^{1/2}\|^{2}\leq\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}, 
 
 
 then 
 

 
 
 [ V 1 , W 1 ] 𝐀 2 sd ≤ 16 ​ 𝓠 . \displaystyle[V_{1},W_{1}]{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbf{A}_{2}}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm sd}}\leq 16\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}. 
 
 
 
 
 
 This lemma implies that  [ W ~ ] 𝐀 2 sd ≤ 16 ​ 𝓠 [\widetilde{W}]{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbf{A}_{2}}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm sd}}\leq 16\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}} , which is exactly what we need. 
 
 
 Proof of Lemma  9.4 . 
 
 Let  I 1 I_{1} ,  I 2 I_{2}  be two adjacent dyadic intervals of equal length, and let  I := I 1 ∪ I 2 I:=I_{1}\cup I_{2} . We
want to show that 
 

 
 (9.5) 
 
 ‖ ⟨ V 1 ⟩ I 1 / 2 ​ ⟨ W 1 ⟩ I 1 / 2 ‖ 2 ≤ 16 ​ 𝓠 . \displaystyle\|\langle V_{1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{1/2}\langle W_{1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{1/2}\|^{2}\leq 16\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}. 
 
 
 First, notice that if  J ∩ I = ∅ J\cap I=\varnothing  or  J ⊂ I J\subset I , then trivially 
 

 
 (9.6) 
 
 ⟨ V 1 ⟩ I = ⟨ V ⟩ I , ⟨ W 1 ⟩ I = ⟨ W ⟩ I , \displaystyle\langle V_{1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}=\langle V\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I},\qquad\langle W_{1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}=\langle W\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}, 
 
 
 so ( 9.5 ) holds (because  [ V , W ] 𝐀 2 sd ≤ 16 ​ 𝓠 [V,W]{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbf{A}_{2}}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm sd}}\leq 16\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}} ). 
 
 
 It remains to consider the case  I ∩ J ≠ ∅ I\cap J\neq\varnothing , but  J ⊄ I J\not\subset I . There are two
possibilities here: 
 
 (i)  
 
 J ⊄ I J\not\subset I  and  I ⊄ J I\not\subset J ; 
 
 (ii)  
 
 I ⊊ J I\subsetneq J . 
 
 
 
 
 In the first case one of the intervals (say  I 1 I_{1} ) does not intersect  J J , but touches its
boundary, and the other one ( I 2 I_{2} ) is strictly inside of  J J  and touches the boundary of  J J  at
the same point. 
 
 
 In this case the identity ( 9.6 ) holds for  I 2 I_{2} , see Lemma  9.5  below.
For  I 1 I_{1}  it holds trivially (the weights do not change), so ( 9.6 ) holds for
 I = I 1 ∪ I 2 I=I_{1}\cup I_{2} . 
 
 
 Therefore, ( 9.5 ) holds for  I I  because
 [ V , W ] 𝐀 2 sd ≤ 16 ​ 𝓠 [V,W]{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbf{A}_{2}}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm sd}}\leq 16\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}} . 
 
 
 Finally, let us treat the case (ii),  I ⊊ J I\subsetneq J .
Writing  J J  as a disjoint union 
 

 
 
 J = ⋃ k = 1 2 N + 2 J k ′ , J k ′ ∈ ch N + 1 ⁡ ( J ) , \displaystyle J=\bigcup_{k=1}^{2^{N+2}}J_{k}^{\prime},\qquad J_{k}^{\prime}\in\operatorname{ch}^{N+1}(J), 
 
 
 we can see that the weights  V 1 V_{1} ,  W 1 W_{1}  are constant on the intervals  J k ′ J_{k}^{\prime} . 
 
 
 Defining the auxiliary weights 
 

 
 
 V ~ := ⟨ V ⟩ J + Δ J 2 ​ Ψ , W ~ := ⟨ W ⟩ J + Δ J 2 ​ Φ , \displaystyle\widetilde{V}:=\langle V\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}+\Delta{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}^{2}\Psi,\qquad\widetilde{W}:=\langle W\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}+\Delta{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}^{2}\Phi, 
 
 
 we can see that for any  J k ′ ∈ ch N + 2 ⁡ ( J ) J_{k}^{\prime}\in\operatorname{ch}^{N+2}(J)  there exists  I ′ ∈ ch 2 ⁡ ( J ) ∪ { J } I^{\prime}\in\operatorname{ch}^{2}(J)\cup\{J\}  such that 
 

 
 
 ⟨ V 1 ⟩ J k ′ = ⟨ V ~ ⟩ I ′ , ⟨ W 1 ⟩ J k ′ = ⟨ W ~ ⟩ I ′ ; \displaystyle\langle V_{1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J_{k}^{\prime}}=\langle\widetilde{V}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{\prime}}\,,\qquad\langle W_{1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J_{k}^{\prime}}=\langle\widetilde{W}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{\prime}}; 
 
 
 (the case  I ′ = J I^{\prime}=J  happens when  J k ′ J_{k}^{\prime}  is a subset of an exceptional stopping interval). 
 
 
 Since for any  φ ∈ L 1 ​ ( J ) \varphi\in L^{1}(J) 
 

 
 
 ⟨ φ ⟩ J = 1 4 ​ ∑ I ′ ∈ ch 2 ⁡ ( J ) ⟨ φ ⟩ I ′ , \displaystyle\langle\varphi\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}=\frac{1}{4}\sum_{I^{\prime}\in\operatorname{ch}^{2}(J)}\langle\varphi\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{\prime}}, 
 
 
 we can see that for any  J k ′ ∈ ch N + 2 ⁡ ( J ) J_{k}^{\prime}\in\operatorname{ch}^{N+2}(J)  either 
 

 
 (9.7) 
 
 ⟨ V 1 ⟩ J k ′ \displaystyle\langle V_{1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J_{k}^{\prime}} 
 = ⟨ V ~ ⟩ I ′ , \displaystyle=\langle\widetilde{V}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{\prime}}, 
 ⟨ W 1 ⟩ J k ′ \displaystyle\langle W_{1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J_{k}^{\prime}} 
 = ⟨ W ~ ⟩ I ′ for some  ​ I ′ ∈ ch 2 ⁡ ( J ) \displaystyle=\langle\widetilde{W}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{\prime}}\quad\text{for some }I^{\prime}\in\operatorname{ch}^{2}(J) 
 
 or 
 
 (9.8) 
 
 ⟨ V 1 ⟩ J k ′ \displaystyle\langle V_{1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J_{k}^{\prime}} 
 = ⟨ V ~ ⟩ J = 1 4 ​ ∑ I ′ ∈ ch 2 ⁡ ( J ) ⟨ V ~ ⟩ I ′ , \displaystyle=\langle\widetilde{V}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}=\frac{1}{4}\sum_{I^{\prime}\in\operatorname{ch}^{2}(J)}\langle\widetilde{V}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{\prime}}, 
 ⟨ W 1 ⟩ J k ′ \displaystyle\langle W_{1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J_{k}^{\prime}} 
 = ⟨ W ~ ⟩ J = 1 4 ​ ∑ I ′ ∈ ch 2 ⁡ ( J ) ⟨ W ~ ⟩ I ′ . \displaystyle=\langle\widetilde{W}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}=\frac{1}{4}\sum_{I^{\prime}\in\operatorname{ch}^{2}(J)}\langle\widetilde{W}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{\prime}}\,. 
 
 
 
 
 Let again  n ∈ ℕ n\in\mathbb{N}  be such that  I ∈ ch n ⁡ ( J ) I\in\operatorname{ch}^{n}(J) . If  n ≥ N + 2 n\geq N+2  then since  V 1 V_{1} ,  W 1 W_{1}  are constant
on intervals  J k ′ ∈ ch N + 2 ⁡ ( J ) J_{k}^{\prime}\in\operatorname{ch}^{N+2}(J) , we have 
 

 
 
 ⟨ V 1 ⟩ I = ⟨ V 1 ⟩ J k ′ , where  ​ I ⊂ J k ′ ∈ ch N + 2 ⁡ ( J ) . \displaystyle\langle V_{1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}=\langle V_{1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J_{k}^{\prime}},\qquad\text{where\ }I\subset J_{k}^{\prime}\in\operatorname{ch}^{N+2}(J). 
 
 
 If  n < N + 2 n<N+2 , then  I I  is a disjoint union of some  J k ′ ∈ ch N + 2 ⁡ ( J ) J_{k}^{\prime}\in\operatorname{ch}^{N+2}(J) , so  ⟨ V 1 ⟩ I \langle V_{1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I} ,
 ⟨ W 1 ⟩ I \langle W_{1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}  are the averages of  ⟨ V 1 ⟩ J k ′ \langle V_{1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J_{k}^{\prime}} ,  ⟨ W 1 ⟩ J k ′ \langle W_{1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J_{k}^{\prime}}  over these
 J k ′ J_{k}^{\prime} . Combining this with ( 9.7 ), ( 9.8 ) we get that in any case 
 

 
 (9.9) 
 
 ⟨ V 1 ⟩ I \displaystyle\langle V_{1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I} 
 = ∑ I ′ ∈ ch 2 ⁡ ( J ) α I ′ ​ ⟨ V ~ ⟩ I ′ , ⟨ W 1 ⟩ I = ∑ I ′ ∈ ch 2 ⁡ ( J ) α I ′ ​ ⟨ W ~ ⟩ I ′ , \displaystyle=\sum_{I^{\prime}\in\operatorname{ch}^{2}(J)}\alpha{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{\prime}}\langle\widetilde{V}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{\prime}},\qquad\langle W_{1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}=\sum_{I^{\prime}\in\operatorname{ch}^{2}(J)}\alpha{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{\prime}}\langle\widetilde{W}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{\prime}}, 
 
 where 
 
 
 α I ′ \displaystyle\alpha{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{\prime}} 
 ≥ 0 , ∑ I ′ ∈ ch 2 ⁡ ( J ) α I ′ = 1 . \displaystyle\geq 0,\qquad\sum_{I^{\prime}\in\operatorname{ch}^{2}(J)}\alpha{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{\prime}}=1. 
 
 
 
 
 But for any  I ′ ∈ ch 2 ⁡ ( J ) I^{\prime}\in\operatorname{ch}^{2}(J) 
 

 
 
 ⟨ V ~ ⟩ I ′ ≤ 4 ​ ⟨ V ~ ⟩ J = 4 ​ ⟨ V ⟩ J , ⟨ W ~ ⟩ I ′ ≤ 4 ​ ⟨ W ~ ⟩ J = 4 ​ ⟨ W ⟩ J \displaystyle\langle\widetilde{V}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{\prime}}\leq 4\langle\widetilde{V}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}=4\langle V\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}\,,\qquad\langle\widetilde{W}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{\prime}}\leq 4\langle\widetilde{W}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}=4\langle W\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J} 
 
 
 so 
 

 
 
 ⟨ V 1 ⟩ I ≤ 4 ​ ⟨ V ⟩ J , ⟨ W 1 ⟩ I \displaystyle\langle V_{1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\leq 4\langle V\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}\,,\qquad\langle W_{1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I} 
 ≤ 4 ​ ⟨ W ⟩ J . \displaystyle\leq 4\langle W\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}. 
 
 The second inequality is equivalent to 
 
 
 ⟨ W ⟩ J − 1 \displaystyle\langle W\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}^{-1} 
 ≤ 4 ​ ⟨ W 1 ⟩ I − 1 , \displaystyle\leq 4\langle W_{1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{-1}, 
 
 
 so we can write 
 

 
 
 ⟨ V 1 ⟩ I ≤ 4 ​ ⟨ V ⟩ J ≤ 4 ​ 𝓠 ​ ⟨ W ⟩ J − 1 ≤ 16 ​ 𝓠 ​ ⟨ W 1 ⟩ I − 1 ; \displaystyle\langle V_{1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\leq 4\langle V\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}\leq 4\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}\langle W\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}^{-1}\leq 16\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}\langle W_{1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{-1}; 
 
 
 here in the second inequality we used Lemma  3.1 . 
 
 
 Applying Lemma  3.1  to the resulting inequality  ⟨ V 1 ⟩ I ≤ 16 ​ 𝓠 ​ ⟨ W 1 ⟩ I − 1 \langle V_{1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\leq 16\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}\langle W_{1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{-1}  we get the desired estimate ( 9.5 ).
∎ 
 
 
 
 The following lemma applies to arbitrary  L 1 L^{1}  functions, not only to weights. 
 
 
 Lemma 9.5 . 
 
 Let  I I  be a dyadic subinterval of an interval  J J , touching its boundary, and let  φ ∈ L 1 ​ ( J ) \varphi\in L^{1}(J) .
Then for any  N ≥ 2 N\geq 2 
 

 
 
 ⟨ 𝒬 ​ 𝒫 J N ​ φ ⟩ I = ⟨ φ ⟩ J . \displaystyle\langle\mathcal{Q}\mathcal{P}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}^{N}\varphi\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}=\langle\varphi\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}. 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 The case  I = J I=J  is trivial, so we assume that  I ⊊ J I\subsetneq J , so  I I  touches the boundary of  J J  at
one point. Let  J 0 ∈ ch N ⁡ ( J ) J_{0}\in\operatorname{ch}^{N}(J)  touch the boundary of  J J  at the same point as  I I . 
 
 
 Let  n n  be such that  I ∈ ch n ⁡ ( J ) I\in\operatorname{ch}^{n}(J) . If  n ≥ N n\geq N , then  I ⊂ J 0 I\subset J_{0} , and since  𝒬 ​ 𝒫 J N ​ φ \mathcal{Q}\mathcal{P}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}^{N}\varphi 
is constant on  J 0 J_{0}  we conclude that 
 

 
 
 ⟨ 𝒬 ​ 𝒫 J N ​ φ ⟩ I = ⟨ 𝒬 ​ 𝒫 J N ​ φ ⟩ J 0 = ⟨ φ ⟩ J ; \displaystyle\langle\mathcal{Q}\mathcal{P}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}^{N}\varphi\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}=\langle\mathcal{Q}\mathcal{P}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}^{N}\varphi\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J_{0}}=\langle\varphi\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}; 
 
 
 the last equality holds by the construction of  𝒬 ​ 𝒫 J N ​ φ \mathcal{Q}\mathcal{P}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}^{N}\varphi . 
 
 
 If  n < N n<N  then  I I  can be represented as a disjoint union 
 

 
 
 I = ⋃ k = 0 m J k , J k ∈ ch N ⁡ ( J ) . \displaystyle I=\bigcup_{k=0}^{m}J_{k},\qquad J_{k}\in\operatorname{ch}^{N}(J). 
 
 
 By the construction of  𝒬 ​ 𝒫 J N ​ φ \mathcal{Q}\mathcal{P}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}^{N}\varphi 
 

 
 
 ⟨ 𝒬 ​ 𝒫 J N ​ φ ⟩ J k = ⟨ φ ⟩ J , \langle\mathcal{Q}\mathcal{P}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}^{N}\varphi\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J_{k}}=\langle\varphi\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}, 
 
 
 and we immediately get the conclusion of the lemma.
∎ 
 
 
 
 We used this Lemma in the proof of Lemma  9.4 , which is now completely proved. Tracking the strong dyadic  𝐀 2 \mathbf{A}_{2}  characteristic of the remodeled weight  W ~ \tilde{W}  is accomplished. This finishes the construction of our counterexample to the matrix  𝐀 2 \mathbf{A}_{2}  conjecture.
